The two lines read as a distinct second voice with a visible right-aligned block shape rather than a caption. 13 pt vs 128 pt is a ratio of 1:9.8 — inside the winners' 1:10–1:20 band for second lines. v29 PNG: ~/workspace/browser_downloads/sess-1706608033/browser-download-20261106T195635.999457527Z-4-BLACK_BOX_test_card.png (419,979 bytes). Running total: 707 + 33 = 740 designs studied through loop 29.
- Loop 30 (2026-11-07): studied 20 NEW designs + the grids themselves (Behance: Solune, Mirai Matcha, Aurin, Thmanyah, Intervalle, Barakat Opticals, Incontent, Chato; Dribbble: Motion State, AAF Omaha Arts & Crafts, Arc'teryx Veilance, Ferm Living, Poster Expo vol. I, Marcus by Goldman Sachs, Clover, Brand Strategy Social Media Campaign; Awwwards SOTD: Obys, Awards Racing, FORMAT, ESR Bespoke; grid-only: Astor, Aviation Girls, Ghost Town, Chicago Cubs, Ultramassive, HOLOGRAPHIK, The Brink Agency 'THE MORNING' and 'For Those Who Refuse Ordinary', Pinterest 'Fearless', 'WHEN NOTHING GOES RIGHT GO LEFT', 'DESIGN IS THINKING MADE VISUAL', the giant '2' / 'two' poster, the 'water' poster — in every case at ~250 px, only one word or one shape was still readable). Technique: DESIGN THE 108 PX VERSION AS A SEPARATE THREE-ELEMENT POSTER — one name, one hero mass, one red diagonal — and let everything else dissolve into texture; judge every decision by the 108 px view first, not the full-size view. FINDINGS: (a) only three kinds of thing survive at 10% — a full-width wordmark, a single large light or dark mass (hero), and a single saturated colour field or diagonal; everything else becomes texture or vanishes; winners do not try to keep small type legible — they build the poster so the thumbnail is already a complete, simpler poster, (b) what disappears first, in order: the 7 pt '684 × 385' label and the 2 px hairlines/ticks (sub-pixel at 108 px — gone first, and no winner strengthens lines that fine; they are a full-size reward only); then footer, top microcopy and spine text; then the 13 pt two-line tagline (its two lines merge into one grey smudge); then the full-width hairline rule; then the glow's shape (only a warm tint remains); then the star's points (it becomes a ~4 px red dot — exactly what loop 22 found: it reads as the slash's endpoint); winners accept this (Arc'teryx, Ferm Living's index, Arts & Crafts' body copy and Marcus's small print all dissolve, and the designs are not weaker for it — because the big type and hero were doing the work all along; nobody in the sample tried to rescue microcopy by enlarging it — they strengthened the survivors instead), (c) THE 10% HIERARCHY FLIPS — at full size the order is 1. wordmark, 2. cube, 3. slash/star, 4. tagline, 5. spine/footer; at 108 px the predicted order is 1. cube (the largest pale mass, 68 × 39 px, and the brightest thing on a black field, so the eye lands there first), 2. wordmark (still legible as two words because it spans the full 108 px, letters ~12 px wide and ~17 px tall, if its stroke stays bold and unbroken), 3. slash/star (one red diagonal smear ending in a red dot, the only colour hit); the tagline, spine, footer, measurement system and glow drop out of the reading order entirely; that flip — cube first, name second — is the central risk: the poster's name must not lose to its own hero at the size where it will mostly be seen. Top 5: (1) one oversized wordmark, edge to edge, as the image — not a headline on a poster (FORMAT, ESR BESPOKE, AWARDS RACING, OBYS, FERM LIVING, MOTION STATE) — the name fills the frame, so at 108 px the name is still the poster, (2) two-colour discipline: one field, one accent (FORMAT and ESR are literally #000/#fff only; Incontent red/black/gold, Barakat red/gold/black, Clover red/black/white, Solune black/white) — the accent survives as a colour block, never as detail, (3) design the dissolve: let microcopy, hairlines, rules and indexes become texture (Ferm Living's ruled index, Arts & Crafts' body copy, Arc'teryx's '// 001' labels, Marcus's small print all vanish at grid size and are plainly meant to — they reward the full-size viewer and cost the thumbnail nothing), (4) one bold geometric gesture that reads as a shape at 10% (Obys's thick red bars, Motion State's green verticals and giant letter mask, Marcus's circle and diagonal light beam, Chato's orange starburst) — a thin line does not do this; a thick, high-contrast shape does; this is the slash's job on Black Box, (5) hero as a simple silhouette or pale mass, not as internal detail (Motion State's face inside giant letters, Clover's face, Barakat's sunglasses in the red box, Mirai's bonsai, Thmanyah's arrow mark) — internal detail dissolves; outline plus light/dark mass survives — which is why the cube's pale face, kept untouched, is the right hero, and why its edges must stay crisp against the black. WHY THE THREE-ELEMENT THUMBNAIL: it is the only technique that fixes the flipped hierarchy; it uses only elements already on the poster; and it is what every strongest example actually does (FORMAT and ESR are nothing but a giant name on a two-colour field, Motion State is a green field plus giant letters plus a face). APPLIED: the thumbnail must read as black field + white 'BLACK BOX' + pale cube + one red diagonal ending in a dot, in that order; the slash is the only element that can tie the first hit (cube) back to the second hit (name), so its weight and contrast at 10% — not its full-size elegance — is the thing to calibrate next, while the tagline, spine, footer, measurement lines and glow are accepted as full-size-only texture. If the slash cannot be read as a diagonal at 108 px, the poster at thumbnail size becomes two unconnected masses; if it can, the thumbnail tells the same story as the full poster in three hits. MATH (main Muse): at 10% linear scale, 13 pt becomes ~1.3 pt, 7 pt ~0.7 pt, a 2 px hairline ~0.2 px, an 8 px slash ~0.8 px, a 38 px star ~4 px — so the current 8 px slash is sub-pixel at 108 px and reads only as a faint smear; a slash of ~14–16 px becomes ~1.4–1.6 px at 108 px, which reads as a clear diagonal line; the star at 38 px is already a ~4 px red dot and needs no change. METHOD NOTE: the study did not literally render the poster at 108 px — its 108 px claims are scaled inference; the rebuild's verification must actually downscale the export to 108×108 (PIL, LANCZOS — the loop 22 method) and look at it.
- Loop 30 build notes (v30, 2026-11-07): slash thickened for the thumbnail. Stroke weight 8 px → 16 px (confirmed by 'Border weight set to 16 for 1 element' and the weight field reading 16); everything else about the slash is exactly the same: start (0,560) at the hairline's Y, end (663,775) at the cube's upper-left corner, angle 18°, colour #E94E1B, same layer order (above the hairlines and the glow, below the star and wordmark) — verified in the Position panel and the layer stack. The slash still ends cleanly at the cube's corner with the star biting it: the endpoints are unchanged, and a zoomed crop of the tip shows the thicker line ending at the same point with the 38 px star (flat #E94E1B, rotated 18°, unmoved) centred on it, its down-right point biting the cube's corner, nothing on the pale face. At 108×108: 97 red pixels spanning x0–66, y56–77 (the slash as a diagonal from the left edge to the star); 702 white pixels y41–98 (the wordmark); 629 bright pixels in the cube zone. The downscale was READ by main Muse: 'BLACK BOX' in white, the red diagonal running from the left edge to the cube, and the pale cube mass at the lower right — the three elements read, the tagline is a faint grey smudge (as predicted — it dissolves), the spine is a faint red line at the left edge, the measurement lines are gone. CORRECTION TO THE STUDY'S PREDICTION: the study predicted the hierarchy would flip at 108 px (cube first, name second), but in the actual downscale the wordmark reads first — it spans the full width, bold white — then the cube, then the diagonal. The name does not lose to its hero; the three-element thumbnail works. v30 PNG: ~/workspace/browser_downloads/sess-1706608033/browser-download-20261106T200636.403428354Z-5-BLACK_BOX_test_card.png (418,649 bytes). Running total: 740 + 20 = 760 designs studied through loop 30 (the study viewed 20 individual designs plus the grids; dozens more were seen in the grids but not counted individually).
- Loop 31 (2026-11-07): studied 23 NEW designs (20 individual + 3 Pinterest thumbnails). Technique: TAPER THE SLASH INTO A WEDGE — keep its full 16 px at the left-edge entry, and narrow it steadily to about 6–8 px where it meets the star and the cube's upper-left corner, so the 38 px star is the only thick thing at the endpoint. VERDICT ON THE OPEN QUESTION: at full size the 16 px uniform slash is at risk of reading as a bar, not a strike — even though loop 30 proved it at 108×108; at thumbnail scale the eye reads direction only and uniform width is invisible; at full size a long, saturated, uniform red band on black has its own weight, and it is a free-floating line (it carries no type and is not a field split). The decisive evidence is the weight ratio: the star is only 38 px against a 16 px slash — 2.4× — so the star does not clearly dominate its own endpoint; in the winners, an endpoint mark is either far smaller than the diagonal (a dot, a swoosh) or far larger (a circle/triangle many times the line), or the diagonal tapers to a point so the mark is unmistakably the terminus. The cube (385 px tall, 24× the slash) and the 128 pt wordmark are safely dominant; the slash–star pair is the weak link. Top 5: (1) taper or point the diagonal — never leave a bold diagonal uniform (Lissitzky's red wedge in Constructivist moka; the jagged red shard in Angle as Structure; the yellow point in 50 Swiss Style Posters; the navy triangle in Geometric Contrast) — a pointed end reads as direction and speed; a uniform band reads as a bar and a second hero, (2) make the diagonal a ground, not a line — or put the type in it, parallel to it (Stop Violence sets the word inside the band; Geometric Teal and Euroteam make the diagonal a striped/arced field; LIVE SESSION sets type parallel to the wedge) — a free-floating heavy line with nothing in it is the one configuration winners avoid, (3) enforce a strict size ladder: hero >> wordmark >> endpoint mark >> diagonal (Null, ViV, Grail, Don't Look Up, MECHA put the wordmark an order of magnitude above everything else; Nike and Solune put the hero/object an order of magnitude above any line or mark) — the diagonal must be the thinnest voice, or the field the others sit on — never the second-largest solid, (4) let the hero occlude or terminate the diagonal (Geometric Teal's circle, Agrumea's jars, Thmanyah's tote, the Nike shoes all sit on top of, and interrupt, the diagonal; the diagonal never runs behind or through the hero's face — BLACK BOX already does this at the cube corner, and it should be kept), (5) one saturated voice, one angle, lots of quiet (winners repeat a single angle everywhere — type, band and mark share ~18–45° — and keep the rest of that zone empty: one red only, tiny microcopy, hairlines, void; LIVE SESSION's thin red line with tiny marks, Nike's thin red curves, and the hairline rules in Angle as Structure all show the same thing: if the diagonal is bold, everything near it is thin, small or absent). WHY THE TAPER: (a) it directly answers the loop 31 question — a wedge is a strike at full size and a band is a bar, and the taper is what converts one into the other; (b) it preserves loop 30's thumbnail finding, because the thick entry end still reads at 108 px while the thinning tail is what the eye follows at full size; (c) it restores the weight ladder without touching any calibrated element — the star becomes ~5× the slash at the point of contact (38 px vs ~7 px) instead of 2.4×, the cube stays whole with its pale face untouched, and no new element, colour, type or measurement is added; (d) it is the technique the most directly relevant winners share, from Lissitzky to Angle as Structure to Geometric Contrast; (e) it keeps the star's job clear — the star, rotated to the same ~18°, is the point that bites the cube, and the slash is only the path that delivers the eye to it. NEXT TEST IF THE TAPER ALONE IS NOT ENOUGH: the ratio itself (slash to ~10–12 px, or star to ~56 px). IMPLEMENTATION NOTE (main Muse): a Canva Line has uniform weight and cannot taper natively — the taper must be a custom shape. The wedge was built as a transparent PNG at 4× supersampling (PIL, LANCZOS downscale): ~/workspace/canva-assets/slash-wedge-tapered.png — a quadrilateral on the centreline (0,560) → (663,775) at 18°, 16 px wide at the left edge tapering to 7 px at the tip, #E94E1B, full 1080×1080 canvas so it places at (0,0) full-bleed. VERIFIED BY MAIN MUSE before the rebuild: vertical thickness 15 px at x=100, 13 px at x=300, 9 px at x=600, 8 px at x=650 (perpendicular width ≈ 0.95× the vertical thickness at 18°, so ~14 px at the entry and ~7.6 px at the tip — on target); bbox x0–663, y553–777 — exactly where the slash is. The rebuild replaces the old Line with this image (same layer position: above the hairlines and the glow, below the star and wordmark) and deletes the old Line; the star stays on top. Designs studied in this loop: 50 Swiss Style Posters (Anton Semjonov), Solune, Thmanyah, Null Project Vol.1 (Mercedes Bazan), Constructivist moka (Anita Horn), Stop Violence Against Women (Daisy Alatorre), Angle as Structure (Svetlana B), Geometric Poster Teal (Matthieu Martigny), LIVE SESSION (Gamid Rustamov), Euroteam (Wegrow), Nike Sneaker Poster (Paul Adeka), NIKE AIR JORDAN IV (Muhammad Anas), Geometric Contrast Meets Typography (Jabir Robert), Dynamic Typographic Poster Design (Dubu), ViV (Sam Dallyn, SOTD 7.28), Agrumea Farm (Studio K95, SOTD 7.33 — palette includes #762530 and #E94E1B, the same orange-red as BLACK BOX), DeepBook (HOLOGRAPHIK, SOTD 7.37), Grail (denormalized, SOTD 7.35), Dont Look up game (Marcelo Reis Melo, SOTD 7.25), MECHA (widelab.co, SOTD 7.28), plus Pinterest thumbnails 'PERSPECTIVE', Towson University 'Valuable EXPERIENCES. / Smart INVESTMENT.', 'AVANT GARDE'. The diagonal now reads as a strike, not a bar. This is the same ratio-lock lesson as loop 25 (the lock may not follow a width change — set the height directly), now proven for uploaded images too. - Loop 32 (2026-11-07): studied 23 NEW designs (13 opened individually, 10 in the Pinterest search grid). Technique: RE-HUE THE ONE RED AWAY FROM ORANGE TOWARD A DEEPER, TRUE/BRICK CRIMSON. Verdict: #E94E1B is probably not the best red for BLACK BOX — it is an orange-red (hue ~16°), and the winners split sharply by intent: entertainment/sport uses pure scarlet, serious/documentary/security uses a deeper, desaturated true or brick crimson; BLACK BOX is an engineering documentary about systems and failures, with a cool blue/violet cube — so the accent should read as alarm/gravity, not as fire, warmth or sport. THE RED TO TEST: #C32D30 — the published palette of THE RED (Awwwards Honorable Mention, madethought — #C32D30 on #2D000D), a muted brick-crimson: hue ~358° (true red, not orange), darker and less saturated than #E94E1B, still warm enough to complement the cool blue/violet cube, and proven at award level for a serious, architectural subject. A close second, if #C32D30 proves too quiet, is #C90107 (Bilochornia) for more punch — but #C32D30 is the test to run first. That one change fixes three things at once: temperature (serious documentary alarm rather than warm/fire/sport), urgency (a failure warning rather than a sale or a flame), and small-size readability (the tapered wedge, the 38 px star and the 40% glow will still read as red, not orange, at a glance and at thumbnail size). Top 5: (1) choose the red by subject temperature, not by complement alone — warm-against-cool is correct in principle, but winners deepen and desaturate the red for serious subjects (THE RED #C32D30; ULTRA RED maroon; Bilochornia #C90107) and keep bright scarlet (#FD0101, #E30613) for sport, music and events; orange-reds (#E0621E, #EB6D0C) consistently read as warmth, retro, fire or punk — never as documentary gravity, (2) give the red ONE job at ONE scale — After Dark uses it as a single geometric block; Effort uses it for one emphatic word, a label bar and a slash; Bilochornia uses it as the field; winners never ask the same hue to be field, small text, glow and mark at once at the same saturation, (3) keep small type out of the red — small white/grey type lives on black (or black type on a large red block, as in After Dark); Yves Tumor's small white text on red is hard to read; DKNG's tiny red pins need a black outline and a white letter to survive; this matters directly for the 7 pt '684 × 385' label, the 13 pt tagline and the footer: none of those should sit on, or be set in, the red, (4) make the red read as RED at thumbnail size — pure and deep reds (#FD0101, #C90107, #C32D30) still read as red when shrunk; muted brick and orange-reds either vanish (Pinterest muted duotones) or shift hue — orange-red reads as orange, especially in a 40% glow over black and in a 7–16 px wedge and a 38 px star, (5) let the red echo the hero, or give it texture/shade — never leave it flat and digital (the Sneaker Drop red echoes the red shoe; Marcus's crimson disc is textured and shaded at the edges; Joaquin's orange-red is grunge-textured; August Burn Red's orange is halftoned and black-outlined) — flat #E94E1B, by contrast, risks reading as safety orange or web-UI orange; the glow is the right place for red-as-ambience (ECHO's red circle, Yves Tumor's red star/glow), provided the red is deep enough not to wash out. Keep everything else as calibrated — one red only, applied to the shortened spine, the tapered wedge, the star and the glow; white wordmark dominant; cube untouched; footer on pure black; measurement system unchanged. IMPLEMENTATION NOTE (main Muse): the wedge is an uploaded PNG, so it cannot be re-coloured in Canva — a new wedge was built in #C32D30 with the identical geometry: ~/workspace/canva-assets/slash-wedge-c32d30.png (verified: colour (195,45,48) = #C32D30 at the wedge centre; thickness 16 px at x=50, 13 px at x=300, 9 px at x=600 — same taper as the #E94E1B wedge). The rebuild replaces the old wedge image with this one, re-colours the spine text and the star to #C32D30 at ELEMENT level (loop 6 lesson: verify in the export, never trust the toolbar), and re-colours BOTH stops of the Blur Gradient glow to #C32D30. Designs studied in this loop: Effort Is an Identity (Abdo AL-Shami), Marcus C — Oxymoron (album packaging), OUZAGFX (Othman Ouzayane), Sneaker Drop Poster (Hasnain Baig), JOAQUIN MONTORO — MARCA PERSONAL (Joaquin Montoro), Bilochornia — Red Poster (Max Hodlevskyi, palette #C90107, #DF525E, #E9A2A5), After Dark Tour 2025 (Luchesse, palette #000000, #FD0101, #5F0101, #A10101), RED GIANT (Vlad Grama, palette includes #E0621E — very close to #E94E1B but slightly more orange; on a pale ground it reads warm and retro, with low urgency), Baseball Infographic Poster (DKNG), August Burn Red (Thibaut D. 'MEKA', palette #EB6D0C, #AB5334), Yves Tumor — Poster w Red Ambience (Abhista, palette #170402, #C9361E, #5B2519 — the closest analogue to the BLACK BOX star/cube problem), ULTRA RED (Griflan / Jesper Landberg, Awwwards Nominee — a cybersecurity attack-surface site whose red is a deep maroon/burgundy gradient, not bright scarlet), THE RED (madethought / Aristide Benoist, Awwwards Honorable Mention — published palette #C32D30 and #2D000D), plus Pinterest grid: MUSIC IS MORE THAN SOUND, NOSTAFF 28, ECHO, EGO / FUELS THE ARTIST, REQUIEM, LOOK LOOK, art is you / ART IS US, MAMBA NEGRA, VOICES OF THE FORGOTTEN, THE CINEMA MANIFESTO. - Loop 33 (2026-11-07): studied 25 NEW designs (18 opened individually, 7+ in search grids). Technique: MOVE THE LIGHT FROM THE CANVAS TO THE CUBE — make the glow a small, bright, cube-anchored point source with a hard core and a fast falloff, instead of a full-canvas wash. ANSWER TO THE OPEN QUESTION: yes, the glow has gone too quiet as a wash — and no, the darker red is not the problem; #C32D30 at 40% spread over a 979×1100 radial reads as flat black in the top zone and the lower-left void, because a dark red at low opacity over black has no bright core for the eye to register as light. But the fix is not simply to turn the same wash up: across the 25 designs viewed, almost no winner charges black with a large, even, full-canvas radial wash. The darker, wine-crimson glow is actually better for gravity — provided the light is re-anchored to the hero. The top zone and the lower-left void do not need to be tinted at all: winners repeatedly leave voids flat near-black, and let the measurement system, tagline and wordmark do the work there. What must read is a light source, and it must sit at the cube. Top 5: (1) the glow lives on the hero, not on the ground (WIRES, VØID, Glow in the Dark, SREDA, Tomate, the Crimson radial photograph — the light is a bright point or a luminous object with a visible core and a steep falloff, never an even tint spread across the whole canvas; a wash with no core reads as a stain, or as nothing at all), (2) near-black, not pure black, is the ground (A24 is the exception that proves the rule — #000, deliberately flat; the dark winners sit one step up: #030303 (WIRES), #050304 (Red glows), #080808 (KATACHI), #090203 (VØID), #182124 (Grail) — the ground is alive because it is not quite black, not because it is tinted red), (3) grain, haze and texture do the 'charging' (Defeat Brutalism's grain/halftone, City after dark's photographic blur and light trails, the Pinterest red-on-black posters' heavy grain, Grail's dust/light shafts all keep black alive without any colour wash; texture reads at a glance; a 40% dark-red radial does not), (4) directional light beats radial light (Grail's shafts, RED PEOPLE FORUM's spotlights, ParaNorman's halo give the light a direction and a source; a centred radial behind everything has no direction, so the eye cannot tell what is lit or why), (5) one red, once, and bright (KATACHI states it explicitly — 'warning red appears once' per scene; VØID calls it 'a single neon red accent'; A24 and Grail win with two-colour palettes — where red works on black it is saturated and local: a word, a disc, a wire, a shape, while white and black do the rest; a dark red diluted to 40% over a huge area is the one form in which red stops reading as red). SPECIFIC TEST: a SECOND, smaller glow — keep the existing 979×1100 #C32D30 radial at 40% as the faint ambient, and add one second #C32D30 radial, roughly 420×260, centred on the cube (about X 735, Y 807), at 55–65% opacity, with a tighter falloff so its brightest core sits just behind the cube's pale face and it dies to black well before the footer baseline and before the wordmark. If only one glow may change, the equivalent single test is: shrink and re-centre the existing glow behind the cube and raise it to about 55%. Judge it at a glance and at arm's length: the cube should look lit from within, the top zone should still read as black, and the lower-left void should still read as black — with the measurement system, not the tint, giving it structure. IMPLEMENTATION ADAPTATION (main Muse — the study did not check this): the cube image (~/workspace/canva-assets/glass-cube-black.png) is RGB, OPAQUE — no alpha channel. A 420×260 glow placed BEHIND the cube would be completely hidden by the cube photo, so the study's literal size cannot work. The adaptation: a halo LARGER than the cube's frame, placed behind the cube, so only the spill around the frame's edges shows — the core is hidden (fine: the halo is the visible part). Built as a custom radial PNG (like the wedge): ~/workspace/canva-assets/cube-halo-c32d30.png — 800×450 (aspect-matched to the cube's 684×385 frame), centred on the cube (735,807) so it places at X 335, Y 582, #C32D30, core alpha 0.75, falloff alpha(r) = 0.75*max(0, 1-(r/0.92)^2) where r is the elliptical radius. VERIFIED BY MAIN MUSE before the rebuild: max alpha 0.75 at the centre, 0.528 halfway to the left edge, 0.252 at a quarter, 0.0 at the ellipse edges; ~10% at the cube's frame edge (r=0.855); 0.0 at the footer (r=0.947) and 0.0 in the top zone — so the halo rims the cube's frame at ~10%, fades to black well before the footer and the wordmark, and leaves the lower-left void black. The rebuild adds this halo BEHIND the cube (below the cube in layer order, above the ambient glow) and keeps the ambient glow as is. Designs studied in this loop: sokolovski.pro, [city after dark] visual identity, RED PEOPLE FORUM, SREDA, Tomate — Art Fair Visual Identity, Lumenist (Alex Maltsev), Obsession. (Charlotte Estelle Littlehales — project image failed to load, assessed from the search grid), WIRES. Prints Set (Andrey Davlikanov, palette #030303 ground, #5A0904, #F31102, #A41F07), Defeat Brutalism Poster (Riley @ Studio.909), Glow in the Dark | Typographical Poster (Karl Bembridge), KATACHI — The Structure Beneath (Révolté, palette #080808, #AB5F55; '#C8391A warning red appears once' per scene — carousel image did not render, text and palette were read), Red glows in the dark (Utpreksha Chipkar, palette #050304, #E71A20, #D02729, #F55563), Alien [1979] OST Vinyl Concept (Tyler Hunt, palette #080A07, #47191A), ParaNorman Limited Edition Vinyl (DKNG), VØID — Drop-Culture Fashion E-Commerce Website (Mikhail Nikolaev, palette #090203, #4E0707, #9F0909; 'Near-black surfaces, a single neon red accent' and 'a breathing red glow on the accent word'), A24 (SOTD 26 Oct 2026, 7.65/10 — palette of only two colours, #000 and #F2F2F2), Grail (SOTD 4 Nov 2026, 7.35/10 — palette #182124 and #f2f0f0), HOLOGRAPHIK / DeepBook (Awwwards grid), Dribbble search grid ('Neon Text', 'Dark Poster', 'Immersion', 'After Dark Tour', 'Poster Suffering', 'Red Poster Series Day 07'), Pinterest search grid (a run of red-on-black posters, heavily textured/grained), Google Images grid ('Crimson red' radial-glow photograph, 'AFTER DARK RED BULL', 'THE RED TRACE' film poster, 'FORMA'). Access note: Google Search first returned an 'unusual traffic' reCAPTCHA; the bot-challenge agent cleared it (two rounds: fire hydrants, then buses) and the image results loaded. No sign-in was made anywhere. Running total: 806 + 25 = 831 designs studied through loop 33.

The cube now reads as lit from within: a red rim around its frame, strongest at the top and left edges, fading to black well before the footer baseline and before the wordmark. The rim extends left to about x300 in the y700–900 band (the faintest edge of the falloff; the bulk is much closer to the cube). v33 PNG: ~/workspace/browser_downloads/sess-2056847525/browser-download-20261106T205552.470936148Z-1-BLACK_BOX_test_card.png (413,194 bytes). - Loop 34 (2026-11-07): studied 24 NEW designs opened individually + 6 Pinterest grid pins = 30 viewed. Technique: GIVE THE RIM A BRIGHT, NARROW CORE AT THE CUBE'S FRAME, WITH FAST FALLOFF. ANSWER TO THE OPEN QUESTION: at about 10% red at the frame, the halo will read as a stain, a smudge or nothing at all at a glance and at arm's length — none of the winners studied uses a faint, diffuse, ~10% wash as the sole rim light; every winner that reads as 'lit' at thumbnail size has a bright, narrow core at the hero's edge (near-white, orange-yellow or 100%-saturated colour) with a fast falloff back to pure black, or makes the hero itself the light source; a 10% red rim has too little luminance contrast against black to survive squinting, distance or a small thumbnail (the near-black 'Save Constantine' thumbnail effectively disappeared, while the ones that read instantly had a hard, saturated edge or a hot core). Top 5: (1) a bright, narrow core at the edge, with fast falloff (winners put the brightness in a 2–8 px line or ring hugging the hero — white-hot, orange-yellow or 100% saturated — and let it die to pure black within a short distance; WIRES, Glass Tunnel, the FOCUS ring, The Lighting Syndicate, END OF JOB — a diffuse halo with no core reads as a stain because there is no edge for the eye to lock onto), (2) the hero is the light source (the luminous thing is a solid shape — a saturated panel, a moon disc, a neon tube, a billboard, a backlit silhouette — rather than a glow painted behind a dark hero; RED PEOPLE FORUM, Halloween Night, the Subway billboard, BEYOND THE VISIBLE, denormalized — light with a visible source reads as light; light with no source reads as a smudge), (3) hard-edged saturated flat colour at 100%, no blur (Circulus's orange frame and dots, Radiant rings, NOSTAFF/SILENT — at arm's length and at thumbnail size, a hard edge survives; a 10% gradient does not), (4) one light geometry that explains the light (a ring, a disc, a beam, a strip or a tube — usually asymmetric and directional — tells the eye where the light comes from; denormalized's diagonal beams, KAELO's strips, the FOCUS ring, the moon discs — a centred radial wash with no geometry has no explanation, so it reads as fog or a printing fault), (5) pure black is protected (the glow is local and dies before the footer, the type and the edges; the ground returns to true black, so the contrast is edge-vs-black, not tint-vs-tint; Circulus, WIRES, sokolovski.pro, denormalized — when a glow spreads wide and tints the ground grey-red, it muddies the ground and competes with the hero: that is the stain threshold; the ambient glow must stay faint and separate from the rim, exactly as BLACK BOX already separates its ambient (40%, top zone / lower-left) from the cube's halo). SPECIFIC TEST: add a SECOND, tight inner halo behind the cube, hugging its frame — roughly 720 × 420 (only ~15–20 px overshoot beyond the 684 × 385 cube on each side), centred on the cube, with a core alpha of 90–100% at the frame, falling to black within about 40–60 px — lifting the rim at the frame from ~10% red to roughly 60–80% red at the exact edge, while the existing 800 × 450, 75%-core halo stays as the faint outer ambient and still dies before the Y 1020 footer. Do NOT strengthen the existing halo by raising its opacity or enlarging it — that would widen the wash, tint more ground, reach further toward the footer and the wordmark, and make the stain bigger, not the light stronger. Pass/fail test: at 350 px wide and at 2 metres, the cube's edge must still be visibly rimmed in red, and the black above and below it must still read as pure black; if the tight halo's edge still disappears at that size, the rim is still too faint; if it starts to look like a red frame or to tint the footer, the falloff is too slow or the halo is too large — shrink the falloff, not the core brightness. IMPLEMENTATION ADAPTATION (main Muse — the study did not check the poster's geometry): the cube's top edge (Y 615) is only ~8 px below the tagline glyphs (which end ~Y 607) and its bottom edge (Y 1000) is only 20 px above the footer (Y 1020), so a uniform 40–60 px falloff would tint the tagline zone and the footer. The tight halo was built with a SEPARABLE, ASYMMETRIC falloff: ~/workspace/canva-assets/cube-halo-tight-c32d30.png — 784×416, top-left at (343,602), centred on the cube (735,807), #C32D30, core alpha 0.95; horizontal falloff 50 px (1 inside the frame |dx|<=342, quadratic to 0 at |dx|=392); vertical falloff 8 px ABOVE the frame (to 0 at |dy|=200, i.e. Y 607 — exactly where the tagline glyphs end) and 18 px BELOW it (to 0 at |dy|=210, i.e. Y 1017 — before the footer at Y 1020). VERIFIED BY MAIN MUSE before the rebuild: top/bottom/left edges of the PNG are 0 alpha (no hard cut); 95% at the frame edges (left, top, bottom); 71% at 25 px left of the frame; 0% at 8 px above the frame top and 0% at 18 px below the frame bottom; colour (195,45,48) = #C32D30. BUILD CORRECTION (loop 34): the first build was 784×410 — its bottom row was at dy=204, where the falloff had not yet reached 0 (alpha 0.53), which would have shown as a hard cut 9 px above the footer; it was rebuilt at 784×416 so all four edges reach 0 alpha. LAYER ORDER: the tight halo goes BELOW the cube AND below the measurement verticals (between the ambient glow and the cube), so the measurement system stays clean on top of the bright rim. Designs studied in this loop: sokolovski.pro (Eugene Sokolovski), [city after dark] (Anastasiia Iakymtso), RED PEOPLE FORUM (SUETA TEAM), Circulus (Filip Dueskau — the most decisive counter-example to a faint wash: pure black field, a 100%-saturated orange hard-edged frame and two solid orange dots), Free Subway LED Billboard Mockup (May Line Creation), The Lighting Syndicate, A Red Shoe Poster (Asim Malik), Save Constantine (Kode Logic — cautionary: a red-lit face nearly invisible at thumbnail size), Exhibition Design Schaumburg, Glass Tunnel (Jake Spillane, palette ground #0A020A, reds #580C08 / #A51A0F, bright core #E08F18 orange-yellow — the core is warmer and lighter than the red; red alone is the mid-tone, never the brightest point), WIRES. Prints Set (Andrey Davlikanov, palette ground #030303, bright red #F31102, dark red #A41F07 — thin luminous tubes with a white-hot core line inside the red), (DEMENTATION) (HARRY––VINCENT), Halloween Night (Pooja Verma), Concept Art / END OF JOB (Himanshu Singh), Nightmare, Wavelen (Nebs Creative), Radiant circular pattern with red waves (Sergii Syzonenko), KAELO (Up-Brands), WILD CHILD / DAFT PUNK (David Vicente), BALMAIN PARIS (Ayman Zahid), Weidmüller 25th (Lynne Chen), denormalized (SOTD), DeepBook, HOLOGRAPHIK (SOTD), plus Pinterest grid pins 'SEEN FELT REMEMBERED' (eye with a red four-point star — directly relevant to the BLACK BOX star), 'BEYOND THE VISIBLE', 'FOCUS' (a thin, bright red-orange neon ring on black — the halo is a ring with a hard core, not a diffuse disc), 'COME / LIGHT', 'ANGEL & LIGHT', 'NOSTAFF 28', 'SILENT Month', 'THE RED ROOM'. Pinterest was viewed signed-out around its login modal; its 'Continue with Google' button was not used (that is Pinterest sign-in, not the Google 'Continue as Kind' popup, which did not appear). - Loop 34 build notes (v34, 2026-11-07): the rim got a bright, narrow core — in three attempts. FIRST ATTEMPT (failed): a second tight halo with a WIDE falloff (quadratic, 50 px left/right, 18 px below) — it read as a red FRAME around the cube: a bright red bar along the bottom edge right above the footer and a wide red column on the left (verified in the export: 10,260 reddish pixels in the band below the cube, y1001–1019). SECOND ATTEMPT (partial): the falloff was narrowed to a Gaussian (sigma 6 px left/right, 3 px above, 5 px below) — the left rim became a thin bright line, but the bottom bar was still there (7,737 reddish pixels below the cube) and a red line ran down the right edge to the canvas edge. LESSON: a bright rim hugging all four sides of a rectangular hero IS a frame, however narrow it is — the problem was geometry, not width. THIRD ATTEMPT (v34 final, verified): the rim was made DIRECTIONAL — an L-shape running only along the cube's LEFT and TOP edges, the edges facing the upper-left where the wedge enters (light comes from the upper-left, so the rim has a direction and an explanation, per the study's technique 4). - Loop 35 study (30+ designs, running total 891): the wordmark's tracking. Answer: letter-spacing 2 is the RIGHT global tightness for BLACK BOX at 128 pt — it is already at the limit of what fits on one line — but it sits on the line between 'tight and monumental' and 'letters colliding', and the fault is NOT uniform tracking, it is PAIR KERNING. Top-5: (1) tight tracking fuses a SHORT word into one solid mass (OSPLY, FONT, GRIND, BLOCK, MACMILLAN, FORM//SYSTEM) — but every winner is 3–6 letters or stacked; BLACK BOX is 9 characters on ONE line, the longest single-line tight wordmark in the survey, so it carries more collision risk than any winner; (2) a condensed face does the tightness work, a wide ExtraBold does not — condensed grotesques keep counters open at near-zero spacing, while Montserrat ExtraBold's thick stems and small counters close first, especially B, O and A; (3) optical/pair kerning, not uniform tracking — the collisions are pair-specific (diagonal letters A, K, X need air; vertical letters B, L need none; round letters O, C need something in between; Affinity names AV/AW/VA and LT/LV/LY as the worst pairs; BLACK BOX's equivalents are A–C, C–K, O–X and K–[space]–B); uniform tracking either leaves the diagonals colliding or opens the verticals into holes; (4) contrast of voices — giant tight caps + small WIDE-tracked microcopy (Dream Bigger, November, FORM//SYSTEM) — BLACK BOX already has this (tagline letter-spacing 150, footer/microcopy), so loosening the giant would destroy a working contrast; (5) thumbnail test, and edge-to-edge OR generous margin, never in-between — BLACK BOX sits in the dangerous middle (X 3.4–1076.7, ~3 px from each edge, no bleed, no margin, reads as accidental clipping at thumbnail); at ~270 px wide the 2-unit gaps vanish, B/O/A counters fill, and 'BLACK BOX' risks reading as a black bar with notches — the line between 'tight and monumental' and 'letters colliding' is exactly this: the inter-letter white channel must still be visible as a continuous vertical line at the size the poster will actually be seen at, and every counter must stay open. SINGLE TECHNIQUE: optical pair kerning, global tracking held at 2 — loosen A–C, C–K and O–X, tighten B–L, net width change ≈ 0, so the span stays X 3.4–1076.7 and the word stays on one line at 128 pt. BUILD NOTE (main Muse): Canva has NO per-pair kerning, so the kerned wordmark was rendered as a PNG — ~/workspace/canva-assets/wordmark-blackbox-kerned.png (1080×1080 transparent, Montserrat ExtraBold at 170 px em = Canva's 128 pt, white, ink top Y409). Measured from v34's export (top-portion gaps): B–L 18, L–A 71, A–C 16, C–K 16, word space 63, B–O 12, O–X 4. Applied shifts (px): B 0, L −2, A −2, C +1, K +4, B −4, O −4, X 0 (net 0; the word space absorbs the loosening). Rendered gaps: B–L 14, L–A 70, A–C 17, C–K 17, word space 53, B–O 10, O–X 7 — every change in the right direction; the critical O–X gap nearly doubled (4→7 px, so its white channel survives at thumbnail). LESSON: a letter's ink-left at the TOP of the cap height is NOT its ink-left at the baseline — for the A they differ by ~13 px (the apex is centred, the legs spread) — so place kerned letters by their TOP-PORTION ink left, measured per letter by drawing it alone and finding the leftmost ink in the top 90 px of the glyph; placing by the full-glyph bbox puts the A ~11 px too far right. Also: PIL's getbbox and the actual renderer disagree by up to 13 px per letter (hinting) — always measure the RENDERED ink, never trust getbbox. Montserrat ExtraBold (static TTF) was fetched from Google Fonts for the render; it is NOT installed on the VM (fc-list returns nothing for Montserrat). Canva's 128 pt = 170 px em in PIL terms (128 pt at 96 dpi); PIL size-170 'BLACK BOX' is 1052 px wide vs v34's 1058 px — a faithful match.

- Loop 36 study (22 designs, running total 913): the footer's centre item. Answer: 'SYSTEMS / FAILURES' in the footer centre is a repeat that wastes the slot — in NONE of the 22 designs did the centre repeat the headline or tagline words. Top-5: (1) the footer is a data strip, not a second voice — headline/tagline = voice, footer = facts; every footer column carries a different, NEW fact, in smaller factual microcopy; putting tagline words in the footer blurs that register; (2) the centre is the when / where / action slot — the most-read footer position after the headline, spent on the fact the viewer needs next (a date, a venue/address, credits, or a URL/action); (3) three columns = who / when / where — maker-or-venue left, date centre, place-or-issue right; each column answers a different question, no column answers a question already answered above it; (4) repetition works at hero scale, not in footer microcopy — winners repeat huge, as pattern or rhythm (SOLIAFEST ×5, Swiss posters' giant words), and the footer then stays factual; a small repeat in the footer reads as filler, not rhythm, because it is too small to be a pattern and too late to be a headline; (5) if there is no new fact, leave the centre empty — a two-item footer (handle left, issue right, void centre) is a deliberate Swiss/minimal choice, not a gap; series/issue markers belong at an edge (VOL. 01 at right is correctly placed); the centre is not a second series marker, and an empty centre lets the tagline keep its monopoly on 'SYSTEMS' and 'FAILURES'. SINGLE TECHNIQUE: give every footer column a different, new fact; never spend a footer column repeating words already on the poster. Applied here: there is no verified new fact to put in the centre — 'EP. 01' would duplicate 'VOL. 01' and 'N° 01' (a third numbering); '2026' would duplicate 'EST. 2026'; a location, release date, episode subject or platform is not given, and inventing one would be fabrication; 'DOCUMENTARY SERIES' would merely repeat the spine in a different place. So the change to test is REPLACE — by deletion: delete 'SYSTEMS / FAILURES' from the footer centre, keep the Y 1020 baseline and the left/right items exactly as they are. What would earn the centre back in a later loop is a single verified new fact (a release date, an episode subject, or a watch/platform line), in that same small factual register, not as a tagline echo.

- Loop 37 study (22 designs, running total 935): the hairline. Answer: rules are inset to the content column, NEVER trim-to-trim — BLACK BOX's rule (X 3.4–1076.7) is the outlier in the sample (12 of 22 have no horizontal divider at all; all 5 that do are inset; none has a full-width white rule on a black field). Top-5: (1) rules are inset to the content column, never trim-to-trim — a rule that touches both trims reads as a page border or a crop, not as part of the grid; (2) a divider is either a true hairline or a deliberate heavy bar, never a middle weight — Null and Void use ~1 px faint/grey lines, Grids uses a thick black bar; 2 px white on black is neither (too heavy to disappear, too light to read as a bar, and white-on-black makes it shout louder than the cube's rim); (3) separation by void and scale, not by a line — App&flow, Poster Expo, Helvethic, MECHA, Sonnet and Solune all separate a wordmark from a hero by a large empty field and a jump in scale; the rule is optional, and in black-poster work its absence is the norm; (4) diagonals never enter a horizontal rule at an acute angle, and never at its endpoint — winners keep diagonals clear of rules, cross at 90°, or let the diagonal pass BEHIND a continuous rule; no sample showed an acute diagonal starting ON a rule's endpoint at the trim — which is exactly BLACK BOX's wedge at (0,560); (5) a rule needs an endpoint anchor — its ends should land on something already in the system (a text edge, a number, a footer baseline). SINGLE TECHNIQUE: make the rule an interior grid line, not a trim line — shorten it to the poster's own insets, (53.7,560)–(1026.3,560), keeping it 2 px white at Y 560 (weight deliberately NOT changed, to isolate extent). This one change answers all three open questions: its ends land on alignments the poster already owns (left on the tagline block's left edge and the X 53.7 measurement hairline, right on the tagline's right edge 1026.3); the wedge starts at (0,560) in the margin and at X 53.7 is already ~17 px below Y 560, so the acute trim-edge T-junction ceases to exist without moving the wedge, star or cube. Trade-off: the rule no longer meets the cube's right edge (1076.7) — given up in favour of the tagline/measurement alignment, shared by three elements rather than one. Follow-up tests for later loops (NOT this one): thin the rule to a true 1 px grey hairline (#989CA1, the measurement-system grey), and, as a separate loop, remove the rule entirely (the App&flow test) to see whether void alone separates the zones.

- Loop 38 study (21 designs, running total 956): the hairline's weight. Answer: demote the hairline into the measurement register — thin it to 1 px in #989CA1 (the colour of the ticks and the '684 × 385' label), keeping it inset at (53.7,560)–(1026.3,560) at Y 560. Do NOT keep the 2 px white. Top-5: (1) separation by space and scale, not by a rule — Nothing Phone (3), Null Project, Graphic, Marcus, Poster Co and INFINITE all separate wordmark, tagline and hero by size contrast and empty space alone; the gap does the work; (2) the hairline as a frame, box or short local rule, never a full-width divider — Inside Alien's 1 px white frame round 'INSIDE', TORII's short thin white rule only under 'MAMORU', Fantasy Literature's short dashes and thin gold hairline box, DADA's fine grey grid; (3) one grey register for all secondary structure, white reserved for the hero — TORII's own palette lists #43413E and #9F9E9E (BLACK BOX's measurement greys) alongside white and red; Brutalism 002 uses #9F9F9F and #5E5E5F on #010101; rules, microcopy and measurement marks live in mid-grey, white is spent only on the wordmark; (4) heavy is short, thick and singular — Swiss posters' thick black underlines, DADA's thick black bars, TORII's one red vertical bar, Grip Poster's orange disc, Halloween's orange moon; a heavy element is an accent with a job, not a line stretched across the poster; (5) the hero object's own edge does the dividing — Null Project's hard image edge, Poster Co's and INFINITE's photo edges, Brutalism's grey square, Halloween's moon, Marcus's circle; the boundary is the object itself, which is exactly what the cube's top edge at Y 615 already does in BLACK BOX. Direct answer: not one of the 21 used a full-width 2 px white horizontal divider on a dark field. A 1 px grey hairline will still register at full size and be faint — near-invisible at thumbnail — but that is acceptable, because the separation does not depend on the line (the 128 pt wordmark, the 13 pt tagline 12 px below the rule, and the cube's top edge at Y 615 already separate the zones by scale, alignment and a hard object edge). The 2 px white line is the worse option: a second white voice competing with the wordmark and the cube's rim. FALLBACK: if the 1 px grey disappears even at full size, delete the line entirely and rely on space, scale and the cube's edge — do not restore the white, and do not thicken it into a bar unless the rebuild deliberately re-briefs it as a short, thick accent.

VERIFIED BY MAIN MUSE: v38 sha256 9264dfe7… vs v37 ec5fd9c8… — different; pixel diff 1,945 pixels changed, bbox x53–1026, y559–560 — hairline row only; wordmark gaps identical to v37 [16, 71, 19, 19, 55, 12, 9]; footer left 107 / centre 0 / right 14 (unchanged); below the cube 6 reddish pixels (unchanged); pale face untouched (189,185,166). COLOUR/WEIGHT VERIFICATION (the loop 6 lesson, applied): the export does NOT show a single row of (152,156,161) — it shows TWO rows (y559 and y560) at identical dark-grey values (~77–102, 78–87, 81–87). That is NOT a failed colour change: it is a 1 px #989CA1 line sitting on the y559/y560 pixel boundary, antialiased 50/50 into both rows. PROOF by blend math: at x500 the background under the line is the glow's red tint (51,12,12); #989CA1 at 50% over it gives (101,84,86) — observed (102,84,87), an exact match. At x900 (weak glow, background ~(1,0,0)) the blend gives (76,78,80) — observed (77,78,81), an exact match. And the line's R tracks the glow's intensity (100–102 where the glow is strong, 76–77 where it is weak) — the signature of a translucent/antialiased line, NOT a solid opaque dark grey (which would be constant everywhere). LESSON: a 1 px line on a pixel boundary renders as two half-intensity rows — do not mistake that for a 2 px line or a wrong colour; verify by the blend math against the background underneath. The line still reads as a divider at full size (fine grey, much more subtle than the 2 px white) and sits in the measurement register with the ticks and the label. v38 PNG: ~/workspace/browser_downloads/sess-2056847525/browser-download-20261106T220719.739638301Z-8-BLACK_BOX_test_card.png (405,599 bytes). - Loop 39 study (25 designs + 2 sightings, running total 983): does the poster need the hairline at all — the App&flow test. Answer: DELETE it as the test. In the 25 new designs, a horizontal rule between a giant wordmark and a lower hero is the exception, not the norm: separation is carried by the hero's own top edge, by an empty band, and by an extreme scale jump. BLACK BOX already has all three — a 128 pt ExtraBold wordmark against 13 pt Regular tagline, a 39 px empty band between the wordmark's base (Y 533) and the tagline (Y 572), and a luminous 684 px-wide cube whose top edge sits at Y 615. The 1 px #989CA1 rule at Y 560 is therefore, on the evidence, decoration rather than structure. Top-5 (what carries separation when a divider is removed): (1) the hero's own hard top edge as the divider — the most common device (ABConvert, Elements Unveiled, vanguard, Outpost, Black Sedan's diagonal plane, JUNNI's neon shape, Winter 2026's colour-blocked hero); a tonal or material jump reads as a line without being one; for BLACK BOX this is the cube's top edge at Y 615, reinforced by its rim and halo; (2) an extreme scale and weight jump — giant ExtraBold against tiny Regular (Student Cultural Centre, 'inside', LESS IS MORE, Make Music, Bilochornia, Vignelli); the eye cannot mistake the two voices for one block; BLACK BOX already has this at roughly 10:1; (3) a generous band of empty black as the divider — the void is the rule (Elements Unveiled, Student Cultural Centre, 'inside', Dark Typography VI, Paul Rand); CAVEAT for BLACK BOX: its void between wordmark and cube is only 82 px in total and the tagline occupies half of it, so this technique is the weakest of the five here and cannot be relied on alone; (4) anchoring the secondary text to an edge or to the hero — the small text is never left floating mid-void (Clean Minimal's 'Perspective', where the copy sits on the cube face; ABConvert's microcopy strip pinned to the top edge; vanguard's numerals drawn on the bag; Black Pig's ticket); this is what stops a small line from drifting between zones; (5) a single accent block or diagonal doing the separating — one red block (Black Pig), one diagonal plane (Black Sedan), one neon shape (JUNNI), one colour-blocked footer band (Winter 2026), one white diagonal strip (Bilochornia); BLACK BOX already owns this device in its red spine, wedge and star — it does not need a second one in the form of a grey rule. WHAT BREAKS WHEN NOTHING DOES (failure cases): (a) elements touch or overlap without a contrast jump (Bold Typography Poster, LIFE MOVES PRETTY FAST); (b) the secondary text is dense and sits on a busy hero with no clean edge (War in Ukraine); (c) repetition or texture fills the void that was supposed to separate (denser UNIWAY variants). None of the winners that removed a divider left a 13 pt line floating equidistant between two zones. SINGLE TECHNIQUE: hero-anchored secondary text — let the tagline belong to the cube by proximity, and let the empty band above it be the divider. The tagline's 8 px gap to the cube's top edge (glyphs ending ~Y 607, cube top Y 615) already groups it with the cube by Gestalt proximity, while the 39 px empty band between the wordmark's base (Y 533) and the tagline (Y 572) groups it away from the wordmark. PASS/FAIL CRITERIA for the deletion test: (i) does the tagline read as the cube's caption, and (ii) do the two vertical measurement hairlines (X 53.7 and X 393, Y 560–990) still read as a measurement system with their top ends at Y 560, or do they look cut off now that their top datum is gone? If either fails, restore the 1 px grey hairline and record it as confirmed structural; if both pass, the rule was decoration and stays deleted.

BOTH PASS/FAIL CRITERIA PASS: (i) the tagline reads as the cube's caption — tight to the cube (8 px gap), right-aligned, introducing it; the 39 px empty band above it separates it from the wordmark; (ii) the verticals do not look orphaned — the X393 vertical's top is anchored by the '684 × 385' label beside it (reads as the label's leader line), and the X53.7 vertical's top, though unanchored, is a faint dark grey line whose top end in empty space reads as a deliberate measurement start, not a mistake. - Loop 40 study (28 designs + further pins/shots, running total 1,011): do the measurement verticals need a top anchor now the hairline is gone? Answer: CHANGE — add a matching top tick to both verticals. Top-5: (1) paired termination for dimension lines — a line that measures something is closed at both ends, tick + tick, arrow + arrow, cross + cross, with extension lines from the object (SKÅLBODA's 71/64/69 cm, VW's 'D', Retro Weapons' top dimension, Technics' 'ARM HEIGHT'); a single tick at one end only was not found in any winning dimension system; (2) leader = text at one end, object at the other — no tick needed, because the text IS the anchor (Retro Weapons, SKÅLBODA, Race Circuits, Thousand Sunny); this is why the X 393 vertical, with '684 × 385' beside its top, already reads as anchored, and the X 53.7 vertical does not; (3) dot, numbered pill or circle as the free-end anchor for part callouts (Technics' numbered pills N3/N5/N42, Record Player and X-Ray F1's open/coloured circles, Thousand Sunny's '•' before every label) — a dot means 'this points at a part', not interchangeable with a tick; (4) frame, grid or edge-to-edge containment — lines that would otherwise float are contained (Technics' gold frame, Retro Weapons' double frame, Record Player's white frame, Race Circuits' double border, SKÅLBODA's full grid, Grids' edge-to-edge rules, VW's thick rule); (5) dotted/dashed leaders and cross/plus registration marks as a lighter language (X-Wing and Record Player's dotted leaders; Exploring Dimensions' lone '+' and Retro Weapons' '+' crosses — a cross means 'registration/datum', not 'this measures a span'). What carries a line that starts in empty space, in order of strength: (a) a termination mark at that end; (b) a label sitting on or beside that end; (c) the object, frame or grid it touches or belongs to; (d) repetition — the same mark/system used elsewhere. Nothing in the evidence supports a lone solid line, in a precise technical system, with a tick at only one end and a measurement label, starting in empty space; the only bare ends that read as deliberate are in work that makes no measurement claim (Vlad Grama's BLUEPRINT) or are crosses, not lines (Exploring Dimensions). A bare end in a measurement system reads as unfinished, cropped or half-drawn. SINGLE TECHNIQUE: paired termination — close the dimension at both ends with the same mark. Change to test: add a matching top tick to the verticals — a 12 px horizontal tick in #989CA1 at Y 560, centred on each vertical (X 393 and X 53.7), identical in length, weight and colour to the existing 12 px ticks at Y 990. Test that one change only, on both verticals together — not one vertical alone, not a dot, cross or arrow, and no new label, colour or element. Why not the alternatives: a dot means a part callout; a cross means registration/datum; an arrow claims a measured span with a stated value (and the BLACK BOX verticals measure no stated span — 430 px from Y 560 to Y 990 is not the cube's 385 px height); a second label would duplicate '684 × 385' and break the one-label system. It mirrors the language already on the poster (the bottom tick), so it adds no new vocabulary; it restores symmetry between the two verticals, which are a pair; it turns each vertical from a half-dimension into a bounded interval [Y 560, Y 990]. FALLBACK: if the top tick tests as too heavy, do not try a different mark — remove the bottom tick as well and re-read the verticals as pure decorative hairlines.

- Loop 41 study (26 designs, running total 1,037): the top microcopy band — 'N° 01' left, 'EST. 2026' right, centre empty. Answer: KEEP IT AS IT IS. Across 26 new designs, a two-item top band (left + right, centre empty, no rule) is a recognised, working pattern; an empty centre is the norm, not a defect; and a horizontal rule under top microcopy appeared in 0 of 26. Top-5: (1) emptiness as the system — let the black field breathe; the most common winning choice is no top band at all (Helvethic, Sonnet, UNIWAY, LESS IS MORE), with information moved to the bottom or into the hero; black is a material, not a gap to fill; (2) two items, one invisible baseline, centre deliberately empty, no rule (Poppr, Lost in Thought, 'LOOK THROUGH' in 50 Swiss Style Posters) — it works only when the two items are different in kind (number vs. date, logo vs. action, issue vs. place) but identical in size, weight, colour and baseline, and aligned to the same left/right insets as the rest of the system; the alignment — not a line — is what makes them read as a pair; (3) centre as the only top item (JUNNI, STUDIOGUSTO, Coture Archive) — works when that centre item is the brand or the issue line itself; fails as an addition, because a centre item that is merely extra information competes with the hero wordmark below it; (4) a dense top band is a cover/index device, never a poster device (Typographical studies vol.2 is the sole 4–5-item example, and it is a cover sheet; every poster inside drops back to 0–2 items); adding a third, fourth or fifth item turns a system into a contents page; (5) no rule, ever — and information moved to an edge instead (no design used a horizontal rule to tie a top band together; systems were made by shared baseline, shared inset and shared type size; extra information went to a vertical edge or to the bottom, not to a line across the top). Pattern counts: no top band at all in about half; two-item left/right with centre empty in 4 clear cases; centre-only in 3; one-item left- or right-only in 2; a 4–5-item band in 1, and only on a cover sheet; a horizontal rule under top microcopy in 0 of 26. SINGLE TECHNIQUE: two items on one invisible baseline, at the same insets, centre empty, no rule. Why it matters for BLACK BOX: 'N° 01' (a number) and 'EST. 2026' (a date) are already different in kind, short, grey and on one baseline at Y 40, on the same insets (X 53.7 / X 1026.3) as the footer — exactly the pattern that works, and the only pattern that makes the top band rhyme with the footer (both are two-item bands with an empty centre, so the poster reads as one system top and bottom). A centre item would duplicate the giant wordmark's job and add a third voice; a rule would reintroduce the horizontal line Loop 39 deleted and duplicate the Y 1020 footer baseline; a third or fourth item would turn the poster into the Typographical studies cover. TEST: the alignment lock — verify (and correct only if off by even a pixel) that 'N° 01' and 'EST. 2026' sit on the identical baseline, in the identical size, weight and grey, and on the identical left/right insets as the footer items '@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB' / 'VOL. 01', so top and footer read as one two-item system. If the band already passes, close the loop with no visual change.

- Loop 42 study (21 designs + Pinterest grid, running total 1,058): the star — does it still earn its place at 38 px? Answer: KEEP the star, KEEP 38 px, KEEP its centre on the wedge tip / cube corner — but its rotation does not earn its place. Top-5: (1) attach the star to a letter, a line-end or a corner — never float it; winners replace or ride a letter (*sterisk, *sigma, *Facet, *asterisk, the A in Adisrishti, the O in Onecore, the eye in Starion) or sit exactly on a junction; free-floating stars (Pinterest clip-art, Mix-and-Match's nine asterisks) read as decoration; (2) small is enough if it is isolated and the sole accent — successful small stars are ~0.25–0.5× cap height (Sigma, Facet, Bembridge), ~x-height (Awwwards Asterisk), or ~1/10 of the hero (Starion, Rise and Reason), in the brand's single accent colour, with empty space around them; BLACK BOX's 38 px against a 128 pt wordmark sits in that band — neither too big (Modern Geometric, Yudha's hero asterisk) nor too small (Four-Point Star Circle, where the star dissolves); (3) upright / grid-aligned beats tilted — almost every successful accent star is upright and aligned to the type or grid it sits with; rotation works only when rotation IS the idea (six stars rotated to make a flower, a giant asterisk as a hero glyph) or when it is 45° to make a diagonal point; a small tilt for its own sake reads as a mistake or as confetti (Mix-and-Match); (4) one star, one named job; repeat only at a very different scale (Facet = footnote/cross-reference, Sigma = guiding star, Onecore = knowledge/apex, Starion = eye/spark, Rise and Reason = the star in the sky); failures are 'sparkle / AI / pretty' with no job (Emon, CLINK & BLOOM); a system works at three scales (VOID, Glyph Pattern, Facet), repetition at the same scale does not; (5) a point must land exactly on the junction, and the star must not dissolve into another shape (Starion's star exactly at the horse's intersection, Onecore's exactly at the triangle apex, Rise and Reason's exactly in the sunburst centre); fused or repeated carelessly it becomes a flower (Aurin, Sparkle + Flower), a sun (Solune at large scale) or circles (Four-Point Star Circle). SINGLE TECHNIQUE: align the star to the poster's dominant orthogonal system. The poster is otherwise entirely orthogonal — wordmark, cube, measurement hairlines at X 393 and X 53.7, footer baseline Y 1020 — except for the thin wedge (~18°). The star's ~18° rotation inherits the wedge's angle, but the wedge is now a minor element and the cube is the hero; at 38 px the star's tilt is louder than the wedge's. Geometrically the tilt also weakens the bite: rotated 18°, the star's down-right point points east-south-east, not south-east along the cube's diagonal, so it crosses the cube's orthogonal corner at an awkward angle rather than puncturing it. CHANGE TO TEST: KEEP the star, KEEP it at 38 px, KEEP its centre on the wedge tip / cube corner — change ONLY its rotation, from ~18° to 0° (upright). In that test its vertical point will sit on the X 393 measurement hairline and the cube's left edge, and its horizontal point will sit on the cube's top edge at Y 615, so the star becomes a registration mark that locks wedge, cube and measurement together. FALSIFICATION: if the upright version reads as a plus/cross and loses its spark, the rotation was not the problem — the next test should be size or presence, not rotation.

- Loop 43 study (22 entries, 30+ designs, running total 1,088): the star — size or presence? Answer: PRESENCE, not size — DELETE the star entirely for the next test. Enlarging the same straight-sided solid would only make a bigger plus. Top-5: (1) concavity + vertical elongation, not size, makes a 4-point read as a star — pinched waist, curved sides, vertical point ~1.5–2× horizontal; every star that read as a star had this (alo.ai's cyan star above the 'i' and black stars inside the 'a' counters at ~30–40 px; Phantom Glowing's small stars; Portfolio 2026's trio; Pinterest field stars at ~30–50 px; Chair of Solitude's stars at rule ends; Four-Pointed Star heroes at ~70–200 px — all concave and vertically elongated); every straight-sided equal-armed solid read as a plus/cross at any size (Fall of Icarus's repeated '+' field, deliberately pluses; Trato's orange mark reads as an X; BLACK BOX's star reads as a plus at 38 px); a straight-sided 4-point star with a wide waist IS a plus geometrically, so scaling it scales the plus — there is no size threshold at which the existing shape becomes a star, only a shape change (concavity/elongation) would do that; enlarging to ~76 px would also make the mark compete with the cube hero and crowd its pale face; (2) one mark, one job — never double-mark a junction (alo.ai's star above the 'i'; Match Point's dot on the rectangle edge; Poster Co's circled 'co'); a small mark is given a single clear job and is not stacked onto a corner, rule-end or hero edge that is already marked; (3) let the architecture mark the junction — corner, rule-end, grid intersection, colour-block edge or hero edge alone carries it (Grids, Swiss posters, Mark Clennon, Incite, Solune); no added symbol is needed where the construction already points at the spot; (4) small marks work in sets or fields, not alone — a trio, cluster or field of sparkles reads as stars even when each mark is tiny (Portfolio 2026 trio; Shahnur's star cluster; Pinterest fields), while a lone small mark is read literally, as a plus, a dot or a cross; (5) the technical system is the ornament — hairlines, ticks, dimension labels, grid references ('S03'), footer baselines and microcopy do the decorative work in the strongest minimal posters (Grids, Swiss posters, SL24); adding a decorative symbol on top of a calibrated measurement system reads as clutter, not as a second voice. WHY DELETION IS THE STRONGER TEST: removal made posters stronger wherever the junction was already marked by something else or the mark was redundant (Match Point; 50 Swiss Style Posters; Grids; Mark Clennon; Poster Co; Incite, OFF BRiEF, Suno); BLACK BOX is the extreme case — the same junction is already marked five times (wedge tip, cube's upper-left corner, L-shaped rim, the two hairlines with ticks and the '684 × 385' label, the tagline block ending ~8 px above the cube), so the star adds a sixth mark and a second, wrong meaning (plus/cross). Removal weakened work only where the star was the sole spark or the subject (alo.ai; Phantom Glowing; Four-Pointed Star and 'Reach for Stars' posters; Portfolio 2026) — none of those conditions holds for BLACK BOX. CHANGE TO TEST: DELETE the 38 px upright star entirely from (658,770) — remove it, add nothing in its place, change nothing else. FALSIFICATION: if the junction now feels unmarked or the poster loses its spark, the star was doing real work and the next loop should revisit it as a concave, vertically elongated mark at ~60–70 px; if, as the evidence predicts, the poster reads cleaner and the wedge-to-cube junction reads more deliberately without the plus, the deletion stands and the star should not return. Do NOT test an enlarged straight-sided star first — the evidence says that would only produce a larger, more prominent plus. LIMIT: sizes above are estimates from on-screen full views, not measurements from source files; the threshold is observational, not a measured rule.

- Loop 44 study (24 designs, running total 1,112): the wedge — the sole diagonal, its tip is the junction. Answer: the exact vertex junction. Top-5: (1) exact vertex junction — the diagonal ends precisely on a corner or vertex, or the diagonal is the hero's own edge, so the corner is the diagonal's end (La Filmoteca, Finnair, Euroteam corner-to-corner band, ARC, Geometric Tiles, DADA, ViV's V apex); the eye is given a single, verifiable meeting point; (2) full bleed / pierce-through — the diagonal runs off the poster's edge or straight through the hero (Star Wars lightsaber, 50 Swiss, Geometric Teal, 21.co, Vancouver Design Week); there is no visible endpoint, so there is nothing to judge as a miss — the strongest option when exactness cannot be guaranteed; (3) deliberate clear undershoot, with a terminal device — the diagonal stops well short of any corner, and the stop is declared intentional by a sharp point, a dot, a tick/arrow, a rounded pinch, or a large gap (Angle as Structure's floating red point, LIVE SESSION's dotted terminus, Interrupted Lines' central break); a small gap reads as a mistake, a large finished gap reads as a decision; (4) one repeated angle, usually 45° — successful diagonals overwhelmingly use 45° (or one steep angle, as in La Filmoteca), repeated across the system so the angle reads as a rule; shallow angles near 18° are almost absent except where the diagonal is the hero's own edge, and a shallow diagonal crossing a vertical or horizontal edge creates a long, sliding intersection that magnifies any error; (5) taper to a true point, or constant width — never a blunt near-point; tapered wedges end in a sharp point (Angle as Structure shard, DADA slivers, Thmanyah arrow), bands keep a constant width and end flat at an edge or corner (Euroteam, Geometric Teal); a taper that stops at a blunt 7 px flat end is the in-between state that reads as cut off rather than aimed; one-colour discipline supports all of the above (the diagonal is in the single hero colour, never a second accent). A near-miss — ending a little before a corner, or crossing a hero's edge just below/beside its corner with a blunt end — has NO successful example in the whole sample, and a shallow ~18° angle makes that failure mode worse. CHANGE TO TEST (endpoint, not angle, not a new element): keep the ~18° angle and the 16 px base, but re-aim and extend the wedge so it tapers to a true 0 px point that lands exactly on the cube's drawn upper-left vertex — and stops there, with no overshoot into the cube and no gap before it. In practice: change the taper from 16 px → 7 px to 16 px → 0 px over the full length, and move the centreline's endpoint onto the actual drawn vertex of the glass cube (its front/top-face vertex, not the bounding box), adjusting the centreline by the minimum needed to hit that vertex. Why not the alternatives: keeping the wedge as it is leaves the junction ambiguous (a 7 px blunt tip near a corner is precisely the 'cut off' reading, and at 18° any small vertical error slides a long way along the cube's edge); overshooting/piercing the cube would contradict the constraint that the cube stays whole with its pale face untouched and would put red across the hero; falling clearly short would need a terminal device and a large gap, stranding the wedge in the void and breaking the 'line into the corner' idea. If, on rebuilding, the drawn vertex does not lie on the current 18° line, the angle — not the junction — is what gives way, by the smallest amount needed.

- Loop 45 study (24 designs, running total 1,136): the hero's scale and position. Verdict: the cube is close but not right — slightly too small, too far right, and its 3 px right margin is an accidental touch (neither a clear margin nor a deliberate bleed). Top-5: (1) width misleads; height and area decide whether a hero feels monumental — a 16:9 hero at 63% width is only 36% tall and reads as a strip or banner; low heroes that work are 40–50% of poster height (TAXI SERVICE's taxi ~55% wide, ~50% tall, cropped; Black Sedan's large car ~95% wide, ~40% tall, cropped; Goals' footballer ~50% wide, ~55% tall); below ~38% height a wide contained hero needs cropping or a second hero to avoid reading as an inset; (2) contained vs cropped is itself a scale decision — fully contained heroes need a clear margin on every side (~5% of canvas, ~55–60 px at 1080 px) to read as deliberate; cropped heroes read 10–20% larger than their box because the eye completes them; the cube is contained but touches the right edge by 3 px — the worst of both, reading as an alignment error, not a bleed; (3) ground must be balanced or occupied, not merely empty — successful asymmetric heroes put something in the ground (TAXI SERVICE's small copy bottom-left, Honda's centred car with ~15% ground each side, Lücke Dachpartner's dark wedge in the lower-right ground, La Filmoteca's silhouettes); BLACK BOX's left ground holds only the spine microcopy and the wedge — too much unoccupied black for a single-hero card; (4) low placement works only when the hero is grounded, not floating — working low heroes sit on or near the bottom edge or are anchored by a base; the cube's bottom at 1000 px (80 px gap) is a reasonable footer gap, but combined with the 3 px right touch it floats on the right and sags on the left; keep the bottom at 1000 px, do not lower it into the footer; (5) when type is already the top hero, the image must be the bottom hero — and big enough to balance the type; dual-hero examples that resolve this (TAXI SERVICE, Black Sedan, Goals, THERE HE…) make the image 55–95% wide and low, so the two heroes stack as top and bottom bands; at 63% x 36% the cube is the smallest dual-hero image in the sample relative to its wordmark. CHANGE TO TEST: enlarge proportionally by 9.1% and move up-left, keeping the bottom fixed — new cube 746 x 420 at (274,580): 69.1% wide, 38.9% tall, 26.9% area (up from 22.6%); left margin 393 -> 274 px, right margin 3 -> 60 px (deliberate, same order as the 80 px bottom margin), top 615 -> 580 px, bottom stays 1000 px, centre (735,807) -> (647,790), aspect stays 16:9. It is the smallest proportional enlargement that crosses all four thresholds — height above ~38%, area above 26%, right margin a deliberate 60 px, left ground below ~28% — without moving the bottom or touching the footer. TWO CORRECTIONS TO THE STUDY (main Muse, verified against the card's known geometry): (a) the change as specified COLLIDES with the tagline — the tagline's glyphs end ~Y607 and the new cube top is Y580, so the tagline's second line would sit on the cube; the study flagged this as its main untested risk and could not verify it, but the tagline is at Y572–612, so the risk is real. Making the change work requires moving the tagline up 35 px (to Y537–577, still 8 px above the new cube top) AND the wordmark up 35 px (ink Y409–533 -> Y374–498) to restore the ~39 px gap between wordmark base and tagline top. (b) The wedge's new endpoint is the cube's DRAWN vertex, NOT the bounding-box corner — the study's falsification test wrongly says the wedge tip should be at (274,580); the drawn vertex sits inside the cube image at source (1184,715), which maps to ~(568,758) in the new placement (scale 746/3000 = 0.2487), so the wedge's new centreline is (0,560)->(568,758) at ~19.2 deg. Consequences that must travel with the change: wedge re-anchored to the new drawn vertex; measurement system re-anchored (X393 vertical -> X274, label '684 x 385' -> '746 x 420'); both halos resize/reposition with the cube; wordmark and tagline shift up 35 px. Rejected alternatives: much bigger 864 x 486 at (156,550) (dominates, squeezes the tagline band, wedge crosses almost the whole canvas); 782 x 440 at (238,560) (overshoot, lifts the top 55 px for no decisive gain); much smaller 540 x 304 (reads as a thumbnail/sticker, left void grows to 480 px); higher top ~450–500 px (collides with the tagline, breaks the low-anchor pattern); bottom-flush at 1080 px (sits on the footer); centred horizontally (destroys the lower-right asymmetry the wedge and spine depend on); full-bleed/cropped (cuts the cube's edges and vertex, breaks the measurement label); shift-left-only 684 x 385 at (333,615) (fixes the 3 px touch but leaves height 35.6% and left void 333 px — a half-measure). Falsification: the change is wrong if the new box is not exactly 746 x 420 at (274,580), the right margin is not 60 px, the bottom is not 1000 px, the wedge tip is not exactly at the new drawn vertex ~(568,758), the label does not read '746 x 420', the tagline has less than 20 px clear above y=580 or overlaps the cube, the footer is touched, the cube still reads as a strip at a 270 px thumbnail, or the cube is visibly softer than the 684 px version (if so, re-render natively at 746 px; if impossible, revert).
- Loop 45 build notes (v45, 2026-11-07): the hero's scale and position.
