- Loop 56 study (30+ designs, running total ~1,463): does X274 alone still earn its place? VERDICT: KEEP X274 EXACTLY AS IT IS — a FREEZE, no visual change. Top-5: (1) a solitary dimension earns its place only when it is exactly anchored at both ends to the thing it measures — a stray is offset, of arbitrary length, or free at one end; X274 passes three times over (length exactly 420 px, the cube's height; ends exactly Y580 and Y1000, the cube's top and bottom; X exactly 274, the cube's left edge — it is the cube's height, traced); (2) judged by blueprint rules alone, one dimension always fails, because blueprints never isolate one dimension (orthogonal pairs, extension lines, many labels, each label mid-span and breaking its own line, each label stating only the span of that line) — but BLACK BOX is not a blueprint, it is a poster with one technical cue, and applying the full blueprint test to a poster is the wrong test; the poster test is whether one restrained cue reads as deliberate, and X274 does, because of finding 1; (3) in Swiss, minimal and editorial work, a single quiet hairline survives when it aligns to a grid or object edge and does a second job — divider, spine, or scale bar (a lone line must touch something at both ends, or be the composition's axis); X274 does a second job — it is simultaneously the cube's left edge and a vertical scale bar for its height; a line in the middle of the black field, 50 px from the cube, would be a stray, but this one is on the cube; (4) label proximity is grouping and alignment, not raw distance — and X274's label is already correctly grouped (starts X223, 51 px from the line, Y583, 3 px below the top tick, reads left-to-right beside the line it belongs to — the standard position for a vertical scale bar: beside the bar, aligned to one end; a scale bar is not required to have its label at mid-span; moving the label to mid-span, about Y786, would put it in empty black field, detached from both ticks, reading as a floating caption; moving it closer would close the small clearance it needs from the 12 px ticks and the line itself, with no gain in grouping — at 7 pt the label already sits immediately left of the line; in blueprints mid-span works only because the label breaks the line, but here the label sits beside the line, so end-alignment is the correct convention, and it is already used); (5) the ticks are what turn an edge into a dimension, and 12 px in #989CA1 at both ends is the minimum that does it (without ticks X274 would be only the cube's left edge; with a tick at Y580 and a tick at Y1000, and nowhere else, the span is closed and the line is declared a measurement; longer ticks, arrowheads, a brighter colour, or a tick at one end only would all break that declaration — longer ticks would extend into the glass cube and into the black field and become a second graphic; arrowheads would import a full drafting system that one line cannot support; brighter ticks would out-shout the 7 pt label; the existing hierarchy is also correct — the line is the quieter #43413E, the ticks the lighter #989CA1, so the closures, not the shaft, carry the signal; a stray has no closures, X274 has two, at exactly the right places). KEEP, WITH EXACT NUMBERS: line X274, 2 px, #43413E, Y580→Y1000; ticks 12 px, #989CA1, at Y580 and Y1000; label '746 × 420', Montserrat Regular 7 pt, #989CA1, at X223, Y583; no other element moves. Rejected: move the label closer to X274 (already left-adjacent, 51 px from its start to the line, top-aligned to the top tick — closing the gap risks touching the 12 px tick zone and the line, crowds a 7 pt string, and does not improve grouping; the problem, if there is one, is not distance); move the label to mid-span, about Y786 (detaches the label from both ticks, floats it in empty black, borrows the blueprint mid-span convention without the blueprint device that makes it work — the label breaking the line); change the ticks — lengthen them (12 px→20 px), make them arrowheads, brighten them to white, or keep only one (longer/brighter ticks become a second graphic and compete with the tagline and footer; arrowheads demand a full drafting system; one tick leaves the span open at one end and makes the line look unfinished); delete X274 and its label (removes the card's only measurement and leaves the 'ENGINEERING DOCUMENTARY' spine and the technical premise unsupported — the minimal posters that delete measurement do so because they have no measurement theme; BLACK BOX's spine, tagline about systems and failures, and cube-as-specimen all promise a document, so deleting the one dimension would make the card a generic black poster with a cube; if the label were kept without the line it would be orphaned; if both went, the engineering signal would be gone); restore X53.7, or add a horizontal dimension for the 746 width (loop 55 deleted X53.7, and restoring it reintroduces the pair that was judged not to earn its place; adding a width dimension would turn the poster into the blueprint system of finding 2, which the card has already moved away from). VERIFICATION BY MAIN MUSE: v56 = v55 byte-identical (sha256 5e2ac91b) — the export already carries the exact numbers (X274 vertical y581–998, ticks 24 grey px each, label present at Y583). FALSIFICATION: (a) PASSES — the 2 px #43413E line is VISIBLE at thumbnail size, not invisible with only the ticks showing (270 px thumbnail, X274 column band mean max-channel 40.9 vs background 2.8 — a clear continuous vertical; the max 214 in mid-span is the wedge crossing the column at y~164); (b)/(c)/(d) NOT RUN — they need fresh viewers and a deletion variant, which were not available; reported honestly to Kind. The KEEP stands on the geometry (exact anchoring) and the visible line. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 57 — the remaining doubt is semantic, not geometric: the label reads '746 × 420' — a whole-image width × height — while the line it sits beside measures only the height, 420. Does that two-number spec belong on a vertical-only line, or should the line carry '420' alone and the '746 × 420' spec, if it is needed at all, live elsewhere (for example as an image specification in the footer)?
- Loop 57 study (27 designs, running total ~1,490): the label reads '746 × 420' while the line measures only the height, 420. VERDICT: NO, the two-number spec does not belong on a vertical-only line — change the line label to '420' alone, and move the whole-image spec to the footer as a labelled image specification ('IMAGE 746 × 420', centre-aligned on X540, at Y1020). Top-5: (1) one line, one number — a dimension line states only its own span (the VW Logo Specification Poster labels its horizontal line 'D', its vertical line 'K', its small-square lines 'S' and 'C', its angles '24°' — one value or letter per line, with the values decoded in a key at the foot; the Ferrari F40 and Porsche 911 four-view blueprints likewise put a single number on each horizontal and vertical dimension line; floor plans do the same on their perimeter lines; a '×' on a 1-D line is a category error — '×' claims a two-dimensional area); (2) a two-number spec is honest only where two dimensions are present — inside the 2-D area it describes (floor-plan rooms labelled inside the room), as two separate labelled lines, one per axis, or in a table or legend where each number is labelled; it never belongs as the label of a single axis; (3) whole-object specs belong in a separate, labelled block, apart from the drawing (Ferrari F40 puts 'SPECIFICATIONS' in a table at top left; the Lamborghini Huracán poster puts 'Dimensions and Weight' in a labelled text block — Length/Width/Height each on its own line, never collapsed to one '×' string on a line; room-size tables put '22×28', '12 x 14 ft' in table cells under column headings; the VW poster puts its values in a legend; the Sofa instructions put their two-number part spec in a labelled parts table, not on a line); (4) a dimension is optional — so if one is kept, it must be true (the Technics SL-1200MK2 exploded poster carries no dimension line at all, only numbered leader lines to parts; the Sofa instructions carry none; Müller-Brockmann's Beethoven/Stadttheater/Strawinsky posters carry none; album and magazine covers carry none — their technical authority comes from numbering, grids and leader lines, not from a dimension; dropping the spec would not make BLACK BOX less technical, but keeping a false one does); (5) file/print size is metadata, not a measurement — a bare '746 × 420' with no noun can be misread as the poster size (1080×1080) or the cube's real-world size (wherever a size appears in the designs studied, it is labelled by what it is and set with the other metadata — in a spec block, a colophon/footer, a parts table, or a barcode area — never floating as an unlabelled number on a drawing). TESTABLE CHANGE: (a) line label: change the text at X223, Y583 from '746 × 420' to '420' — keep Montserrat Regular 7 pt, #989CA1, same position; X274 hairline unchanged (2 px #43413E, Y580→Y1000, exactly 420 px), 12 px ticks in #989CA1 at Y580 and Y1000 unchanged; bare '420', no 'px', no 'H', no '×' — the existing label carried no unit, and the ticks already define the span; (b) footer centre: add 'IMAGE 746 × 420', centre-aligned on X540, at Y1020, in exactly the same type, size and colour as the existing footer items '@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB' (left) and 'VOL. 01' (right) — the footer centre is currently empty, so nothing moves and nothing is crowded; 'IMAGE' — not 'CUBE' — because 746 × 420 are the placed image's pixel dimensions, not the depicted cube's real-world size, and it distinguishes the spec from the 1080×1080 poster; (c) everything else locked. WHY: the line becomes literally true (it spans 420, and says 420); the width is not lost, it is preserved where the precedents put whole-image specs — with the other metadata, labelled; and the measurement system stays exactly ONE dimension, as loop 56 kept it. NOTE: loop 36's rule was 'if no new fact, leave centre empty' — now there IS a new fact (the image spec), so the centre gets filled; that is consistent, not a reversal. Rejected: keep '746 × 420' on the line (fails finding 1 — every design studied contradicts it, and a reader must either think the vertical line also spans 746 or that 746 is a second vertical measure); change the label to '420' alone and drop 746 entirely (honest and clean, the runner-up — rejected only because it deletes the only record of the width while the footer centre sits empty; acceptable if the footer is ever judged inviolable and must stay two-item); add a horizontal dimension line for 746 (no room and forbidden by the loop 55/56 decision — the cube's top edge Y580 is only 8 px below the tagline glyphs ending ~Y572, a bottom line at Y1000 would collide with the footer at Y1020 and the X274 bottom tick, and it would restore a second dimension); put '746 × 420' inside/over the glass cube (finding 2 allows a pair inside a 2-D area in principle, but the cube is not empty white space — it would obscure the hero, fight the wedge point at (568,758), and be illegible at 7 pt over glass; no precedent does this); put the spec in the spine or tagline (the spine is vertical microcopy and the tagline is locked, loops 46–53 closed); label the line 'H 420' or '420 px' ('H' duplicates what the vertical line already says, 'px' is a second, unasked change — keep the card's existing bare-number convention); move a bare '746 × 420' to the footer with no noun (fails finding 5 — it could still be read as the poster size; the word 'IMAGE' is doing the work). Falsification (in the export, at 100%): show the export to a fresh reader who has not seen the loops and ask (i) 'What does the X274 line measure?' (ii) 'What does IMAGE 746 × 420 refer to?' — the change is WRONG if the reader says the line measures 746, or both numbers, or reads '420' as a page/volume/count; if they say the footer spec is the poster size or the cube's real-world size; if the centred footer item overlaps or crowds '@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB' / 'VOL. 01'; or if '420' at X223, Y583 is read as belonging to the tagline, wedge or cube top edge rather than the X274 line. Measure the export too: label still at X223, Y583; footer item centred on X540 at Y1020; line still exactly Y580→Y1000. If the footer spec is still read as a second dimension for the X274 line (it sits 20 px below its Y1000 end, but 266 px away horizontally at X540), the move has failed and the correct fallback is the runner-up: drop the spec entirely and keep '420' alone. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 58 — does a fresh reader actually use the footer spec, or is 'IMAGE 746 × 420' ignored metadata — i.e. is the spec needed at all, and is the single '420' line itself needed, or is it decorative?
- Loop 57 build notes (v57, 2026-11-07): the label's wording — '746 × 420' → '420' on the line, spec moved to the footer. CHANGE: (a) line label retyped from '746 × 420' to '420' at X223, Y583 (Montserrat Regular 7 pt, #989CA1, same position; width 44.7 px → 17.6 px); (b) footer centre: 'IMAGE 746 × 420' added, centre-aligned on X540, at Y1020, made by duplicating '@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB' so it inherits the exact type/size/colour. X274 hairline and ticks untouched; footer left/right items untouched. VERIFIED BY MAIN MUSE: v57 sha256 cf040800 (391,067 bytes); 749 px changed vs v55, bbox x223–605, y584–1033 (label zone + footer centre zone only), 0 px changed elsewhere; label zone bright px 45 (was 92); label ink (excluding the tick row) x223–239, width 17 px, matching the worker's 17.6 px; footer centre ink x473–605, centre 539.0–540.0 (threshold-dependent, centred on X540), y1022–1033; footer left/right bright px identical to v55 (734 and 160); X274 vertical y581–998, ticks 24 px each; tagline white rows 541–572, identical; wordmark gaps identical; spine grey px 156, unchanged; wedge centreline strong red (199,46,48) at (300,665). FALSIFICATION (measurable criteria): label still at X223, Y583 ✓; footer item centred on X540 at Y1020 ✓; line still exactly Y580→Y1000 ✓; footer centre item clear of left/right items (x473–605 vs left ending ~x180, right starting ~x977) ✓; '420' does not sit on the wedge (wedge at x223 is y~638, label at y583) ✓; footer spec is 20 px below the X274 line's Y1000 end but 266 px away horizontally, and labelled 'IMAGE', so it reads as footer metadata, not a second dimension ✓. Fresh-reader questions NOT RUN — no fresh viewers available, reported honestly to Kind. SPEC CORRECTION (from the export's pixels): the footer items are Montserrat 12 pt, Grey #9a9a9a (max pixel (154,154,154)), NOT #989CA1 — the ticks and the dimension label are #989CA1 (152,156,161), but the footer is a different grey. Record this: footer = 12 pt #9a9a9a; ticks/label = #989CA1. The new footer centre item matches the existing footer items because it was duplicated from one. BUILD LESSON: when a text box is much wider than its text (300 px box, ~124 px text), centring the box does NOT centre the text — measure the INK's centre in the export and shift the box so the ink is centred (the worker shifted +80.8 px; ink centre 540). v57 PNG: ~/workspace/browser_downloads/sess-677548892/browser-download-20261107T014816.381405326Z-5-BLACK_BOX_test_card.png (391,067 bytes). Running total: ~1,463 + 27 = ~1,490 designs studied through loop 57. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 58 — does a fresh reader actually use the footer spec, or is 'IMAGE 746 × 420' ignored metadata — i.e. is the spec needed at all, and is the single '420' line itself needed, or is it decorative?
- Loop 58 study (30+ designs, running total ~1,520): does a reader actually use the footer spec, or is 'IMAGE 746 × 420' ignored metadata — and is the single '420' line needed, or decorative? VERDICT: DROP BOTH — delete the X274 dimension entirely (hairline, both ticks, '420' label) AND delete the footer centre item 'IMAGE 746 × 420'. Top-5: (1) footer metadata is read only when it changes a decision, identifies the thing, or is actionable (a date/venue, issue/series identity, or the name that distinguishes otherwise identical shapes) — 'IMAGE 746 × 420' does none of these: no reader chooses, files, prints, crops or understands BLACK BOX differently for knowing the hero image is 746×420 px; it is a file fact about something already visible, and it is not even the card's size (1080×1080); it is ignored metadata; (2) most small footer microcopy in posters is texture, signature or series rhythm, not information — and it is only defended as a repeated system (Vol. V repeats 'BLOCKVOID 2025', 'READ +', '08 OBJ FOUND' at the foot of every poster; 50 Swiss Style Posters use lorem-ipsum body copy as grey texture; minimal posters carry only '01' or a one-line motto) — BLACK BOX's footer is not such a system: left = who (@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB), right = which volume (VOL. 01), a coherent two-item identity/volume system, and centre = a file size, a third, unrelated category; the centre item breaks the system it sits in; (3) a dimension line is functional only when it is complete enough to check, build or compare (Technics SL-1200 is the control case: three sheets, exploded parts, many dimensions, a title block and a spec table — 'From afar it is clean graphic art. Up close it is a working technical drawing'; the Pinterest blueprints are the same — dimensions everywhere, tied to views and parts, with units, tables or a formula) — a single dimension, no unit, no second dimension, no tolerance and no part label cannot check anything; '420' alone cannot tell a reader whether the cube is correct, tall or to scale, least of all when the line sits flush on the cube's own left edge (X274), so its length is self-evident from the cube itself; it is a quotation of engineering, not engineering; (4) when a spec IS the poster, it is the content, not the footer (in Technics, Race Circuits, Retro Weapons Blueprints and Gear Ratio, the drawing and its labels are the hero, large and central) — BLACK BOX inverts this: the spec is banished to the two least-read places on the card, 7 pt at X223 in the left margin and 12 pt grey at Y1020 in the centre of the footer; placement itself tells the reader not to use it (brand identity confirms the split from the other side: grids, clearspace and dimensions appear on separate construction pages, never on the hero lock-up; album art puts any spec on the spine or back, the front is title/artist only; editorial puts a folio/page number inside, never a spec on the cover); (5) redundancy kills both items at once — the footer ('746 × 420') and the line ('420') state the same height twice, in two sizes and two places, neither with a unit, and neither adds what the other lacks in a usable way (the footer alone gives the width, 746; the line alone gives a drawn length); a reader who notices one discounts the other as decoration, and a reader who notices neither loses nothing; two measurements also imply a measurement system, but the card defines it as 'exactly ONE dimension' — a system of one is a contradiction; loop 55 already proved the card survives deleting a measurement line (X53.7), while loops 56–57 kept and moved this one without testing whether anyone uses it. TESTABLE CHANGE: (a) delete the X274 dimension entirely — the 2 px #43413E hairline Y580→Y1000, both 12 px #989CA1 ticks at Y580 and Y1000, and the '420' label (Montserrat Regular 7 pt #989CA1 at X223, Y583); (b) delete the footer centre item 'IMAGE 746 × 420' (Montserrat 12 pt #9a9a9a, centre-aligned on X540, Y1020). Keep exactly, at the same coordinates: '@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB' X53.7, Y1020 and 'VOL. 01' X1026.3, Y1020; the cube 746×420 at (274,580); the wordmark, the locked tagline X282, Y537–577, the spine, and the red wedge ending at (568,758). Do not recentre the remaining footer items, do not add a replacement centre item, and do not add any new line. The lower-left quadrant Y580–Y1000, X20–274 becomes plain black; the footer becomes a two-item footer with a blank centre. This is a pure deletion test, not a redesign, and it is honest for an 'ENGINEERING DOCUMENTARY' card: a decorative dimension is a false claim to measurement, and removing it leaves the documentary claim to the spine, tagline and cube, which actually carry it. Rejected: keep both (fails findings 1, 3 and 5 — no poster in the 30+ studied repeats a file size in the footer and a single dimension beside the image; keeping both preserves a false measurement system and ignored metadata); drop the footer spec only, keep the '420' line (keeps the weaker of the two — the line gives only height, no unit, no width — and removes the only place the width 746 is stated, leaving '420' even more orphaned, and keeps a 420 px vertical whose only cross-reference has been deleted); drop the '420' line only, keep the footer spec (keeps the most ignored element of all — centre footer, 12 pt grey on black, Y1020, the last-read position, stating a file fact — and removes the only element that at least draws the eye to the cube's height); merge them, e.g. label the line '746 × 420', enlarge '420' to 12 pt, or add 'px' (a bigger or better-labelled single dimension is still a single, unactionable dimension; 'px' does not give any reader a task to perform with it, and enlarging it would compete with the tagline and wedge); add a second dimension line for 746 (top or bottom of the cube) to 'complete' the system (would change the hero, add a horizontal rule across the cube's top/bottom, and still answer no reader question — nobody needs the hero image's pixel size to read a test card; completing a measurement nobody uses is worse than deleting it); move the spec to the spine or tagline area (both are closed/locked, and a file size would contaminate them). Falsification (export the drop-both version at 1080×1080 and judge it against the keep-both version; either failure overturns the change): (a) graphic failure in the export itself — if the lower-left quadrant Y580–Y1000, X20–274 now reads as an unfinished or unbalanced void that pulls the eye away from the cube and tagline, or the footer reads as a mistake (two items stranded at the edges with a ~900 px blank centre that looks like a missing item), or the cube's left edge at X274 now floats with no anchor and the red wedge's point at (568,758) loses its reference, then the line was load-bearing graphically, not informationally, and the deletion was wrong as a composition even if it was right as information (check at full size and at 50% thumbnail, where the footer gap and the empty quadrant are most visible); (b) reader-task failure — show both versions for 5 seconds each to 5 fresh readers and give them a task that needs the spec (e.g. 'recreate this cube at the same height on a 1080 canvas' or 'tell me the image's pixel size'); if 3 or more of the 5 can answer from the keep-both version and none can from the drop-both version, and they say the missing number blocked the task, the spec was used and the change was wrong; if, as the evidence predicts, readers in both versions either do not look at the footer/line at all or cannot recall the numbers after 5 seconds, the change stands. Honest limit (stated by the study): this loop did no eye-tracking or reader testing; the conclusion is an inference from placement, repetition, completeness and genre contract across the designs above, not a measured reading — if the falsification test in (b) contradicts it, that test, not this study, should decide loop 59. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 59 — does a two-item footer (@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB / VOL. 01) with a blank centre hold at poster distance, or does the centre need a different, non-spec item — and does the empty lower-left quadrant need any vertical at all, or is plain black the correct Swiss answer?

NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 59 — does a two-item footer (@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB / VOL. 01) with a blank centre hold at poster distance, or does the centre need a different, non-spec item — and does the empty lower-left quadrant need any vertical at all, or is plain black the correct Swiss answer?
- Loop 59 study (28 designs, running total ~1,548): does a two-item footer with a blank centre hold at poster distance, and does the empty lower-left quadrant need any vertical at all? VERDICT: KEEP BOTH EXACTLY AS THEY ARE (LOCK) — a FREEZE, no visual change. Top-5: (1) a blank centre holds when the two footer items are true bookends — same baseline, same size and colour, at the extreme edges (the closest direct analogue is POCKET ROCKET FOUNDATION.: its bottom row is '179.99' and '$' with the centre empty, and it reads as a designed table row, not a missing item; the same bookend logic holds in Explosion/Lines and Jacob Karlzon. Wanderlust.) — BLACK BOX meets all three bookend conditions (@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB at X53.7 and VOL. 01 at X1026.3, both Montserrat 12 pt, Grey #9a9a9a, both at Y1020); (2) a blank centre reads as missing only when something implies a third slot — a rule or grid with three columns, a three-item header above a two-item footer, or items not sharing a baseline (I found no clean two-item edge footer in the grids that read as a mistake; the failure mode in the grids was the opposite — a weak, smaller centre item that looked orphaned) — BLACK BOX has no rule, no three-column system anywhere (wordmark centred, spine left, tagline left), so nothing implies a third footer slot; the deleted centre spec (loop 58) must not return in any form; (3) plain black in the quadrant opposite a single off-centre hero is the correct Swiss answer, and it holds at thumbnail/poster distance (ESSENCE: giant top wordmark, vast empty field, one tiny figure, one small text block bottom-left, lower-right empty — the emptiness is the design; Sonnet Posters, Arrival, BOKKA – Blood Moon, Student Cultural Centre, Jacob Karlzon — in every case the eye does not hunt for fill opposite the hero); (4) a vertical, mark or texture is needed only when the quadrant is an information zone, or when the hero is centred and symmetrical so the eye has no diagonal to follow (counter-examples: posters#002, a programme poster where density is its brief; Typographic Posters II, where type itself fills every quadrant; 50 Swiss Style Posters, dense grids; SL24, brutalist data-logistics, every cell filled; Demokratie, a black bar filling the lower quadrant) — BLACK BOX is neither: its hero is right (cube 746×420 at (274,580)), and its lower-left already has a gesture — the red #C32D30 wedge entering from the left edge and ending in a sharp point exactly on the cube's upper-left vertex at (568,758); the wedge is the lower-left's content, and a vertical would duplicate its job; (5) at poster distance there is no safe middle size for anything added in either place — anything at or below ~12–13 pt in the lower-left, or a 12 pt centre footer item, is invisible at 25% / 270 px wide and at 3 m (it would change nothing at the distance this loop is about), while anything large enough to be seen (roughly ≥24 pt, or ≥2% of the 1080 width) becomes a second hero / second headline and competes with the cube, the wedge point and the tagline; the choice is therefore binary — plain black / blank centre, or a competing element — and the evidence says the competing element is wrong here. KEEP, WITH EXACT NUMBERS: footer — @BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB left at X53.7, Y1020, and VOL. 01 right at X1026.3, Y1020, Montserrat 12 pt, Grey #9a9a9a, centre EMPTY; no rule, no centre item, no movement inward, no size or colour change. Lower-left quadrant — X0–274, Y580–1000 plain black, exactly as it is, with the red wedge unchanged; no vertical microcopy, no mark, no texture, no grain, no second cube, no text. Rejected: centred non-spec item, e.g. a grey '—', '●', 'EST. 2026', 'DOCUMENTARY', or a repeated 'BLACK BOX' at X540, Y1020, 12 pt #9a9a9a (invisible at poster distance; if enlarged to be visible it becomes a third headline and re-creates the loop-58 centre-spec problem in a new costume); centred red mark, e.g. an 8×8 px #C32D30 square at X536–544, Y1016–1024 (creates a second red focal point at the bottom centre, pulling the eye away from the wedge point at (568,758) and the cube; red on this card is the wedge's alone); any centred spec / dimension / label (expressly deleted in loop 58; no spec anywhere on the card); a vertical in the lower-left, e.g. rotated microcopy at X20–40, Y600–950, or a vertical rule at X137, Y600–1000 (duplicates the existing left spine and boxes in the wedge; two left-edge verticals would read as a frame, not Swiss air); a small mark, texture or grain in the lower-left, e.g. a 60×60 px cube outline, dot grid, or noise at X60–200, Y700–900 (at poster distance it reads as dirt / a printing fault on pure black, or, if bolder, as a second hero competing with the 746×420 cube); a footer rule spanning X53.7–1026.3 at Y1005, or moving the footer items inward to 'close' the gap (a rule would imply the three-column table that finding 2 warns about, and moving the items inward would destroy the bookend condition that makes the blank centre work); filling the lower-left with tagline-sized type (the tagline is locked above the cube at X282; repeating its register below would split the message in two). Falsification (export at 1080×1080, view at 270 px wide (25%) and from 3 m, 5-second glance, with three viewers who have not seen the brief): 'keep both' is falsified if, unprompted, two or more of the three point at the footer centre and say something is missing there, OR describe the lower-left (X0–274, Y580–1000) as 'unfinished', 'cropped' or 'a black hole' rather than 'empty' / 'calm', OR read the footer as one item plus a stray dot; a vague 'I would add something' with no pointing does not count. If falsified, the next test in loop 60 is the smallest possible single change, not both at once: a centred grey '—' (Montserrat 12 pt, #9a9a9a, centred on X540, Y1020) for the footer only, re-tested the same way — and if that fails, the footer change is reverted before any lower-left change is tried. VERIFICATION BY MAIN MUSE: v59 = v58 byte-identical (sha256 cc0b949e) — no rebuild was done. The measurable bookend conditions hold in the export (footer left 734 bright px and right 160 bright px, both at Y1020–1033, same colour #9a9a9a, at the extreme edges; lower-left quadrant mean RGB (16.9, 3.9, 4.1) — plain black except the wedge's red glow). The fresh-viewer part of the falsification test was NOT RUN — no fresh viewers available, reported honestly to Kind. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 60 — finding 2's real risk is not the blank centre but faintness: does a 12 pt #9a9a9a footer on black register at all at poster distance, and if not, is the correct answer white 12 pt, grey 13 pt, or no footer at all? That is a legibility question, not a centre-item question, and it should be tested at 270 px / 3 m before any centre or lower-left element is reconsidered.
- Loop 60 study (38 designs, running total ~1,586): footer legibility at poster distance — does a 12 pt #9a9a9a footer on black register at all at 270 px / 3 m? VERDICT: KEEP THE FOOTER EXACTLY AS IT IS — a FREEZE, no visual change. The numbers: #9a9a9a on pure black has a contrast ratio of ~7.46:1 (relative luminance ≈ 0.323), which passes WCAG AAA for normal text (7:1); white would be 21:1 (2.8× the contrast), but contrast is not what fails at distance. Size is what fails, and no offered change fixes it: 12 pt on a 1080 px canvas is a 16 px em; at 270 px wide that is a 4 px em, ~2.8 px cap height, with a Regular stroke of ~0.5 px; 13 pt is 4.3 px — an 8.3% gain, still sub-legible; white 12 pt is the same 4 px; at 3 m that is at or below normal acuity for a letter stroke, and grey's lower effective contrast at high spatial frequency pushes it under the threshold. A footer that genuinely read at 270 px / 3 m would need roughly 28–32 pt (a 9–11 px em at that scale), 2.5× larger, at which point it stops being a footer and becomes a third headline. The premise of the loop question is therefore confirmed, but it is not a defect: in every design examined, the small footer/credit does not register as words at poster distance — that is its job; it is a close-range whisper (attribution and series marker), read after the wordmark, tagline and cube, at arm's length. Top-5: (1) at 270 px / 3 m, nothing at ~12 pt registers as words, grey or white (Pinterest's 'GET BETTER EVERY DAY' at exactly 270 px wide: the giant grey wordmark registers, the small grey footer is a faint smudge — its first line only becomes readable when the crop is enlarged 2×, its second paragraph never does; the same happens to the dense small white text at the foot of 'IMPACT' and 'DEMISE', and to the top microcopy block of '"Elements Unveiled"', which reads as a grey band at tile size); (2) what registers at distance is size and isolation, not colour ('Student Cultural Centre': white text roughly 3× footer size, centred on black, is fully readable at a 355 px tile; the isolated mark 'JTM' at the foot of 'Elements Unveiled' registers while the two credit lines directly above it do not; wordmarks survive distance, their small print does not); (3) grey works close up and fails only in combination with small size, distance and dim light (the 'Poster Black Friday Sale Template' is the direct analogue: a small grey paragraph on black is a grey smudge at tile size but is plainly a paragraph at full size; 'Movie Poster – Black Earth' and 'MAGAZINE POSTER (BLACK)' show the same split — title readable, small credits not — with no loss of quality at full size); (4) the working alternatives in the wild are not 'white 12 pt' or 'grey 13 pt' — nobody does those; designers do one of three things: (a) accept distance invisibility and keep the footer as close-range credit (serial/editorial work); (b) delete the footer entirely (single-image works with nothing to credit); or (c) jump to ~24–30 pt bold white, which changes the element's job from credit to message; no sampled design used a 1 pt bump or a colour-only swap to fix distance legibility, because neither can; (5) a grey footer is a hierarchy signal, and whitening it flattens the card — the card already has two white text levels (128 pt ExtraBold wordmark; 13 pt Regular tagline); a third white level at the bottom edge would frame the card in white top and bottom, promote '@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB' / 'VOL. 01' to the same visual rank as the tagline, and break the plain-black lower-left quadrant locked in loop 59; grey #9a9a9a says 'read me last, up close', which is exactly the footer's role. Rejected: white 12 pt (same 4 px at 270 px, so it still does not read at 3 m; at close range it gains nothing the footer needs — it already passes AAA — and costs hierarchy, a bright bottom band competing with the tagline and the cube, and a white frame effect with the wordmark); grey 13 pt (+8% size, 0% contrast gain, still illegible at distance, and it breaks a locked specification for a difference no viewer could detect — a noise change); delete the footer entirely (the only alternative with a real distance benefit — nothing to misread, cleaner black field — and the correct answer for a one-off image poster; it fails here because the footer carries the only attribution and the only series marker on the card — without '@BLACKBOXSYSTEMSLAB' a close viewer cannot find the maker, and without 'VOL. 01' the series framing has no on-card evidence; the sampled serial works all keep their credit line for exactly this reason; rejected, but the clear second choice, ahead of white 12 pt). Falsification (export at 1080×1080, display at 270 px wide, in normal room light, with three viewers of normal/corrected vision): (1) at 3 m, ask each to read the footer aloud — expect 0/3 to read either item word-for-word (that is the predicted, acceptable result); (2) at 0.5 m / arm's length, ask each to read it aloud — expect 3/3 to read both items within 5 seconds, first try; (3) ask whether anything at the bottom edge looks like dirt, a scratch, a rendering error or a stray mark. 'Keep' is falsified if: fewer than 3/3 read it at close range (grey is then genuinely too dim and white 12 pt, not grey 13 pt, becomes the fix); or ≥2/3 call it dirt/a defect at either distance (then delete it); or a viewer at 3 m reads it word-for-word (then the distance premise was wrong and the footer is over-prominent, so darken it). If it passes, the footer is doing its job and the question is closed. VERIFICATION BY MAIN MUSE: v60 = v58 byte-identical (sha256 cc0b949e). Contrast ratio computed independently: #9a9a9a on black = 7.46:1, passes WCAG AAA (7:1) — the study's number is correct. Footer at 270 px: the footer band shows only a faint grey smudge (a few dozen bright px, no distinguishable letterforms) — matching the prediction that 0/3 would read it word-for-word at 3 m. Footer at 100%: glyph height ~11 px (12 pt = 16 px em), clean ink, no stray pixels — readable at arm's length, nothing that looks like dirt or a defect. The fresh-viewer part of the falsification test was NOT RUN — no fresh viewers available, reported honestly to Kind. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 61 — distance hierarchy between the two things that DO register at 3 m: does the red #C32D30 wedge's sharp point at (568,758) read as a deliberate pointer to the cube at poster distance, or does it vanish into the cube's edge, leaving only the wordmark and the cube?
- Loop 61 study (24 designs, running total ~1,610): does the red wedge's sharp point read as a deliberate pointer to the cube at poster distance (270 px / 3 m)? VERDICT: IT VANISHES AS A POINTER — CHANGE THE ENDPOINT, do not keep it as it is. The red body still reads as a diagonal slash in the plain-black lower left, but its point does not read as pointing at the cube — because it touches the cube, inside the cube's own busy diagonal drawing, and because a tapering point gets thinner exactly where it needs to be widest. Top-5: (1) area beats line — a diagonal reads at 270 px / 3 m only if it is a shape, not a line; the survivors are all at least ~24–28 px wide on the full-size card (6–7 px at 270 px) for a sustained stretch near their tip; a wedge that tapers to zero is thinnest exactly where the eye must locate it, so its last 30–40 px fall below the 12–16 px legibility threshold and disappear into anti-aliasing; (2) a gap beats a touch — no successful pointer in the set touches its target; arrows, bands and triangles that stop clear of their target read as deliberate gestures, while a tip that lands exactly on a drawn vertex has no figure–ground separation, so the eye cannot tell where red ends and target begins; BLACK BOX's central defect: its point at (568,758) is not on the cube's outer silhouette against black, it is inside the cube's bounding box (cube 274–1020 × 580–1000), on a drawn vertex inside a glass image; (3) colour alone does not save a thin tip — #C32D30 on plain black is high-contrast for a field, but a thin red tip against the cube's own dark glass, highlights and edges loses chroma and edge contrast; red survives at distance as area, while as a hairline against a busy photograph it becomes a stain on the cube, not a pointer to it; (4) competing diagonals kill a single diagonal — the cube is itself a drawing of isometric diagonals, and the wedge adds one more diagonal at a similar scale, with no difference in width, angle or separation to mark it as a separate gesture; many diagonals at thumbnail size collapse into texture and the viewer cannot follow any one of them; the wedge's length (~568 px to the point, 142 px at 270 px) is ample — length is not the problem, distinctness at the endpoint is; (5) the three distance tricks are thickness, repetition, and a widened head — and the wedge uses none of them at its tip (thickness, repetition, and a head wider than its shaft are what survive; a long taper is the inverse of all three); the wedge's body, in the plain-black lower-left quadrant, probably does read as a red diagonal slash at distance, but its point does not read as pointing. THE CHANGE (loop 61): shorten the wedge and re-shape its tip, in one move — (a) pull the tip back 24 px along the wedge's own centreline, so the new tip stops 24 px short of (568,758), leaving a 24 px plain-black gap between red tip and cube vertex (6 px at 270 px; 6 mm on a 270 mm print); keep the same left-edge entry, same angle and same centreline, so the extension of the centreline still passes through (568,758) within ±4 px; (b) steepen the final taper so the wedge is at least 24 px wide (measured perpendicular to the centreline) at 24 px back from the new tip, and reaches its point only in the final 12 px — the tip becomes a short, steep point / small arrowhead, not a long hair taper. Why these numbers: 24 px is the smallest gap that is unambiguously a gap at 3 m (~7× acuity) while still reading as 'pointing at' rather than 'missing'; a 12 px gap would be marginal (3 px at 270 px) and a 40 px+ gap would start to read as a miss; the 24 px near-tip width puts the visible head above the 12–16 px legibility threshold for a sustained stretch. Everything else stays locked: colour #C32D30, tagline, wordmark, cube, footer and plain-black lower left are untouched. Rejected: keep as is (a touching tip inside a busy glass drawing has no separation at 270 px, and a tapering tip has no width where it matters — keeping it preserves a gesture that is only legible close up); thicken the whole wedge only, keeping the tip on (568,758) (a thicker tip that still touches the vertex still merges with the cube; it would read as a larger red stain / red face on the cube, and risks crowding the tagline block and the footer; width without a gap does not create a pointer); lengthen it / overshoot into the cube (fails worst — the tip would sit inside the glass image and read as a crack, reflection or mark on the cube, breaking the cube's illusion); shorten it by a large amount (80–100 px) or move the endpoint to a different vertex (beyond ~40 px the gap reads as a miss — the wedge aims at empty black, not at the cube — and a different vertex would need a new angle and would no longer be the drawn upper-left vertex the composition was built around); delete the wedge (tempting, and the card would still work as wordmark + cube, but the wedge's body is the only warm colour and only diagonal in the lower half, and at thumbnail a red diagonal in plain black is a genuine third distance element; deleting it removes the problem by removing the gesture, rather than fixing the gesture for 24 px; delete only if the falsification test below fails for the shortened version too); change the colour (the failure is geometric — touch + taper + competing diagonals — not chromatic; #C32D30 already has ample contrast on black, and a brighter tip would still merge where it touches the cube). FALSIFICATION (export at 1080×1080, then view at 270 px wide or print 270 mm wide and view from 3 m; show it to a fresh viewer for under 3 seconds, with no prompting, and ask them to point to where the red ends and what it is pointing at): the change FAILS if (a) the viewer says the red touches the cube, or cannot find a black gap between red and cube; (b) the viewer cannot trace the wedge's line to the cube's upper-left vertex, or says it points at empty black / at the wrong part of the cube; (c) the red tip reads as a smear, a red edge of the cube, or a mark on the glass, rather than as a distinct red point separated from the cube; (d) measured in the file, the gap is not 24 px (±4 px) along the centreline, or the wedge is under 24 px wide 24 px back from its tip, or the extended centreline misses (568,758) by more than 4 px. Pass = the viewer locates the gap, follows the line to the vertex, and names the cube as the target, at 270 px, in under 3 seconds. If the shortened version fails (b) in particular — the gap reads as a miss rather than a pause — the next step is not to close the gap again, but to delete the wedge. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 62 — after this change, does the 24 px gap read as a deliberate pause or as a broken / missed wedge at 270 px / 3 m, and does the wedge's angle still unambiguously align with the cube's upper-left vertex when the gap is present? That needs the shortened export itself.

The old wedge element was deleted via the Layers panel and the new file placed full-bleed (0,0) in the old wedge's exact layer position (below the wordmark image, above the cube image). VERIFIED BY MAIN MUSE: v61 sha256 cab0c396 (389,148 bytes); 6,886 px changed vs v58, bbox x0–567, y552–757, ALL within the wedge band — 0 changed px outside it (max sum-abs diff outside the wedge band = 0; the footer is byte-identical between v58 and v61, and the historical '734' footer-left count was a different measurement region — with identical parameters both versions give 505 for x53–200 and 740 for x0–300, so the footer did NOT change). Falsification criterion (d) PASSES on all measurable parts: last strong-red centreline px s=576, (544,750); gap 25.5 px along the centreline (24 ± 4); no red in the gap s579–601; vertex (568,758) pixel (47,46,37) — dark, not red; width at s=553.5 (24 px back from the new tip) = 24 strong-red px (≥24); extrapolated wedge axis at x=568: y=758.0, miss 0.0 px (within ±4). Width at s=565.5 measured 22 by the strict threshold — the geometric width is 24 px there and the source PNG measured 27, so this is an antialiasing/threshold artifact, not a shortfall; the stated criterion is s=553.5 only. At 270 px the change is visible: last red centreline px at s=143 (gap 7.4 px at 270 px), red px in the 30×30 box around the vertex 50 (v58 was 13) — a solid arrowhead ending in a clear black gap, not a fade; the gap reads as a deliberate pause in the thumbnail. CORRECTION TO THE STUDY (verified by main Muse): the gap is DARK, not pure (0,0,0) black — the cube image covers that region and its background there is ~5–30 (max 31), with the cube's glass on the lower side of the gap band (up to (125,123,121) near the vertex); the gap reads as black at 270 px and the red is clearly separated from the cube, so the study's substance holds and 'plain-black' was a simplification. Falsification parts (a),(b),(c) (fresh viewers) NOT RUN — no fresh viewers available, reported honestly to Kind. Build note: the 4×-supersampled PNG has a single (255,0,0) pixel at the tip (s=578) from LANCZOS ringing — 1 px, invisible at 270 px; the export's tip pixel is (199,48,48) at s=577. v61 PNG: ~/workspace/browser_downloads/sess-677548892/browser-download-20261107T022113.581379658Z-7-BLACK_BOX_test_card.png (389,148 bytes). Thumbnail: ~/workspace/canva-assets/v61-thumb-270.png. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 62 — after this change, does the 24 px gap read as a deliberate pause or as a broken / missed wedge at 270 px / 3 m, and does the wedge's angle still unambiguously align with the cube's upper-left vertex when the gap is present?
- Loop 62 study (30+ designs, running total ~1,640): does the 24 px gap read as a deliberate pause or as a broken/missed wedge at poster distance? VERDICT: KEEP THE 24 PX GAP EXACTLY AS IT IS. THE GAP IS NOT THE FAILURE. THE ARROWHEAD IS. At poster distance the gap itself will be seen (6 px at 270 px, ~6.9 arcminutes, well above acuity) — but it will be misread, because the tip that has to explain it disappears: the wedge is 24 px wide at 24 px back from its tip and reaches its point only in the final 12 px; at 270 px that taper is 3 px long, and a 3 px point cannot be read as an arrowhead, so the red shape reads as a blunt bar that stopped short, and a blunt bar stopping 24 px short of a soft photographic vertex reads as a miss, not a pause. The centreline is geometrically exact (19.2°, tip-to-vertex (23,8) is also 19.2°) — but geometry alone does not survive when the pointer's head is illegible and its target is a glass photograph rather than a crisp point. Top-5: (1) a gap of 1.0× the pointer's width is the worst, most ambiguous ratio — gaps that read as deliberate are either ≤0.5× the pointing element's width (a near-touch where Gestalt closure completes the line) or ≥2× the width (a clear interval, plainly a field of negative space); a 1.0× gap is large enough to be seen as separation and small enough to look like a near-miss, a misregistration or a crop; it is also a singleton (nothing else on the card repeats a 24 px diagonal gap, so there is no system for the eye to read it as a rule; the 8 px tagline-to-cube gap is a different size and direction and does not rhyme); (2) the head must be wider — and longer — than the shaft to survive distance; a sharpened shaft does not (every arrow that stayed legible at thumbnail size had a head clearly broader than its shaft; BLACK BOX's head is only the shaft sharpened over 12 px, so at distance it is a bar, and a bar cannot 'pause' — it can only stop); (3) alignment survives distance only when the target is a crisp point — here the target is the upper-left vertex of a glass-cube photograph, a soft, reflective, photographic corner, not a drawn point, and no reference showed a thin pointer aiming across a gap at a soft photographic vertex and succeeding; (4) a gap reads as a pause only when the composition gives it a second witness — repetition, a rhyme, or the gap as the subject; without one, a single gap between the only red element and the only photograph reads as an accident; (5) high contrast makes the problem worse, not better — #C32D30 on black is highly visible, so the gap will be noticed and judged; contrast guarantees the viewer sees the separation, and only a legible arrowhead can tell them the separation was intended. THE CHANGE (loop 62, LAST): WIDEN THE TIP, KEEP THE GAP. Keep the tip at (545,750), keep the 24 px gap, keep the centreline through (568,758), keep the shaft 24 px wide, and change ONLY the arrowhead: arrowhead width 48 px at its base (2.0× shaft width), centred on the existing centreline; arrowhead length 36 px (the taper now runs over the final 36 px, not the final 12 px); base centre at tip minus 36 px along the centreline ≈ (511,738), base half-width 24 px perpendicular to the centreline. Everything else unchanged: wedge still enters at (0,560), still sits above the cube, still ends at (545,750), gap still 24 px. Why: it moves the gap/head ratio from 24/24 = 1.0 to 24/48 = 0.5 — into the near-touch band — without moving the tip or undoing loop 61's shortened wedge; at poster scale the head becomes 12 mm wide and 9 mm long (12 px × 9 px at 270 px), a triangle that can actually be seen as a triangle; the wedge then reads as 'arrowhead held off its target by one half-head-width', the standard stand-off in technical drawing, rather than 'bar that fell short'; it also preserves the reason for the gap (the wedge stays above the cube and never overlaps the glass vertex, so the cube edge stays clean). Rejected: keep as is (the 12 px taper is not legible at 270 px / 3 m — keeping it bets the whole reading on a point the viewer cannot see); widen the gap to 48 px (doubles the extrapolation distance to a soft photographic vertex, weakens the alignment, and makes the wedge look detached and floating); narrow the gap to 8–12 px (reads as a near-miss / misregistration at close viewing and collapses into apparent contact at poster distance); delete the wedge (removes the card's only diagonal, only red and only left-to-right thrust); add a dimension line, ticks, a label or a spec to 'explain' the gap (out of scope — the card's locked character). FALSIFICATION (export at 1080×1080, downscale to 270 px, view at 3 m, plus a copy with a 2 px Gaussian blur to simulate distance acuity; show 5 seconds each to three people who have not seen earlier loops, ask 'What is the red shape doing? What is it pointing at?'): PASS = at least 2 of 3 say 'an arrow pointing at the top-left corner of the cube' and describe the tip as a point/arrowhead, not a bar or stripe. FAIL = at least 2 of 3 say it 'missed', 'stopped short', 'is broken / cut off', or cannot name the cube corner; OR they call the red shape a bar/stripe; OR, objectively, in the 270 px image the head is not distinguishable as a triangle from a blunt end. If it fails, the head is still too small — do not then move the tip or widen the gap; first try a still broader head (60 px wide × 42 px long, same tip and gap) and re-test; if that also fails, the gap-to-soft-vertex construction itself is at fault and the wedge should be re-thought, not patched. This is the LAST loop (62 of 62).

- Kind's rating, v62 (2026-11-07 ~12:15): 4/10. He ordered 40 MORE loops (63–102, 102 total). Never argue with his rating; the loops are the answer. His eye is the only score. Previous ratings: v1 'not even 1/10', v6 2/10, v12 3/10, v22 3/10, v62 4/10.
- Loop 63 study (35 designs, running total ~1,675): whole-card question — what is the single biggest weakness at poster/thumbnail distance, and what ONE change would most improve it? VERDICT: THE GLASS CUBE HERO IS THE WEAKEST ELEMENT. At 270 px it has no hard, light silhouette against the black field, so it collapses to a faint grey smudge in the lower right and the card has only one read — the wordmark — floating in black. The one change is to harden the cube's silhouette inside its existing box, not to re-litigate the wedge, tagline or footer. Top-5: (1) only mass and contrast survive at thumbnail size; detail, microcopy, gaps and arrowheads do not — the tagline/spine/footer vanishing at 270 px is normal and is not the weakness; the weakness is that once they vanish, only the wordmark is left, with no second mass to carry the card; (2) a hero must have a hard silhouette or a colour field against its background — a transparent or low-contrast hero vanishes (the glass cube is the opposite case in the whole sample: transparent, on black, with its drawn start inset 294×178 px inside its own image box, so at 270 px it cannot be told apart from the black field around it; it occupies 746×420 px nominally, yet contributes almost no visible mass); (3) one dominant mass must anchor to an edge, fill the frame or bleed — floating fragments in a void read as unfinished (neither mass anchors: the wordmark touches no edge, the cube stops 60 px short of the right edge and 20 px short of the footer, and the top 34.6% is empty; the void reads as 'nothing has been placed yet', not as intention — consistent with the owner's 4/10: at first glance the card looks like a black slide with a title, not a poster); (4) hierarchy needs a middle weight — the card jumps from 128 pt to 13 pt to 12 pt (~10:1) with the cube as the only middle element, and when the cube fails at distance the hierarchy collapses to one level (white type on black); (5) colour and direction must be a mass, not a line — and a line that points at an invisible target tells no story (the wedge's red does register as a slash at 270 px, but its narrative does not survive: a 12×9 px arrowhead, 6 px from a cube that itself cannot be seen, pointing at a vertex that has no visible shape around it; the wedge's failure at distance is therefore a consequence of the cube's failure, not its cause — loops 61–62 settled its gap and arrowhead at full size, and at thumbnail size the gap (6 px versus 0 px) is below the threshold that could matter, so the arrowhead could be twice as large and still point at nothing visible). THE CHANGE (loop 63): harden the cube's silhouette inside its existing box — (a) fill the cube's top face solid #F5F5F5 at 100% opacity (no transparency, no glass on that face); (b) stroke the cube's entire outer drawn silhouette with a solid white #FFFFFF line, 10 px wide, centred on the drawn edges, 100% opacity; (c) leave the remaining faces glass as now, and do not move, scale or re-crop the cube image. Why: 10 px at full size is 2.5 px at 270 px — the minimum for a line to survive shrinking (a 1–2 px glass edge is 0.25–0.5 px at thumbnail size, i.e. invisible); a solid top face gives a filled mass, not just a wireframe (an outline alone would still read as a diagram at distance, whereas a filled light polygon reads as a shape). Together they make the cube a distinct geometric mass in the lower right at 270 px. If the cube reads, the wedge suddenly has a target, the top void becomes deliberate negative space, and the card goes from 'white label on black' to 'white label + a box + a red arrow' — a poster with an idea — in one operation. Rejected: enlarge the wordmark (already survives; makes a bigger label, not a second read); close the wedge gap / enlarge the arrowhead / move the tip to the vertex (locked by loops 61–62, and at thumbnail size the difference is imperceptible — and it would still point at an invisible cube; re-litigation, not a whole-card fix); scale or move the cube larger (its box is already near its limits — top capped by the locked tagline, bottom 20 px above the locked footer, right 60 px from the edge, left capped by the locked plain-black quadrant; under 10% headroom, and scaling glass-on-black still leaves glass-on-black — a bigger smudge, not a shape); enlarge the tagline, spine or footer (locked; even doubling 13 pt to 26 pt is 6.5 pt at thumbnail size, still illegible); fill the lower-left quadrant (locked plain black; adds a third mass that competes with the cube); add a dimension line, ticks, a label or a spec (none anywhere on the card, and at 270 px any such line would be illegible clutter); put a solid red or white panel behind the cube (introduces a new rectangular block that competes with the wordmark and changes the black field; red would fight the wedge's red); thicken or recolour the wedge (already the highest-contrast colour accent; a thicker red line with no visible target would read as a larger stray mark). FALSIFICATION (export at 1080×1080, shrink to 270 px, show at 3 m to five people who have not seen the full-size version; ask 'What is the second thing you see after BLACK BOX, and where is it?'): PASS = at least 4 of 5 name 'a cube / a box' and point to the lower right; also a masked test (hide the wordmark; the cube alone at 270 px must still read as a distinct geometric shape, not as black). FAIL if (i) 2 or fewer of 5 name the cube; (ii) the cube is named first, ahead of the wordmark (the stroke/solid face now dominates the hierarchy); (iii) at full size the solid top face or white stroke makes the cube look like a paper diagram or a wireframe and destroys the glass idea; or (iv) the stroke breaks or fades where the wedge crosses it, so the cube reads as two fragments. Any of those outcomes falsifies this change and the next lever to test would be the wordmark's anchoring, not the wedge gap. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study's falsification): if this change fails, the next lever is the wordmark's anchoring.

- Loop 63 build notes (v63, 2026-11-07): harden the cube's silhouette — solid top face + white stroke. CHANGE: the cube image was replaced with a new file built by main Muse, `~/workspace/canva-assets/glass-cube-black-v63.png` — the same glass cube, but (a) its top face is filled solid #F5F5F5 at 100% opacity, and (b) its entire outer drawn silhouette is stroked solid white #FFFFFF, 10 px wide in card coords (40 px in image coords, centred on the drawn edges). The inner cube's vertices were measured by main Muse from the cube image: the cube is a perspective glass render, NOT an isometric drawing — the convex-hull approach failed (48–58 noisy hull vertices, interior angles ~170°, because the glass edges are soft) and colour segmentation failed (chromatic aberration pollutes every face). Vertices were identified visually from a coordinate-grid overlay and verified by drawing the overlay on the image: UL=(1184,715) [the known wedge target], T=(1600,360), UR=(2050,480), LR=(2100,1100), B=(1500,1300), LL=(1100,1150), centre C=(1700,750). At 270 px the cube now reads as a distinct geometric mass: white hexagon outline + light top face, clearly a box in the lower right; cube-zone mean brightness 36.2 (v62: 31.3), bright px 2552 (v62: 2010); the masked test (wordmark hidden) also reads as a distinct shape. - Loop 64 study (22 designs, running total ~1,697): the wordmark's anchoring — now that the cube reads, what is the single biggest remaining weakness at poster distance? VERDICT: THE UNCLAIMED TOP. The wordmark is already almost edge-to-edge horizontally (ink X 17–1073, 17 px margins), but 374 px / 34.6% of pure black sits above it with nothing tying it to the top edge, while the hardened cube anchors the lower half. At 270 px / 3 m the top void is ~94 px tall — three times the wordmark's ~31 px height — so the first read is a black rectangle with a white band in the middle, not a top-anchored title plus a bottom-right box. The tagline/spine/footer are invisible at that distance and cannot bridge it. Top-5: (1) huge type normally touches or bleeds an edge — it does not float with a third of the field empty above it ('WEED' / 'TALL' bleed, 'GET BETTER EVERY DAY' fills full height, 'LIFE MOVES PRETTY FAST' is top-anchored); (2) floating type works only when it is the ONLY mass ('LESS IS MORE', 'EMPI' float centrally but there is no hero object below them; BLACK BOX breaks this by adding the cube below a floating wordmark); (3) two-mass posters anchor the masses to opposite edges (red type top-left + photo below; blackletter across the top + photo at the right edge; headline top + car middle + prices bottom, all on a grid); (4) tiny top microcopy alone does not anchor a wordmark ('N° 01' / 'EST. 2026' sit in opposite top corners with 300+ px of black between them and the wordmark, so they float separately too); (5) a single geometric hero anchors itself by filling its zone (Sonnet Posters' circle/arcs fill and touch their field; loop 63 did this for the cube, which now exposes the wordmark as the unanchored mass). THE CHANGE (as specified by the study): top-anchor the wordmark — move it up 256 px (top Y 374 → 118), enlarge 128 → 168 pt equivalent (+31%, ink height 124 → ~163 px, new ink Y 118–281), move the tagline up 235 px (Y 537–577 → 302–342, 21 px below the wordmark); cube, wedge, spine, microcopy, footer unchanged. Top void shrinks 34.6% → 10.9% (118 px); the 238 px black between tagline bottom (342) and cube top (580) becomes an intentional separator. Rejected: bleed the wordmark off the top (crops caps, collides with microcopy); grow it in place (cannot grow wider, already 1,056/1,080 px); split 'BLACK' / 'BOX' onto two lines (halves the read size, changes the identity); move the cube up (creates one central cluster, throws away the bottom-right anchor); move the wordmark down onto the cube (crowds it, destroys hierarchy); add something to the top void (noise, a third element); fix the tagline/spine/footer instead (invisible at 270 px, a loop-65 question). FALSIFICATION (270 px / 3 m, 5-second test): a viewer names 'BLACK BOX' in under 2 seconds and describes 'white title at the top, white box at the bottom right'; wordmark still read first; cube still distinct. Measurable: wordmark top Y ≤ 130 px; ink height ≥ 150 px; pure-black above the wordmark ≤ 12%; microcopy-to-wordmark clearance ≥ 40 px; tagline-to-cube gap ≥ 200 px; no overlap. FAIL if the top void is still >15% at thumbnail, the wordmark is cropped or collides with the microcopy, or the cube is read first. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 65 — the tagline (at 13 pt it is invisible at 270 px, so enlarge it, move it under the wordmark, or delete it) and, secondarily, whether the cube should bleed to the bottom/right edge to match the wordmark's new top anchor. CORRECTION BY MAIN MUSE (verified from the v63 export's pixels BEFORE building — the study as specified is NOT buildable in two places, and the build below is the corrected version): (1) THE SPINE COLLIDES. The spine's grey #989CA1 ink is a vertical column at X 23–34, Y 43–355 (measured: non-black px at Y 150, 200, 300 all fall in X 23–34). The wordmark's 'B' spans X 17–129. Moving the wordmark to Y 118–281 puts the 'B' directly over the spine's letters for Y 118–281 (more than half the spine's length) — the white 'B' would cover the grey spine (the wordmark image is the topmost layer) or the grey would show on white, either way a visible defect. The study listed the spine as 'unchanged' and never checked it. The spine cannot stay, cannot be shortened to fit the 65 px gap above the new wordmark (it is 318 px long), cannot move to the right edge (the 'X' covers X 954–1073 there) and cannot move below the tagline (the Y 342–580 gap is 238 px, shorter than the spine). So the corrected build DELETES the spine: its job was to fill the empty top third (loops 6/19), and the wordmark now fills the top itself. (2) THE 168 PT ENLARGEMENT IS GEOMETRICALLY IMPOSSIBLE without bleeding. The wordmark is already 1,056 px wide (X 17–1073) at 128 pt; a 31% size increase makes it 1,386 px wide, exceeding the 1,080 px canvas, and the study's own falsification forbids cropping the wordmark. It cannot grow at all while staying within the canvas (max ~2%). So the corrected build does NOT enlarge the wordmark: it stays 128 pt equivalent (1,056 × 124 px) and moves up 256 px to Y 118–242. The tagline therefore moves up 256 px too (Y 537–577 → 281–321), preserving v63's 39 px wordmark-to-tagline gap (not the study's 21 px, which assumed the enlarged wordmark). The falsification's 'ink height ≥ 150 px' criterion is voided by the same geometry (it stays 124 px); the operative criteria are the top void ≤ 12% (118 px = 10.9%), microcopy clearance ≥ 40 px (65 px), tagline-to-cube gap ≥ 200 px (259 px) and no overlap. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (loop 65, as named by the study): the tagline, and secondarily whether the cube should bleed to the bottom/right edge.

- Loop 65 study (29 designs, running total ~1,726): the tagline — at 13 pt it is invisible at 270 px, yet it is the only place stating what BLACK BOX is about. VERDICT: ENLARGE AND RE-ANCHOR THE TAGLINE; DO NOT BLEED THE CUBE. At 13 pt the tagline is a grey smudge at 270 px / 3 m, but 'BLACK BOX' alone is ambiguous without 'THE SYSTEMS YOU DEPEND ON. / THE FAILURES YOU NEVER SEE.' A whisper is only justified when the headline already carries the meaning; here it does not. Top-5: (1) a meaningful second line is set at headline scale, not one-tenth of it (ReadyMag's second clause is the same size as the first; Incite's 'Insights thrive in unlikely company' IS the large bold headline; Apex's 'PROFESSIONAL CAR DETAILING' matches 'APEX AUTO DETAILING' in size; BLACK BOX at 13/128 pt = 10% is far outside this pattern, 32/128 pt = 25% puts it inside it, while ExtraBold vs Regular still preserves hierarchy); (2) tiny text is a deliberate close-range whisper only when the headline already says it all ('DREAM BIGGER' is the whole message, so its tiny line can whisper; 'Sacred with Design' is the whole message; the Paul Rand tiny line is a quote, not the premise; BLACK BOX fails this test); (3) when a sentence is the message, its lines are stacked tight and left-aligned with the headline at the same or near-same size ('LOOK / THROUGH / THE HOLE / IN THE WALL' all the same bold scale, tight, left-aligned; 'BIG / BANG' second line ~60% of the first, tight, left-aligned) — the model for the 20 px gap and X 17 alignment, replacing the floating 39 px gap and X 282 alignment (which aligns with nothing; the cube's left edge X 274 is not a text alignment); (4) deletion works only when one word or image is enough ('Ukraine' alone, 'TWIST' alone, headline-only posters) — the honest second-best for BLACK BOX, kept as the fallback if 32 pt fails, because it would leave the premise unstated; (5) a complete hero object holds a margin, a cropped hero bleeds (Ukraine white card, Poster Generator, Sacred hold their hero inside a margin; 'One step at a time', TWIST, Poster Co crop theirs) — the BLACK BOX cube is a complete, stroked silhouette, so its 60 px right margin and 20 px above the footer read as intentional, not a mistake; do NOT bleed it (bleeding to the bottom would put it behind the footer at Y 1020; bleeding to the right would crop the 10 px silhouette stroke). THE CHANGE (loop 65): enlarge and re-anchor the tagline, keeping its wording, two lines, Montserrat Regular and white — size 13 → 32 pt (exactly one quarter of the 128 pt wordmark); left edge X 282 → X 17 (aligns with the wordmark's left edge); top Y 281 → Y 262 (gap below the wordmark 39 → 20 px, one tight lock-up); letter spacing 150 → 50 (at 32 pt the old 150 tracking would be loose and push the lines wider than needed). Expected block ~X 17–874, Y 262–360 (width estimate 348 × 32/13 ≈ 857 px before the tracking reduction; height ~98 px for two lines). Nothing else moves. Collision check (verified by the study, and confirmed by main Muse): wordmark ends Y 242, tagline top Y 262 (20 px gap); cube starts Y 580, tagline bottom ~360 (220 px gap); wedge starts Y 560, tagline bottom ~200 px above it; microcopy Y 40 and footer Y 1020 far away. Rejected: keep 13 pt as a whisper (adds a smudge, no information); delete the tagline (works visually but removes the premise — the fallback if 32 pt fails, NOT a further enlargement); one line at a readable size (56 characters at 32 pt would be ~1,700 px wide; at a size that fits one line, ~17 pt, it would be a smudge again); enlarge only to 20–24 pt and keep X 282 (a half-measure — cap height at 270 px still ~5–6 px, ratio 19%, left edge still floating; at 32 pt from X 282 the right edge would be ~X 1,139, off the canvas, hence the move to X 17 is forced); centre the tagline (the wordmark is not centred); move the tagline down or beside the cube (detaches it, collides with the wedge and cube top); bleed the cube (collides with the footer, crops the silhouette); make the tagline bold, red or a different face (competes with the ExtraBold wordmark and the red wedge). FALSIFICATION (270 px / 3 m, decisive): a fresh viewer reads both lines aloud correctly, in order, without guessing, and can say in their own words that it is about systems and failures. FAIL if either line is a smudge, words are guessed, or only 'BLACK BOX' is read. If it fails at 32 pt, DELETE the tagline — do not go to 36–40 pt. Measurable: font size field 32 pt; left edge X = 17; top Y = 262; letter spacing = 50; bounding box ~X 17–874, Y 262–360, no overlap with the wordmark (ends Y 242), cube (starts Y 580) or wedge (starts Y 560); if the measured right edge exceeds ~X 900, reduce letter spacing further — do not reduce the size below 32 pt and do not move the block right. Hierarchy test (5 seconds, full size): viewer names 'BLACK BOX' first and the tagline second; if the tagline is read first, the size is too strong. Full-size legibility alone is NOT a pass — the tagline was already legible at full size at 13 pt; the loop 65 pass requires the 270 px / 3 m test. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 66 — after the tagline is enlarged, the gap from its bottom (~Y 360) to the cube top (Y 580) is still ~220 px of black and the lower-left quadrant is still plain black. Is that void now the main weakness — should the cube be enlarged and/or moved up to close it, and by how much, without colliding with the wedge or the footer — or is the black field the point, and should loop 66 leave it alone? NOTE (main Muse): the study worker stated it was not given the list of earlier-loop designs, so it selected fresh results from current searches — some named designs overlap with earlier loops ('50 Swiss Style Posters', 'Sacred with Design', 'War in Ukraine', 'Exhibition Rotenturmstraße', 'Paul Rand', 'UNIWAY', 'Diane Arbus', 'Dream Bigger', 'LESS IS MORE', 'GET BETTER EVERY DAY', 'LIFE MOVES PRETTY FAST', 'DESIGN IS THINKING MADE VISUAL'), but the set also contains genuinely new designs ('Treatise on typography', 'Incite', 'Poster Generator', 'Lokal Poster Co.', 'Skullpture', 'The Blackpepper Studio', 'Poster Expo vol. I', 'One step at a time', 'Sidecar Studio', 'THE ONE YOU ARE LOOKING FOR IS → YOU', 'NATURE') and the total (29) is above the ≥17 minimum, so the verdict stands; the overlap is recorded here so loop 66 can check against it.

Wording, font (Montserrat Regular), colour (white) and left alignment unchanged; the box (972.6 px wide) did not constrain the text, so it stays on exactly two lines with the same break after 'ON.' — no widening, no reflow. - Loop 66 study (33 designs, running total ~1,759; none from loops 61–65 — the worker was given the banned list this time and honoured it): the ~220 px void between the tagline and the cube IS the main weakness, but DO NOT MOVE OR ENLARGE THE CUBE — RE-AIM THE RED WEDGE. The void is an unbridged, asymmetric L-shaped void (~220 px band between the tagline ink Y 352 and the cube top Y 580, joined to the empty lower-left quadrant X 0–274, Y 580–1000); at 270 px it reads as two separate posters stacked — a heavy top block and a bottom-right hero — with nothing joining them. The black field is not yet deliberate negative space, because in the work studied deliberate voids are symmetric, spanned by a full-width element, or crossed by a diagonal; BLACK BOX is none of those (asymmetric void, cube starts at X 274 so it does not span the width, and the red wedge only enters at (0,560), at the very bottom of the gap). Top-5: (1) intentional voids are symmetric or centred (Student Cultural Centre centres logo + name in a vast black field with black above = black below; Arc'teryx Veilance centres a full-width photo strip; Trivunic's 'FOCUS' and 'Perspective' centre the hero so the black around it is balanced) — BLACK BOX's top block is heavy and left-anchored, its hero bottom-right, so the middle + lower-left combine into an unbalanced L; (2) diagonals bridge a gap, heroes do not need to grow to do it (Bilochornia cuts a diagonal white strip across huge grey type joining top to bottom; Poster of Positivity runs diagonal stripes the full height with the quote on the diagonal and a portrait bottom-right; Black Sedan puts a text block top-left, a huge grey diagonal wedge middle-right, and the car at the bottom — the diagonal, not a bigger car, joins the two halves and fills the opposite corner — the direct model for re-aiming BLACK BOX's wedge); (3) when the title is huge, the hero touches or overlaps it — there is no empty band (Ferrari F40: 'FERRARI F40' top, giant 'F40' letters middle, car bottom, everything touches or overlaps; GitHub 'Commit' and 'ISSUES': type at the bottom, a black graphic filling the rest, a white circle bridging centre; Negative Space Marks 15 Years: title fills the top half, a grid of marks fills the bottom half, no band between; Rolls-Royce Wraith Black Arrow: a continuous vertical stack, title top, cityscape middle, car lower-middle, specifications bottom); (4) a large mid-gap works only when the lower element spans the full width ('Elements Unveiled': small top text, a large black pause, then a strip of five small photographs spanning the full width near the bottom third — the full-width strip frames the gap and makes it a pause; BLACK BOX's cube starts at X 274 and ends at X 1020, so it cannot frame the gap — the gap is open on the left); (5) an empty lower-left opposite a bottom-right hero is left alone only when a single diagonal or mark already crosses it, or the hero is centred (Black Sedan crosses it with a diagonal; Trivunic's 'Perspective' centres a cube so no corner is orphaned; Student Cultural Centre centres its mark) — no studied poster solved this by adding a second competing object in the opposite corner; the wedge is already BLACK BOX's lower-left content, it simply starts too low to do that job. THE CHANGE (loop 66): re-aim the red wedge, leave the cube exactly where it is. Move only the wedge's entry point from (0,560) to (0,430), keeping its tip, gap and target unchanged, and rotate its arrowhead to the new angle. Cube unchanged (X 274–1020, Y 580–1000); footer unchanged (Y 1020); tagline, wordmark and microcopy unchanged. Wedge tip unchanged (545,750); target vertex unchanged (568,758); gap unchanged (24 px); arrowhead unchanged in size (48×36 px). Wedge angle 19.2° → 30.4° (+11.2°, arctan(320/545)); arrowhead rotates by the same +11.2°. Wedge length 577 → 632 px (+55 px). Why (0,430): it is 78 px below the tagline ink (Y 352), so it cannot touch the tagline; at X 17 it is at Y 440, 88 px below the tagline; it crosses the centre of the gap (Y ~466) at X ~61, and it crosses Y 580 at X ~256, just before the cube's left edge — so it occupies the gap for its first ~256 px and fills the upper part of the lower-left quadrant, using only the existing vocabulary (one red diagonal, no new element, colour or type). Collision check: the wedge ends 24 px short of the cube's upper-left vertex, as now, so it does not touch the cube; it ends at Y 750, far above the footer; it is 78–88 px below the tagline; no element overlaps. Rejected: enlarge the cube ~15% anchored bottom-right (to 858×483 px, top Y 517, left X 162, gap still 165 px — still a void — while the left edge would invade the lower-left, the new vertex would move to ~(500,722), the wedge tip to ~(478,714), its angle would flatten to ~17.9°, and the wedge would shorten — high rebuild cost, low gain; enlarging ~26% to close the gap to ~120 px (938×528 px, top Y 472, left X 82, vertex ~(452,696)) would make the cube dominate and almost touch the left edge — worse); move the cube up 100 px without enlarging (to Y 480–900: closes the top gap to 128 px but creates a new 120 px black band above the footer, breaking the cube's footer anchor, and flattens the wedge to ~9.4° (tip (545,650), dy 90 over dx 545), losing its diagonal thrust); fill the lower-left with text, a small mark, a second cube or a grid (creates a competing hero at 270 px and splits attention — the studied work that fills a corner does so as part of a grid or a centred system, not as a lone orphan opposite a large hero); leave it alone as deliberate negative space (fails all three tests — not symmetric, not spanned, not crossed — and at 270 px the 220 px band is ~55 px on screen, about 20% of the height, almost as tall as the whole top block (234 px); 'Elements Unveiled' is the closest counter-example and differs in exactly the ways named in observation 4). FALSIFICATION (measurable in the file): cube X 274–1020, Y 580–1000 unchanged; footer Y 1020 unchanged; wedge tip (545,750) ±2 px, gap 24 px, target vertex (568,758) unchanged; wedge entry Y 560 → 430 (±10 px); wedge angle 19.2° → 30.4° (±1°); arrowhead 48×36 px, rotated +11.2°; tagline-to-wedge clearance at the left edge ≥70 px. At 270 px / 3 m: in a 3-second glance the wordmark, wedge and cube must read as one diagonal chain, not as two stacked posters. Blind test with 5 viewers, loop 65 vs loop 66 side by side: pass if at least 4/5 say 'one poster' for loop 66 and can name the red diagonal as what connects top to bottom, and if no viewer says the wedge touches the tagline or the cube. Fail / revert if the 30° wedge looks too steep or aggressive at thumbnail scale, if the arrowhead looks mis-aimed, or if the red diagonal is lost at 3 m — in that case try entry Y 460 (angle ~27.5°) before considering any cube move. If the wedge test passes, do not then also enlarge the cube in the same loop; that would confound the test. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 67 — now that the wedge bridges the gap at 30°, does it pull the eye out to the left at its new entry (0,430) instead of landing on the cube — i.e. is the wedge now too dominant, and does the cube's glass reading (solid #F5F5F5 top face vs white 10 px stroked silhouette) still hold as the hero at 270 px, or does the steeper red diagonal compete with the cube's upper-left vertex?
