Wedge entry at the left edge Y 429–438 (centre 433.5, was Y 560–567); centreline samples match entry (0,430)→tip (545,750) within 0.5 px at X 50/100/200/300/400/500 (angle 30.4°); last strong-red on the centreline s=631 at (544,749), 1 px from the tip (545,750); tip→vertex distance 24.35 px (gap 24 px). NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the loop 66 study): Loop 67 — now that the wedge bridges the gap at 30°, does it pull the eye out to the left at its new entry (0,430) instead of landing on the cube — i.e. is the wedge now too dominant, and does the cube's glass reading (solid #F5F5F5 top face vs white 10 px stroked silhouette) still hold as the hero at 270 px, or does the steeper red diagonal compete with the cube's upper-left vertex?

- Loop 67 study (21 designs, running total ~1,780; none from loops 61–66): the wedge does NOT dominate, and its left-edge entry does NOT pull the eye out. The cube still holds as the hero. The single biggest remaining weakness is the 24 px NEAR-MISS AT THE LANDING: the wedge tip at (545,750) stops 24 px short of the cube's upper-left vertex (568,758), so at 270 px the red arrowhead sits in black void, 6 px from the cube, and the arrowhead and the vertex read as two competing termini instead of one landing. Top-5: (1) a diagonal is a pointer only when it lands in/on a solid target; a gap makes it a near-miss (Lissitzky's red wedge pierces the white circle, it does not stop short; Svetlana B's red shard lands on the large white 'P'; Vlad Grama's arrowhead touches the outlined circle; aninndesign's diagonal rays converge to a single central point — BLACK BOX fails this test by 24 px); (2) a left-edge entry does not pull the eye out; an unterminated diagonal does (Lissitzky and Thomas de Franzoni's light shaft entering top-left and landing in mist both read as entering; by contrast Aya C's thin white blade enters top-left, crosses the black field and exits bottom-right with no hero to stop it — the eye follows it out; the BLACK BOX wedge points down-right, into the frame, and its only exit risk is the gap at its tip, not its tail); (3) a saturated diagonal becomes the hero only when it is a field, not a shaft (SUETA TEAM's huge solid red vertical block is the hero; Gamid Rustamov's 45° black diagonal band is the structure; Matthieu Martigny's repeated diagonals become texture and the central dark circle still wins — the BLACK BOX wedge is one narrow shaft against a 746×420 px cube, so it is still subordinate); (4) on black, a hero holds by solid light mass, not by outline; true glass needs colour/fringe or highlights to survive at thumbnail size (INDUSTRIA's solid sphere reads as volume, a ring reads as a void; Vitaly Perfilev's one small solid tape roll is the hero because it is the only volumetric solid; Ion Lucin's glass type reads through chromatic fringing; Paul Rover's small glass cube reads through dispersion colour) — the BLACK BOX cube's #F5F5F5 top face is therefore the hero-maker; its white stroke alone would not be. As glass specifically the cube does NOT hold at 270 px: the 10 px stroke is 2.5 px at 270 px and merges with the #F5F5F5 face, so the cube reads as a solid light block, not glass — a real loss, but secondary to the landing, and NOT fixed in this loop (changing the top face or stroke as well would be a second change and would risk the hero mass that currently wins); (5) steepening helps when the diagonal rhymes with the hero's own angle (DEMOCRÀCIA's tilted white screen's diagonal edge is the hero's own edge, so its angle cannot compete with it; Anthony Gribben's dozens of competing diagonals cancel each other and there is no single hero — at 30.4° the BLACK BOX wedge is near-isometric and rhymes with the cube; the competition is at the vertex (tip vs. corner), not in the angle). THE CHANGE (loop 67): close the gap, nothing else. Extend the wedge tip, on its existing line, from (545,750) to exactly (568,758), the cube's upper-left vertex. Keep the entry fixed at (0,430), keep the shaft width and the 48×36 px arrowhead unchanged, with the arrowhead's point at the new tip. Length 632 → ~656 px (+24 px, ~4%); angle 30.4° → ~30.0° (atan 328/568); entry unchanged. Do not extend past the vertex into the cube. The tip touches only the vertex; it does not enter the cube interior, does not touch the tagline (ink ends Y 352), the wordmark, the footer (Y 1020), or the cube's top face. Moving the tip 23 px right and 8 px down along its own line creates no overlap with any other element. Why this, and not a bigger fix: a diagonal reads as a pointer only when it terminates in/on a solid target; a gap, however small, turns a pointer into a near-miss. Closing it transfers the wedge's energy into the cube and makes the cube the single terminus, without making the wedge longer, steeper, wider or redder in any material way. Rejected: flatten/lower the wedge (back towards 19.2°, entry Y ~500 — would reopen the tagline-to-cube gap that loop 66 closed and break the chain, to fix a dominance problem the studies do not support); start the wedge inside the frame (e.g. X ~150 — would turn an off-canvas entry into a floating arrow; Lissitzky and the Thmanyah arrow show a cropped entry is a strength, not an exit); narrow or desaturate the wedge (red is the only accent; at 270 px a thinner/paler wedge would lose pointer legibility without changing the landing problem); enlarge the cube or strengthen its top face/stroke (the cube is already 746 px wide, bottom Y 1000, footer Y 1020 — enlarging it would collide with the footer; and a second change would confound the test of the landing — this is the loop 68 question); move the cube left/up to meet the tip (would break the cube's bottom alignment Y 1000 and its right margin X 1020, and risk the tagline); leave it alone (rejected, narrowly — the card is close, but the 24 px gap is exactly the difference the studies draw between 'pointer' and 'near-miss', and at 270 px the 6 px black gap is visible as a hesitation). FALSIFICATION: (1) file check — tip = (568,758), length 655–657 px, angle 29.9–30.1°, entry still (0,430), arrowhead still 48×36 px; no red pixel crosses the cube's top-left edge into the cube interior. (2) At 270 px: no black gap is visible between red and white at the vertex; the wedge and cube read as one touching chain. (3) 5-second / 3 m test with fresh viewers: they name the cube as the hero, trace wordmark → tagline → red diagonal → cube without pausing at the tip, and do not report the eye leaving to the left. Pass = at least 4 of 5 do this, and none names the wedge as the hero. (4) Fail conditions: if viewers still pause at the tip, still call the wedge the hero, or still report an exit to the left, the change has failed — do not then lengthen, widen or redden the wedge; revert the tip and reconsider the cube's top-face reading instead. (5) The glass question is explicitly NOT passed by this change: if viewers call the cube 'a white block' rather than 'glass', that is expected and is loop 68's work, not a failure of loop 67. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 68 — at 270 px the cube now reads as a solid light block, not glass. Is the solid #F5F5F5 top face + 10 px white stroke enough to signal glass, or does the cube need one internal refraction/diagonal edge (and would that edge, if set at the wedge's ~30°, rhyme with the wedge or read as a second diagonal inside the hero)?

Is the solid #F5F5F5 top face + 10 px white stroke enough to signal glass, or does the cube need one internal refraction/diagonal edge (and would that edge, if set at the wedge's ~30°, rhyme with the wedge or read as a second diagonal inside the hero)?

- Loop 68 study (49 designs, running total ~1,829; none from loops 61–67 — banned items that surfaced in search were excluded and not counted): at 270 px the cube has exactly ONE visual zone inside its silhouette — a uniform pale slab. The solid #F5F5F5 top face + 10 px white stroke (2.5 px at 270 px) is NOT enough to signal glass. In every glass object studied that still read as glass at thumbnail scale, the signal was never a pale solid face with a white outline — it was always at least one of: faces at sharply different values, background or type visible through the object, a saturated colour or rainbow rim on the object's own perspective edges, or a hole/empty interior. The BLACK BOX cube has none of these at 270 px: its side faces (pale yellow front-left, blue right) collapse to a faint smudge, nothing shows through it (the render is RGB opaque), and the white stroke merges with the top face. So the cube reads as a solid white block — and because the poster's concept is transparency/fragility ('the failures you never see'), that is a concept failure at the distance where the poster is first seen, not just a detail loss. Top-5: (1) glass reads at thumbnail by facet-value contrast, not by a pale fill ('This is Cube' — one face near-black, one bright white-yellow, one deep blue, with thick red/green/blue dispersion bands along its own internal edges; at grid scale it still reads as glass because there are three distinct value zones and coloured seams, even though its bright face is as large as BLACK BOX's top face; BLACK BOX has one zone); (2) glass reads by seeing through it (Čepanko's transparent glass cube over huge 'WEB GRAPHICS' type — the faces are mostly empty, the black type is visible through the cube, only the rainbow edges and a small blue core are solid; at thumbnail it reads as glass precisely because it is NOT filled; BLACK BOX's opaque #F5F5F5 top face is the opposite construction); (3) glass reads by a saturated rim/edge, not a white outline (Askarkhanova's blue glass torus/disc over white 'VISUAL' type — the type bends at the glass edge and the disc carries a bright saturated blue rim; Samolevsky's 162 chromatic glass shapes read as glass at thumbnail through thin bright rims and rainbow interiors, with black showing through holes — a white stroke on a white face can never do this, because there is no value step); (4) a pale glass disc only survives at small size if it is coloured and overlaps something (jona.'s 'Index of Refraction' — pale BLUE discs overlapping the type, distorting the white type behind them, each with a hard bright rim; Cuong Nguyen's glass lens over 'october.2026' with chromatic fringing at its rim, the bent text inside the lens is the glass cue — BLACK BOX's cube overlaps nothing and distorts nothing); (5) a solid light block CAN be a successful hero — but only when solidity is the point ('ABSOLUTELY FREE' and 'Lucifer Sans' work as black posters with solid light masses because nothing in them claims to be transparent; conversely Farid Ghanbari's 'Glass cubes' is the cautionary case — its mostly-dark glass cubes are nearly invisible in the search grid on black, so darkening the BLACK BOX top face to chase 'glass' would risk the same disappearance; hence the verdict keeps the #F5F5F5 top face and adds the coloured seam instead of darkening anything). THE CHANGE (loop 68, option (b) in a specific form — NOT option (a)): in the image file only (glass-cube-black-v63.png, 3000×1688), replace it in place. Do NOT add a diagonal across the top face. Instead, amplify the chromatic dispersion the render already has along the cube's own existing facet edge — the longest internal perspective edge that divides the front face from the side face — into one thick, saturated, three-stripe refraction band. Specification: band 56–64 px wide in the 3000 px file (= 14–16 px on the 1080 poster = 3.5–4 px at 270 px; the current 10 px stroke fails precisely because it is only 2.5 px at 270 px, so the band must clear 3 px at thumbnail). Stripes, in order across the band: saturated red #C32D30 (the wedge red, already in the palette) — white — saturated blue (deepened from the existing right-face blue). Measurable in the file: each coloured stripe at least 16 px wide in the file, red stripe R−B > 60, blue stripe B−R > 60 (saturated colour, not the current pale smudge). The top face stays solid #F5F5F5 and the outer silhouette, stroke, position (274,580), size (746×420) and layer stay exactly as they are — the change is additive, so the poster's light hierarchy (white wordmark, white cube) is untouched. Collision check: the band lies strictly inside the cube silhouette (X 274–1020, Y 580–1000); it must start at least 24 px (poster scale) clear of the wedge tip at (568,758) so it never touches or merges with the wedge landing, and end at least 20 px above the cube's bottom edge (Y 1000), so it cannot reach the footer at Y 1020; it is nowhere near the tagline (ink ends Y 352). No collision with any other element. The wedge landing is unchanged. WHY NOT A 30° FACE DIAGONAL (option (a) as posed): a diagonal drawn across the top face at ~30° would be parallel to the wedge (30.0°) and would read as a second wedge, a slash or a crack — not as refraction. In all the studied cubes, refraction lines follow the object's own perspective edges and meet at its vertices; none runs parallel to an external diagonal across a face. At 270 px a thin face diagonal would vanish, and a thick one would only split the top face into two pale triangles — still a solid block. The facet-edge band works for the opposite reason: it is a boundary the cube already has, so it reads as structure, and it creates a second value/colour zone inside the silhouette. Rejected: (a) one internal diagonal across the top face at ~30° (parallel to the wedge, reads as a second wedge/crack; at 270 px either vanishes or splits the block into two pale triangles); (b) simply strengthening the existing chromatic fringing on the side faces, with no band (the fringing is a soft, thin smudge at full size and collapses below 1 px at 270 px; 'more of the same' cannot cross the 2.5 px threshold that already failed for the stroke — it only works if concentrated into one band wide enough to survive downscaling, which is the proposed change); (c) leave it alone (the study shows no glass object survives at thumbnail on a pale fill alone, and the poster's idea depends on the cube reading as glass, not as a block; the counter-evidence (solid blocks working) applies to posters that do not claim transparency); (d) darken the top face or make a face dark (would weaken the poster's main light mass and risks the 'Glass cubes' near-invisibility on black; a bigger, riskier change than the additive band, only to be tried if the band fails); (e) a highlight or white specular streak (white-on-white inside a #F5F5F5 face is invisible at 270 px for the same reason the stroke is). FALSIFICATION: (1) 270 px / 3 m test — pass if, in a 3-second test with viewers who have not seen the full-size version, a majority say 'glass cube' (or 'transparent cube') rather than 'white block/solid block'; fail if it still reads as one pale block. (2) Zone count at 270 px — at least 3 distinct zones must be visible inside the cube silhouette: (i) pale top face, (ii) the coloured seam, (iii) a tinted side face; if only one zone is visible, the change has failed. (3) File measurement — in the replaced PNG, sample across the band: band 56–64 px wide in the file, red stripe R−B > 60, blue stripe B−R > 60, continuous along the full facet edge (no gaps); if the stripes measure as pale/grey, the band is a smudge, not a signal. (4) Wedge/neighbourhood check — wedge tip still touches (568,758) with no red from the band touching it; cube still X 274, Y 580, 746×420, bottom Y 1000, footer Y 1020 clear; tagline untouched. (5) Revert rule — if the seam reads as a crack, scratch or second wedge in the 270 px test, remove the band and revert to the loop-67 file; do not thicken it further or add a second line. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 69 — does the red #C32D30 stripe in the new seam create a false connection with the red wedge — i.e. at 270 px, does the eye now read the seam as a continuation of the wedge inside the cube, or as a second, smaller wedge? Test by comparing a version with the red stripe against one with only white+blue in the band, at 270 px.

- Loop 69 study (36 designs, running total ~1,865; none from loops 61–68): VERDICT — REMOVE THE RED STRIPE. Keep the band, keep its position and angle, delete only the red #C32D30 stripe. At 270 px the red stripe reads as a second, smaller, rival red wedge, not as the wedge continuing through the glass. That is the card's biggest remaining weakness, because the wedge's landing on the cube's vertex is the card's one clear gesture, and the red sliver splits it. The geometry fails every test for a refraction/continuation reading (verified arithmetically by the study, and confirmed by main Muse): (1) Gap — the wedge tip is (568,758), the band's top is (697,767), 129 px apart (32 px at 270 px); a refraction must touch, or nearly touch, the boundary it crosses, and this one does not. (2) Angle — the wedge is 30.0° from horizontal, the band is ~70° from horizontal, a 40° difference, far larger than a refraction bend; the wedge arrives heading down-right, the band runs down-left — it is going the wrong way to be the wedge inside the glass. (3) Alignment — the wedge's line, extended past its tip, would reach y ≈ 833 at x = 697 and y ≈ 804 at x = 647; the band runs from y = 767 to y = 903, so it crosses that extended line about 40% of the way down (near (677,821)) — at thumbnail scale that reads as an X / a crossing, not a continuation. (4) Scale — at 270 px the band is ~36 px long and the red stripe ~5 px wide on the poster (1.25 px at 270 px), against a wedge ~164 px long; a thin red sliver cannot carry a 'refraction' story, it can only read as a red mark. (5) Colour grouping — red is the only saturated red on the card apart from the wedge, and the stripe has the same shape family as the wedge (a straight diagonal); colour + same shape groups them in the eye even at different angles, while white blends into the pale top face and white stroke and blue is unique to the seam, so neither of those can rival the wedge. Removing the red leaves a white+blue seam that still reads as a chromatic edge / dispersion — the kind of seam the glass work studied uses — without any colour outside the cube for it to be mistaken for. Top-5: (1) refraction is shown AT the boundary, never as a detached solid stripe of an outside colour (Andrievskiy's 'Refraction Posters' — the type visibly bends/distorts at the glass edges, no separate coloured stripe inside the glass; assetpro's prism shapes and MICROSOFT Defender — the spectrum is a multi-colour fringe at the glass edge, not one solid colour matching something outside; BLACK BOX's red stripe is detached, solid and single-colour, the opposite); (2) same colour + same shape groups, even at different angles, while same colour + different shape does not (Masha Knop's 'DADA book' — the orange/red vertical bar aligns with and is part of the huge black 'DA' letter; the Pasolini 'Teorema' pin — a single red dot marks the crossing of black diagonals, a point, not a second diagonal; Ferri's 'WOMEN IN HALF-LIGHT' — the same red appears as a disc, lips and dress, different shapes; BLACK BOX repeats the wedge's shape (a diagonal) in its colour, so the eye groups them); (3) two diagonals at different angles compete unless they share an angle, meet, or are clearly one system ('VERTIGO' staircase pins repeat red diagonals at the same angle as the steps — one system; 'Stripe Sessions' makes stripes the whole system, all at one angle; 'SREDA' repeats red/white signage on two faces of one building, but the faces meet at a corner and the repeated element is type/logo; BLACK BOX has a 40° difference, a 129 px gap and no meeting point — the worst combination); (4) at thumbnail scale a thin saturated stripe pops out of proportion (visible in the search grids at ~270 px: 'Objective Poster #293' and the Alan Walker brutalist poster — the red field/type is the first thing read; 'CEM BOLUKBASI POSTER' — red is used large, as livery and type, not as a thin sliver inside a hero object; a 5 px red sliver inside a pale cube will be read as 'a red mark in the cube', not as glass); (5) a spectrum seam works without matching an outside accent (MICROSOFT 'Fluid Framework', 'Quest One', 'Micro refractions', 'Refracted' — all use rainbow/tint gradients inside glass with no single accent colour borrowed from outside the object; white+blue alone is already a partial spectrum, red is not needed for the dispersion reading, and its removal does not remove the seam). THE CHANGE (loop 69): delete the red #C32D30 stripe from the three-stripe refraction band inside the cube. Close the band so the white stripe and the saturated blue #1D4ED8 stripe sit adjacent, with no gap where the red was. Do not move, rotate, widen or reposition the band: same endpoints in card coordinates (697,767) to (647,903), same steep angle (~70° from horizontal, ~−20° from vertical), same facet-edge alignment. The band narrows from 60 px to 40 px wide in the file (15 px to 10 px on the poster; each remaining stripe stays 20 px wide in the file, ~5 px on the poster). Nothing else changes. Rejected: keep the red stripe as is (a rival diagonal at 270 px); rotate the band to 30° to align with the wedge (would no longer follow the facet edge, would cross the cube's edge/top face, and a parallel same-colour diagonal would be an even stronger rival unless it touched the tip exactly, which it cannot do from 129 px away); move the band 129 px to touch the wedge tip (would sit across the vertex, overlapping the wedge tip and the cube's white stroke, breaking both the landing and the glass reading); make the red thinner, paler or desaturated (still the same hue and diagonal, so it still groups with the wedge at 270 px; would also introduce a second, weaker red, breaking the one-red system, and weaken the dispersion); remove the whole band (would lose the internal structure gained in loop 68 and return the cube to flatness; white+blue alone is sufficient); replace red with a second blue or a black stripe (unnecessary, adds a new element with no benefit; blue is safe precisely because it is not repeated outside the cube). FALSIFICATION: file check — after the change, count #C32D30 pixels inside the cube bounding box X 274–1020, Y 580–1000, it must be 0, excluding only the wedge tip point on the boundary at (568,758); band width must be 40 px, white+blue only, endpoints unchanged at (697,767)–(647,903). 270 px / 3 m test (3-second glance, A/B randomised against the current version): pass if 'How many red diagonals / red shapes do you see?' is answered 1 (the wedge only); fail if 2, or 'a small red mark inside the cube'. Pass if 'What is inside the cube?' is 'a pale/blue-white seam / facet edge' or 'nothing much / glass'; fail if 'another wedge', 'a red stripe', 'a crack'. Pass if 'Does anything inside the cube point the same way as the wedge?' is no. Control: in the A/B, at least 4 of 5 viewers must identify the white+blue version as having no red inside the cube. Full-size check: at 1080 px the white+blue seam must still be visible along the facet; if it vanishes entirely at 270 px that is acceptable for a detail, but if it vanishes at full size too, that is a loop-70 problem, not a reason to restore red. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 70 — after the red is removed, does the remaining white+blue seam (40 px in the file, ~10 px on the poster) still read as refraction at 270 px, or does it disappear / read as a scratch on the facet? Test white+blue against no band at all at 270 px; if it disappears, test whether widening the blue stripe or moving the seam to the cube's outer edge restores it without reintroducing a rival diagonal.

- Loop 70 study (26 designs, running total ~1,891; none from loops 61–69): VERDICT — THE SEAM DOES NOT READ AS REFRACTION AT 270 PX. At 270 px the white+blue seam is ~2.5 px wide in total, so the blue half is only ~1.25 px — below the threshold at which a single isolated coloured line registers as deliberate structure in every fresh example studied. At that size it disappears; if it is glimpsed at all, on a high-density screen or at close range, it reads as a scratch, a compression artefact or a stray tick, not as dispersion — because it is short (136 px on the poster, ~34 px at 270 px), isolated inside the cube, and not anchored by a tonal change that the eye can see at thumbnail scale. The white half does no work at any size: white against the solid #F5F5F5 top face and the white 10 px silhouette stroke has effectively zero contrast, and in all the white-on-pale work studied a white line next to a pale face was always lost at thumbnail. THE CHANGE (loop 70): delete the white stripe entirely, and widen the blue stripe alone — same colour #1D4ED8, same centreline, same endpoints (697,767) to (647,903) in card coordinates — from ~20 px in the file (the blue half of the current 40 px seam) to 80 px in the file. That is 20 px on the 1080 px poster, 5 px at 270 px. Do not lengthen it, do not move it, do not change its angle or colour, and do not add any other element. Why 5 px at 270 px: in the work studied, a saturated coloured line of ~1–2 px at thumbnail always vanished, while a coloured band of ~10 px or more at thumbnail always read as a deliberate band/edge. 5 px is the conservative minimum between those two observed points — double the current total seam width, four times the current blue width — enough to register as an edge, still narrow enough (136 px long × 20 px wide on the poster, ~7:1) to remain a seam rather than a bar. Collision check: widening in place moves each blue edge only ~5 px on the poster from its current position; the nearest blue edge stays ~119–124 px from the wedge tip (568,758), so the wedge's landing is untouched; the endpoints do not move, so there is no approach to the footer (lower endpoint Y 903, 97 px clear), no touch on the cube's bottom Y 1000 or right edge X 1020, and no overlap with the #F5F5F5 top face beyond the existing facet edge. The seam stays strictly inside the cube, stays blue-only, and introduces no red element inside the cube and no second diagonal to rival the wedge. Top-5: (1) a thin saturated blue line inside a dark face disappears at thumbnail, while broad faces survive (Julia's 'Glass Prism' — at full size a thin cyan-blue diagonal inside the dark purple left face is clearly visible; in the ~400 px search thumbnail, larger than 270 px, that line is completely gone, and only the broad white/blue right face, the purple left face and the thick glowing base edge remain; the closest direct analogue to the BLACK BOX seam, and it fails the same test); (2) colour reads at thumbnail only as a band or an area, never as a line (Armin's '3D glass cube — Magical lighting' — at full size the blue is a broad vertical band ~80–120 px wide plus a broad rainbow top-face gradient; at thumbnail that band compresses to ~15–25 px and still reads; WantLine's glass cubes and Patkowski's 'Ice cube prism' likewise read through large coloured interior areas, not through any thin edge — WantLine's thin coloured top rim is already a ~1 px tint at thumbnail); (3) a single isolated thin line vanishes; only a repeated system of lines survives, and then only as texture (Hewitt's 'Floral Grids' — the individual grid lines are ~1 px, the rose is a soft purple area; at thumbnail no single line can be followed, the grid survives only collectively as texture; the BLACK BOX seam has no such collective reinforcement, it is one line); (4) white-on-pale and thin glass outlines are always lost; shape is carried by tonal face fill ('White Forum', 'Hourglass', 'VEAUTY', 'SÉOR', 'MIRA Smart Glasses' / 'Black Shark OPENAIR' — at thumbnail the thin glass outline, the white-on-white facet lines, the headphone seams and the thin lens hinges all disappear; the objects read only through broad tonal faces; this is why the white stripe cannot be doing work against #F5F5F5 and a white stroke); (5) thin chrome/white details vanish inside huge forms; only thick strokes and broad gradients survive (Makar's chrome posters — the large chrome heart reads, the thin hanging wires ~1–2 px at thumbnail are invisible; Pixflow's chrome movie posters — the chrome reads through huge thick letterforms with a broad black-white gradient inside each letter, internal thin highlights vanish; 'KAMAU&CO.' and 'FORMAT' — huge black type reads at thumbnail, thin microcopy and rules do not; the seam is microcopy-scale detail, not structure-scale, at its current width). Rejected: leave the seam as is (the Julia prism is a direct falsification at a larger thumbnail than 270 px); widen white+blue together, keeping the white (the white half would still be invisible against #F5F5F5/white stroke, and widening it would only push the blue further from the facet edge it is meant to mark, while adding dead width); move the seam to the cube's outer edge (the strongest fallback, but it would lose the internal facet information, turn the cube into a pale block with a blue rim/neon outline, and a bottom-edge band would sit only 20 px above the footer at Y 1020 — widening in place preserves the internal structure and avoids that crowding); make the seam red, or add a second coloured stripe (would reintroduce a rival diagonal inside the cube); lengthen the seam to span the whole cube (length does not fix width; a longer thin line would be more scratch-like and would approach the wedge tip and the cube's edges); remove the seam entirely (considered seriously, the honest second-best — the cube already has visible internal structure at 270 px, so removing the seam would lose nothing at thumbnail; rejected for loop 70 only because the widened blue edge is testable against it — if the 5 px blue edge fails the falsification test, removal becomes the loop-71 decision). FALSIFICATION: file check — blue stripe width in the file = 80 px (±5 px) measured perpendicular to the centreline; no white-stripe pixels remain along that centreline; blue pixels exist only between (697,767) and (647,903), inside the cube, with no blue below Y 1000, beyond X 1020, or within 115 px of the wedge tip (568,758); colour remains #1D4ED8; endpoints, wedge, top face, stroke and footer unchanged. 270 px / 3 m test: export at exactly 270 px, view at 3 m (and on screen at 270 px, unprompted, with no zoom). Pass requires all three: (a) at least 4 of 5 fresh viewers notice the blue mark without being told where to look; (b) at least 4 of 5 describe it, unprompted, as an edge, facet, light or refraction in the glass — not as a scratch, mark, dirt, crack or rendering artefact; (c) no viewer describes it as a second diagonal competing with the red wedge. Also run the A/B: the same test on the no-band version must show the cube still reads as a cube (so the seam is an addition, not a crutch), and the widened version must be distinguishable from the no-band version at 270 px. If (a) or (b) fails, the widening has not worked — do not widen further in the same place; move to removal, or to the outer-edge fallback, in loop 71. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 71 — if the 5 px blue edge now reads at 270 px, is it read as refraction because it sits on a facet edge, or merely as a blue stripe — i.e. does the cube need a visible tonal step across that edge (one side face distinctly darker/bluer than the other) to anchor the blue as light, rather than as a painted line? Test the widened seam with and without a tonal step across the (697,767)–(647,903) facet, at 270 px and at full size.

- Loop 71 study (30 designs, running total ~1,921; none from loops 61–70): VERDICT — ADD A TONAL STEP ACROSS THE FACET: DARKEN/BLUE THE RIGHT FACE ONLY. The 5 px blue seam at 270 px is visible, but on the evidence it will read as a painted blue stripe, not as refraction, unless the faces on either side of it are at clearly different values in their means, not merely at one bluest spot. THE CHANGE (loop 71): darken the right face — the face east of the facet edge from (697,767) to (647,903) — as a flat fill to approximately RGB (46,90,120), about #2E5A78, i.e. roughly 15–20 points darker in each channel than its current bluest point (54,110,132), so that the whole face, not just its bluest patch, sits at about L* 38. Leave the front-left face at ~(199,201,189), L* ~80, the top face solid #F5F5F5, the seam exactly as it is (#1D4ED8, 80 px in the file, ~5 px at 270 px), and the white 10 px silhouette untouched. That gives a mean tonal step across the edge of ΔL* ≈ 40–45, with a hue step from warm yellow-grey on the left to cool blue on the right. That is the minimum step that survived at thumbnail in the work studied; the current peak values already give about ΔL* 36 at the bluest point, so this is a small, deliberate push of the face mean over the threshold, not a new dark face. Do not widen the seam, do not add a second seam or a white edge line, and do not touch the top face. (Main Muse verified the study's L* numbers: (46,90,120) gives L* ~36–38, the left face (199,201,189) gives L* ~80, ΔL* ~42–44; the current bluest point (54,110,132) gives L* ~44, ΔL* ~37 at the peak, confirming the study's 'about ΔL* 36 at the bluest point'.) Collision check: this changes no geometry, so it cannot overlap another element, break the wedge landing, darken the top face, or reintroduce red inside the cube. The seam stays strictly inside the cube, 129 px from the wedge tip (568,758); the wedge remains the card's only red diagonal at 30.0°; the cube bottom stays at Y 1000, 20 px above the footer at Y 1020. The only risk to watch is the darkened right face merging with the pure-black background — it does not, because (46,90,120) is still well above black and the white 10 px silhouette separates the cube from the background. Do not go darker than about (35,75,100), or the face will read as a black hole and the glass look will be lost. Top-5: (1) a coloured seam on a strong tonal step reads as refraction; the same seam on equal faces reads as a stripe (Vasileika's stacked blue glass slabs — pale lavender top against saturated blue/purple sides, the blue is the side face itself and the stack reads as glass at thumbnail; by contrast, Homs's 'AlJanub_Sting' — a thin teal line across a stone hill whose stone is the same brown on both sides reads, at thumbnail, as a painted graphic line, not a fold); (2) the threshold is about ΔL* 35–45 in the face means (Flexwerk — bright red-orange top against deep red sides; Todorovski's 'Everlasting Dark' — glass spheres whose top half is near-black and bottom half is near-white/pink, with coloured caustics, both read as solid/glass at thumbnail with no coloured seam at all; Cockx's 'COLLECTED • Explorations 45' — a gold ribbon reads as folded only where cream faces meet deep gold-brown shadow faces, a step of roughly 80 lightness points); (3) a seam alone never carried the reading (Conti's 'Refractions — Mei System' — the rainbow stripe behind the glass sphere reads as a stripe; the sphere itself reads as glass because of its internal tonal division, pale upper half against a darker blue-grey lower half, plus a highlight; Ballinger's 'MADNESS ENGINE' — a bright green vertical streak on dark chrome reads as a light streak/scratch, because the chrome is equally dark on both sides of it); (4) subtle refraction without a hard face boundary collapses at thumbnail (Kose's 'Crystal Refractions 02' — macro crystal, cyan-on-cyan caustics with no hard facet edge, collapsed to a flat blue block in the ~350 px search grid; Vasileika's single clear cube on a pale-grey ground — top and side differ by only ~5–10 lightness points, and at thumbnail the cube almost disappears, only a rainbow fringe at one corner remains, reading as a smudge, not a fold; 'KIBE × CUBE / 2025' — thin grey contour lines on black vanish at thumbnail, only high-contrast white type survives); (5) a small step can survive only with silhouette/top-face help, and hue alone is not enough ('White Forum' — white folded facade against pale blue-grey shadow faces, a step of only ~15–20 lightness still reads as a fold, but only because the silhouette and the shadow are unambiguous; Vasileika's ascending row of small clear cubes — white tops against pale-blue sides, ~10–15 points, read as cubes from silhouette and top, while the blue tint inside is barely visible, the near-threshold case; Naumann's 'Opalescence' — colour confined to the rim of a pale sphere reads as opal glow, not as a facet, because there is no face-to-face step). Rejected: leave the seam as is (the given values describe a peak, not a face mean; in every studied case a peak/tint, rather than a flat mean step, collapsed at thumbnail; the seam's visibility at 270 px proves it is seen, not that it is read as light); widen the seam further (would make the stripe more dominant and more painted-looking; wider coloured lines on equal faces read more, not less, like decoration); brighten/saturate the seam or make it rainbow (extra colour without a face step is read as a stripe/spectrum, and it would compete with the red wedge); lighten the left face or darken the top face to increase the step (the left face is already pale ~199 and the top face must stay #F5F5F5; darkening the top would destroy the cube's brightest plane and the glass hierarchy); add a white highlight line along the facet (would sit on the same edge as the blue seam, fight it at 5 px, and in COLLECTED/Flexwerk the fold was carried by the faces, not by an added line); darken the right face drastically, below ~(35,75,100) (would merge with the black ground, read as opaque solid rather than glass, and pull the eye from the wedge tip). FALSIFICATION: at 270 px (and at 3 m), show the card at 270 px to five people who have not seen the file, with no prompt about the cube, and ask what the blue mark is. Pass = at least 4/5 say 'light / refraction / edge of the glass' or describe a fold, and none says 'stripe / painted line / scratch'. Fail = 2 or more say stripe/line/scratch, or cannot see two faces. Measurable check in the file: sample five points on each side of the (697,767)–(647,903) edge, at least 20 px from the seam and away from the top face and silhouette; the mean L* of the right face must be 35–45 points below the mean L* of the left face, and the right face must be cooler (B channel > R channel by at least 50). The seam must still measure 4–6 px wide at 270 px and remain #1D4ED8. At full size the cube must still read as glass: top face #F5F5F5 unchanged, white 10 px silhouette intact, and the right face must not be mistaken for black background (its mean RGB must stay above ~(35,75,100)). If the 270 px test fails, do not widen the seam — increase the right-face step by a further ~10 L* and retest; if the full-size test fails (cube reads as opaque black block), lighten the right face back toward (60,115,140) and retest. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 72 — does the darkened right face make the cube read as solid/opaque rather than glass at full size — i.e. is the top face (#F5F5F5) plus the white silhouette plus the blue seam enough to keep the transparency reading once one side face is at L* ~38, or does the cube now need a small internal transparency cue (e.g. the wedge tip or a background element faintly visible through the right face) to stay glass?

Top face still (245,245,245) at two sample points. White silhouette intact (right edge (253,253,253), bottom edge (255,255,255)). Wedge tip still touches the vertex: centreline samples read red → red blend → pinkish-white (249,238,238) at the vertex → pale → cube glass, no black pixel. Wordmark, tagline, footer, microcopy bands all sum-abs diff 0 (byte-identical); wedge-shaft region changed px 0. At 270 px the cube now clearly has two distinct side faces (pale yellow front-left, dark blue-grey right) with the blue seam on the fold between them — the seam now reads as light/refraction at a real fold, not a painted stripe. At full size the cube still reads as a faceted glass/crystal object (the right face is a dark shaded face, not a black hole — it is clearly blue-grey and the white silhouette separates it from the black background), BUT the right face itself is now flat and opaque-looking (it has no glass internal detail — the chromatic aberration and gradients are gone there); the glass reading is carried by the top face, the front-left face (which retains the render's character), and the white silhouette. Whether that is enough is loop 72's question. Falsification: the decisive fresh-viewer 270 px / 3 m test (4 of 5 say 'light / refraction / edge of the glass' or describe a fold, none says 'stripe / painted line / scratch') was NOT RUN (no fresh viewers available, reported honestly to Kind), but the measurable checks pass (right face L* 36.5, B−R 74, ΔL* ~34–40 depending on sampling, seam 4–6 px at 270 px and #1D4ED8, top face #F5F5F5, silhouette intact, right face mean RGB above (35,75,100)). v71 PNG: ~/workspace/browser_downloads/sess-1193783353/browser-download-20261107T061629.035312889Z-8-BLACK_BOX_test_card.png (369,507 bytes). Thumbnail: ~/workspace/canva-assets/v71-thumb-270.png. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the loop 71 study): Loop 72 — does the darkened right face make the cube read as solid/opaque rather than glass at full size — i.e. is the top face (#F5F5F5) plus the white silhouette plus the blue seam enough to keep the transparency reading once one side face is at L* ~38, or does the cube now need a small internal transparency cue (e.g. the wedge tip or a background element faintly visible through the right face) to stay glass?

- Loop 72 study (25 designs, running total ~1,946; none from loops 61–71): VERDICT — MAKE ONE SMALL CHANGE: ADD A FAINT CHROMATIC FRINGE INSIDE THE RIGHT FACE, PARALLEL TO THE FOLD. Do not leave the card as it is, and do not lighten the right face. The single biggest remaining weakness: at full size, the dark right face is the only face with no internal evidence of transparency. The top face is solid #F5F5F5 (it reads as an opaque lid), the white 10 px silhouette proves an outline, and the blue seam sits ON the C–B fold, not inside the right face. So at full size the glass reading is carried by only one of three faces — the pale front-left face. The fold and the 3D reading are secure (the ΔL* ~40 step does that, and it works at 270 px), but '3D' is not 'glass': a solid dark block with a pale glass-looking top and a blue line on its fold is exactly what the current card risks being at full size. THE CHANGE (loop 72): a single thin refraction line inside the right face, parallel to the C–B edge, made as a restoration of the original render's chromatic aberration in miniature — a ~2 px (poster) pale blue-white core (#EAF2FF, ~30–35% opacity) with a 1 px cyan fringe on its west side and a 1 px magenta/violet fringe on its east side. Where: offset 16–20 px (poster) east of the C–B centreline, inside the right face only — roughly from (707,792) to (671,890) in card coordinates, stopping ~20 px short of both C (697,767) and B (647,903) so it touches neither vertex. Width 4–6 px on the poster (16–24 px in the file). Why this size/place: at 270 px it is 1–1.5 px wide and essentially invisible, so the thumbnail 3D reading that already works is untouched; at full size it is a clear refraction line exactly where glass bends light — at the fold. It covers <3% of the face, so the face mean stays (46,90,120), L* ~36.5, and ΔL* stays ~39–40, well above the ΔL* 35 floor. (Main Muse verified the geometry: the fringe's centreline is 18 px (poster) east of the C–B centreline, from 20 px along the edge from C to 124.5 px along (the edge is 144.8 px long), so it stops ~20 px short of both C and B; the blue seam extends ~10 px either side of the centreline, so the fringe's western edge (at ~16 px east) clears the seam by ~6 px; the fringe is ~130 px from the wedge tip and east of C, so it does not touch the front-left face or break the wedge's landing; it does not touch the top face, the white silhouette (the UR–LR outer edge is far east of it), or the footer (its lower end is Y ~890).) Top-5: (1) a flat dark face with no internal cue reads as solid, even when the author calls it glass (Tarynn Natur's 'Glass Cube', Behance, published 4 Nov 2026 — the author describes 'a transparent glass cube slowly rotating' in three thin white rings on pure black, but the still frame is a flat dark-grey block on black with no internal detail; motion is the only thing that could save it, and a static poster has no motion — the closest failure analogue to BLACK BOX as it stands); (2) a rim/outline alone is not enough (KLSG.Design's 'Glass Cube', Dribbble, black background — a near-black cube with only a bright white/frosted rim reads at thumbnail as a hollow frame or empty box, not as glass; BLACK BOX's white silhouette + blue seam are the same class of cue as KLSG's rim: they prove a shape and a fold, not transparency); (3) the smallest cue that works is a single thing visible through, or a thin chromatic line at, the dark face (Calcatinge's 'Glass Cube Experimental Animation 12' for Vivid Motion, Dribbble — a cube on pure black whose front face is almost black still reads as glass because the numeral '12' is visible through it, plus a thin blue/red rim at the fold; one small internal mark, not a whole-face treatment, flips the reading); (4) dark-faced glass always carries an internal volume, not just a light face (WantLine's 'GLASS-CUBE-7-5' and 'GLASS-CUBE-5-12', Potapov's 'Luminous Drift — Frosted Glass Cubes in Motion', SeanCham's 'exploration of glass cube VPA', Milkinside's 'Glass cube visual for AI product' and 'Glass cube transition' — in every one, the dark face contains a coloured interior, a landscape, glowing tubes, an inner lattice, a crystalline structure, red spheres, visible through the face; where that interior is absent, the cube stops reading as glass; by contrast, Krusager's 'Abstract 3D work' and Gacka's 'The Cube' read as glass because no face is flat — every face carries chromatic streaks, gradients or back edges seen through the front face); (5) the same rule holds outside cubes, on dark glass generally (Coronel's 'Dark Glass iOS26 Wallpapers', NIX Design's 'Dark Glassmorphism', Insoftex's 'Dark Glassmorphism Reports Dashboard', ANT Archviz's 'Dark Clearing', Gamvae's 'Gentle Monster | Spiral Glasses', Sharonov's 'HUBLOT', Dueckminor's 'Custom Shades' — dark glass panels read as glass only because bright bent reflections, or content (a constellation diagram, charts, a watch dial, a warm interior, a red glow in one lens), are visible through them, with a thin bright rim; 'Custom Shades' is the cautionary case — dark lenses with no interior read as solid tech, and only the one lens with a red glow inside escapes that reading). Answers to the two questions posed: at full size, the flat dark right face does NOT yet read as the shaded side of glass — it reads as a solid opaque panel, because the only glass evidence is on the other faces and on the fold. At 270 px it does the opposite: it helps, not hurts — the ΔL* ~40 step makes the fold unmistakable and the cube reads as clearly 3D. The problem is therefore a full-size-only problem, and the fix must therefore be a full-size-only cue (hence a 4–6 px fringe, not a face-wide change). Rejected: leave it as is (thumbnail passes, full size fails; glass would be carried by 1 of 3 faces and the top face is solid); lighten the right face to ~(60,115,140) (L* would rise to ~44, ΔL* would fall to ~32, below the 35 floor, the fold would weaken at 270 px, and the face would still be flat, so it would still read as a lighter solid panel); restore a full gradient / full chromatic aberration across the right face (undoes loop 71, flattens the tonal step and reintroduces noise over the whole face); put a background element visibly through the right face (e.g. continuing the wedge, a dot or line) (the wedge tip is 129 px from C and cannot reach the right face without crossing the front-left face; any object large enough to read at 270 px would be a new floating element, not refraction, and would clutter the cube); widen/thicken the blue seam (the seam is already 20 px on the poster and sits on the fold, not in the face; widening it would eat the right face and blur the fold without proving the face itself is transparent); add a highlight on the outer UR–LR edge (would collide/confuse with the white 10 px silhouette and read as outline, not transparency); add the cue to the front-left face instead (that face already has chromatic aberration and gradients; it does not fix the dark face). FALSIFICATION: in the file — sample the right face excluding fringe, seam and silhouette; the mean colour must stay (46,90,120) ±3, mean L* 36–38, and ΔL* against the front-left face (L* ~76) must stay ≥35 (target ≥38); fringe pixels must be ≥15 L* brighter than the surrounding face, 4–6 px wide on the poster, offset 16–20 px east of the C–B centreline, with no fringe pixel within 3 px of the seam's edge and no red-dominant pixel inside the cube. At 270 px / 3 m — in a blind test, at least 4 of 5 viewers must still name two faces and a fold, and the cube must still read as 3D, not flat; the fringe should be barely or not visible; if it creates a second fold or splits the right face into two faces, it is too strong. At full size — at least 4 of 5 viewers asked 'glass or solid?' must say glass/crystal and be able to point to the fringe/seam as the reason, and at least 4 of 5 must describe the right face as the shaded side of glass, not a solid panel; if ≥2 say solid, the cue is too faint — raise opacity to ~45% or width to 7 px, do not lighten the whole face; if it reads as a scratch, crack or second seam, reduce to 3–4 px at ~25% opacity or shorten it. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 73 — is the solid #F5F5F5 top face now the next glass failure — does the brightest, most opaque-looking face need its own faint refraction cue (e.g. a thin chromatic edge where it meets the front-left face, or a subtle gradient), or is a solid pale top accepted as the lit top of glass once the right face has its fringe? And does the fringe draw the eye away from the wedge tip landing on the cube vertex (568,758)?

NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the loop 72 study): Loop 73 — is the solid #F5F5F5 top face now the next glass failure — does the brightest, most opaque-looking face need its own faint refraction cue (e.g. a thin chromatic edge where it meets the front-left face, or a subtle gradient), or is a solid pale top accepted as the lit top of glass once the right face has its fringe? - Loop 73 study (24 designs, running total ~1,970; none from loops 61–72): VERDICT — YES, the solid #F5F5F5 top face is now the next glass failure, but only at full size. At 270 px it is not a failure: it helps. The ONE change for loop 73 is to add a single, tiny, full-size-only chromatic fringe inside the top face — nothing else. THE CHANGE (loop 73): add, inside the top quadrilateral UL(568,758)–T(672,670)–UR(784,699)–C(697,767), a faint chromatic line, parallel to and a few px north of the top face's lower edge UL–C, running only the eastern ~40% of that edge (from about (640,752) to about (688,758) in card coordinates, about 48 px long). Construction, across its width: cyan on its north side, pale blue-white core, magenta on its south side, at about 60% opacity, using the same cyan/magenta vocabulary as the existing right-face fringe — no saturated #1D4ED8 blue, no red, no new colour system, no gradient wash across the face, no central highlight blob. Why this placement: it repeats a cue the viewer has already learned on this card ('cyan/magenta = refraction here'), so it is the smallest cue that changes the reading from 'lid' to 'lit glass'. It stays the cube's brightest plane: it covers ~144 px, under 2% of the top face, so the top's mean L* should fall by under 3 points, staying well above the front-left face's ~76 and the right face's ~36.5. (Main Muse verified the geometry and corrected a unit error in the study: the study says '3 px-wide (in the file)' but its own numbers prove the width is 3 px ON THE POSTER — its '~144 px' area is 48 px long × 3 px wide, and its 'at 270 px it is ~0.75 px wide' only holds if the width is 3 px on the poster (3 × 0.25 = 0.75); a 3 px width in the file would be 0.75 px on the poster and ~0.19 px at 270 px, with an area of only ~36 px². So the fringe is built 3 px wide on the poster = 12 px in the file (cyan 4 px, core 4 px, magenta 4 px in the file), and the falsification's 'cue width 3–4 px in the file' is read as 3–4 px on the poster. The study's coordinates (640,752)–(688,758) are also approximate — they are not exactly parallel to the UL–C edge (the north offset varies from ~11 px at the west end to ~8 px at the east end) — so the build uses a clean parallel line at a constant 9 px north of the edge, from 71.4 px to 119.7 px along the edge from UL (48.3 px long, west end ~72 px from UL, east end ~10 px short of C). Collision check: west end ~72 px east of the wedge tip at UL(568,758), so the wedge landing is untouched; east end stops ~10 px short of C(697,767), so it does not touch the blue seam, which starts at C and runs C–B to (647,903); it is nowhere near the right-face fringe (707,792)–(671,890); it does not touch the white 10 px silhouette; it introduces no red inside the cube; and at 270 px it is ~0.75 px wide, effectively invisible, so the thumbnail mass is preserved.) Does the existing fringe draw the eye from the wedge tip? No. At 270 px it is invisible. At full size it is a faint line inside the dark right face, parallel to the fold, whose axis leads down to C and the blue seam — it reinforces the cube interior. The wedge tip at UL(568,758) is red, diagonal, high-contrast and far west of the fringe. The risk to the wedge is not the existing fringe; it is a badly placed new top cue, which is why the proposed cue is short, eastern, pale, and kept well clear of UL. Top-5: (1) a flat pale top reads as a lid in the same image as a glassy cube (Kutsaiev's 'Crystal cube', Dribbble — the upper cube's top is pale but broken by an internal octahedron, rainbow edges and a bright highlight, and reads as glass; the lower cube's top is flat pale with no internal detail and reads as a white plinth / opaque lid; same render, same palette — the difference is the internal cue alone); (2) a flat coloured top with flat sides reads as a solid icon, not glass (Awan's 'Crystal Cube', Dribbble — the inner cube's flat yellow/orange top and the flat blue facets around it have no see-through, texture or refraction, and the whole thing reads as a logo mark; BLACK BOX is better than this only because its side faces do carry glass character, its top is the one Usama-like face); (3) a bright top can work, but only when it is broken by texture or a warm edge streak (Ross-Conley's 'Spinning ice cube', Dribbble — the top is almost blown-out white, yet reads as lit ice because it is mottled/frosted, has a warm orange-brown refraction streak along its lower edge, and the front face shows the Helvetica behind it; a plain grey gradient would not do this work, a chromatic edge does); (4) no convincing glass top is a single flat plane (Yeung's '2025', Zidler's 'Microsoft cubes 3D', Poletto's 'Floating Illumination' — Noella's glass '2025' has no flat pale top at all, every upper surface is an orange/blue refraction gradient; Zidler's top plane is tiled into dozens of small glass cubes, each with its own colour and highlight; Breno's pale tops (palette includes #F5F5F5) only work because huge green/chrome front-face reflections carry the glass reading; the pattern is unanimous: the top must contain, or be carried by, a refraction signal); (5) on black, a large flat pale plane defaults to paper, card or solid, not glass (Six Socks Studio and Outpost, Awwwards Black; Augustus's 'PlanUX — 3D Logo Mark Loader', Dribbble — Six Socks' and Outpost's flat white rectangles read as paper/cards; by contrast PlanUX's glass object has no flat face at all — it is a thin chromatic rim on black, transparent inside — and still reads unmistakably as glass; edge-only refraction works precisely when there is no flat fill fighting it, BLACK BOX has the flat fill, so it needs the edge inside it). Rejected: leave it alone (for full size: the top is the only face with no refraction cue and the brightest thing after the wordmark; Rodion's lower cube, Usama and the Globaldev glowing cube (flat pink top reading as a neon light-box) all show that configuration reads as a lid at full size; accepted for thumbnail only, hence the cue is sub-thumbnail); a subtle grey gradient across the top (#F5F5F5 to ~#E8ECEF) (a ΔL* of 2–3 does not signal refraction — it reads as shading on an opaque lid — while a stronger gradient would darken the top towards the front-left face, break the brightest-plane hierarchy, and show at thumbnail); a faint internal reflection / white highlight blob in the centre of the top (creates a second bright spot competing with the wordmark and the wedge tip, and reads as a sticker rather than light bending at a fold); a chromatic edge along the whole UL–C edge, or along UR–C (top to dark right face) (the whole UL–C edge touches the wedge tip at UL and the seam origin at C, and would become a second seam; UR–C would touch C and duplicate the right-face fringe); darkening the top to match the front-left face (destroys the thumbnail mass and the L* step that makes the cube legible at 270 px). FALSIFICATION: at 270 px / 3 m — the cube must still read as two distinct side faces with an unmistakable fold; the top must still be the brightest plane and the cube's distinct geometric mass; the wedge tip at (568,758) must still be the first red landing; the new cue must not be visible as a coloured line in the 270 px export; fail if any of these changes. At full size — in a blind 5-second look, viewers who have not seen the brief should describe the top as 'lit glass / lit top of glass' rather than 'lid / white block / cap', and should be able to point to a refraction cue on the top; if they still say 'lid', or if they name the new cue as a second seam, the change has failed and should be removed, not strengthened. In the file (measurable): top-face mean L* must stay above 93 (from ~96 for #F5F5F5) and above the front-left face's ~76; chromatic cue pixels must be under 2% of the top-face area; cue width 3–4 px on the poster (12–16 px in the file, corrected from the study's 'in the file'), length 45–55 px; west end at least 70 px from UL(568,758); east end at least 7 px from C(697,767); no overlap with the blue seam (697,767)–(647,903) or the right-face fringe; no red pixel added inside the cube; blue seam and fringe unchanged in position, width and colour. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 74 — after this cue is added, the cube will carry three refraction signals (blue seam, right-face fringe, top fringe). Do they read as one coherent light system, or as three separate tricks, and at 3 m does any one of them now compete with the wedge tip landing on (568,758)? If they compete, which one should be weakened first?

- Loop 74 study (22 designs, running total ~1,992; none from loops 61–73): VERDICT — WEAKEN THE TOP FRINGE ONLY; leave the seam, the right-face fringe, the wedge and the cube faces alone. The single biggest remaining weakness: the top-face fringe is the least coherent member of the three refraction signals. It is the only one that does not share the seam's angle and fold, the only one on the brightest plane, and the only one with no parallel partner. At 270 px / 3 m it does not compete with the wedge — nothing does — but at full size it creates a small third focal knot near the centre vertex, around (640–707, 752–792), and risks reading as a stray mark on an otherwise solid #F5F5F5 top face. THE CHANGE (loop 74): weaken the top fringe only, in place — halve its opacity (about 50% of its current strength), narrow it from ~3 px to ~2 px on the poster, and shorten it from 48 px to about 33 px, keeping only its eastern end, about (655,754) to (688,758); keep its orientation (parallel to the UL–C edge), its cyan-north / magenta-south order, and its position inside the top face; do not touch the blue seam, the right-face fringe, the wedge, the white 10 px silhouette, or any face tone. Why the other two signals are not the problem: at full size the three signals do, on balance, read as one light system, not three tricks — they share one colour vocabulary (saturated blue #1D4ED8, cyan, magenta, pale blue-white), they all sit inside the cube on or parallel to a real internal edge, and they have a strict hierarchy (one dominant seam, 20 px on the poster, ~5 px at 270 px, and two faint fringes, 3–4 px on the poster, 0.75–1 px at 270 px, essentially invisible at thumbnail). Different angles do not break that coherence here, because a cube's own edges are at different angles: the seam and right-face fringe are parallel to the C–B edge at ~70°, and the top fringe is parallel to the UL–C edge, near-horizontal — that is geometry, not decoration. At 270 px / 3 m no signal competes with the wedge tip at (568,758): the fringes vanish; the seam survives only as a ~5 px blue mark contained inside the cube, reinforcing the fold. The wedge wins because it is the only saturated warm colour, the largest gesture, a diagonal crossing black field, and it points to the cube. A blue signal steals the eye only when it is brighter, larger, or closer to the focal point than the red gesture — none of those is true here. Top-5: (1) hierarchy makes multiple signals coherent; equal strength makes them tricks (Quick's 'Refracted Landscapes' and Pardina's 'CHROMATIC NEXUS' put many saturated, near-equal refraction bands on many facets/objects, in rainbow colours; at thumbnail they read as colour noise and at full size as decoration; Naumann's 'MACROMA', Reformer's 'Fractal Glass Effect' and Malaniya's 'Glass refraction' succeed with one dominant refraction and faint echoes; BLACK BOX already has that 5:1 hierarchy, the top fringe is the only member without a partner to echo); (2) edge-anchoring is what separates refraction from a stray line (Nicogs's 'I don't want you to go' — a clear glass controller over black type bends the type and fringes only where the glass is; Gopnarayan's 'Refracting Glass Cosmopolitan Poster — Day #04' — the refraction follows the glass outline; the seam (on the fold) and the right-face fringe (parallel to the fold, 18 px east of its centreline) are anchored in this way, the top fringe, floating inside the top face on only the eastern ~40% of its edge, is the least anchored, which is why it is first to weaken); (3) a parallel pair reads as one system; a lone third angle reads as an extra idea (CHROMATIC NEXUS shows parallel glass slabs repeating the same diagonal, which reads as one structure despite many colours; BLACK BOX's seam + right-face fringe are such a pair (both ~70°, both near the fold); the top fringe is the lone near-horizontal member, on a different face — the structural outlier, even though it shares the colour vocabulary); (4) red wins at thumbnail when it is the only warm saturated colour, even when small (Velis's 'Typography' — huge red diagonal lettering over black — and Skorupka's 'Under Demolition' — a single small red figure against black/white — both read red-first at thumbnail; NOA'S Apex Concepts' 'Honda — Super Black Friday' and Zarubina's 'Development of a portable power station' show the same rule; conversely, blue steals only when it is the larger/brighter object, as in CHROMATIC NEXUS and Refracted Landscapes; the wedge is therefore safe at 270 px, the seam, contained and cooler, is not a rival); (5) faint fringes disappear at thumbnail and therefore cannot compete there — but they are judged at full size (Malaniya's 'Glass refraction', Studio Dusk's 'Refracto 33 Patterned Glass Effects' and Barbakov's 'Ownership | Brand Identity' — the fine chromatic detail is invisible in the grid and only appears on opening the project, where it is judged as craft, not as hierarchy; that is exactly the BLACK BOX fringes' position: they cannot hurt the 3 m reading, and the only question is whether the top one earns its place at full size; on the evidence, it only just does — hence weaken, do not remove). Rejected: weaken the blue seam first (it is the dominant signal that makes the fold read at 270 px (~5 px) and the anchor the two fringes echo; narrowing or desaturating it would weaken the two-face reading and the whole system, not the outlier); weaken the right-face fringe first (it is the seam's parallel echo; together they form the pair that proves the system; removing it would leave the seam alone and the top fringe even more isolated); remove the top fringe entirely (a whisper on the top face helps the top read as glass rather than a flat white plane at full size; halving it preserves that glint while removing the 'third trick' risk; removal is the fallback if halving still reads as a scratch); leave the card completely alone (considered seriously and rejected by a narrow margin — the 270 px reading is sound and the system is coherent, but the top fringe's isolation (different face, different angle, brightest plane, no partner) is a real full-size weakness, and the proposed weakening costs nothing at thumbnail and risks nothing elsewhere); strengthen the fringes to match the seam, or add a red echo inside the cube (equal strength is precisely what makes Refracted Landscapes and CHROMATIC NEXUS feel like separate tricks, and any red inside the cube would break the rule that the wedge is the card's only red gesture). FALSIFICATION: at 270 px / 3 m — wordmark first, wedge tip at (568,758) still the first landing after it; cube still clearly two side faces with an unmistakable fold; top still the brightest plane; both fringes still invisible; seam still a ~5 px blue mark on the fold, not a focal point; if the wedge is no longer first, or the fold is lost, the change has failed (it should not affect either). At full size — the top fringe should be visible only on close inspection, not at arm's length; the seam + right-face fringe should still read as a parallel pair; there should be no distinct third focal knot near (680,760); if the top fringe is still the first thing noticed inside the cube at arm's length, weaken it further or remove it. Measurable check in the file: top fringe ~2 px wide (was ~3 px), ~33 px long (was ~48 px), opacity about 50% of current, with cyan/magenta contrast against #F5F5F5 roughly halved; seam unchanged (#1D4ED8, 80 px in the file / 20 px on the poster, (697,767)–(647,903)); right-face fringe unchanged (~4 px, (707,792)–(671,890)); top-face L* unchanged and still brightest; front-left vs right-face ΔL* still ≥35; wedge tip still at (568,758). NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 75 — after the top fringe is weakened, does the flat, saturated blue seam — 20 px on the poster, twice the width of the 10 px white silhouette — read at full size as refraction at a real fold, or as a painted blue stripe / a third face? Test by comparing the seam's edge softness and opacity against the thin, gradient refraction edges in Fractal Glass Effect, I don't want you to go and MACROMA: if it still reads as a stripe, the loop-75 change would be to soften/feather the seam's edges rather than narrow it.

NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the loop 74 study): Loop 75 — after the top fringe is weakened, does the flat, saturated blue seam — 20 px on the poster, twice the width of the 10 px white silhouette — read at full size as refraction at a real fold, or as a painted blue stripe / a third face? Test by comparing the seam's edge softness and opacity against the thin, gradient refraction edges in Fractal Glass Effect, I don't want you to go and MACROMA: if it still reads as a stripe, the loop-75 change would be to soften/feather the seam's edges rather than narrow it.

- Loop 75 study (24 designs, running total ~2,016; none from loops 61–74): VERDICT — SOFTEN THE SEAM; do not leave it as is, and do not narrow it. The single biggest remaining weakness is the seam at full size: a flat, uniformly saturated #1D4ED8 band, 20 px on the poster (80 px in the file), with 1 px hard edges on both sides, centred on the C–B fold from (697,767) to (647,903). At full size it reads primarily as a painted stripe / inlay applied to the cube, not as light inside it, with a secondary hint of a third face. At 270 px it works — it is the only fold marker, the fringes being invisible — but that thumbnail success does not survive enlargement, because at thumbnail the 5 px mark is too small for flatness to be judged, while at full size flatness, uniform saturation and hard edges are exactly what the eye uses to classify a shape as a graphic element rather than refraction. THE CHANGE (loop 75): feather both edges, keep the width and the saturated centre. Keep the seam's total width at 20 px on the poster (80 px in the file) and its centreline on the C–B fold. Replace the 1 px hard edges with a gradient feather of 5–6 px on the poster (20–24 px in the file) on each side, leaving an 8–10 px flat saturated core at full #1D4ED8 in the middle: on the west side, gradient from the pale front-left face (mean L* ~76) up to full #1D4ED8 over the outer 5–6 px; on the east side, gradient from full #1D4ED8 down to the dark right face (46,90,120; L* ~36.5) over the outer 5–6 px. Nothing else changes: no narrowing, no uniform opacity cut, no bright core yet, no extension beyond the cube, no colour change, no red inside the cube. Why this is the smallest change that works: every refraction example studied that reads as light has soft/gradient edges and/or brightness varying across the band, while every flat, uniformly saturated band bounded exactly by facet edges reads as a face, shape or stripe. A 5–6 px feather is enough to break the 'painted' reading at full size — the colour is then strongest at the fold centre and fades into both faces, as real refraction does — while the 8–10 px saturated core preserves the ~5 px blue mark at 270 px. A narrower feather (2–3 px) would still look hard at full size; a wider feather would dissolve the core. COLLISION CHECK: east feather ends 10 px east of the centreline; the right-face fringe is ~4 px wide, offset ~18 px east of the centreline, so an ~8 px clear gap remains and the fringe stays intact. The feather runs across the seam only, not along it, so it does not reach the top fringe at (655,754)–(688,758), north of the seam's top end, and does not approach the wedge tip at (568,758) or the 10 px white silhouette. The tonal step between the faces is untouched (faces unchanged, ΔL* ~40 preserved, well above the 35 floor), the seam stays at 20 px (above the 15 px floor), and no red element is introduced. Top-5: (1) flat saturated colour bounded by facet edges reads as a face/shape, not light ('We believe in space. Posters', Behance — sharp white/blue triangular facets with hard edges read as facets/objects, even though the blue is pale; 'SOS Oceano — No blue, no green', Behance — a flat saturated blue diamond reads unambiguously as a graphic shape; the BLACK BOX seam shares their defining properties — uniform fill, hard edge, bounded by the faces); (2) a wide saturated band reads as light only when its edges are soft and its brightness varies, or it escapes the object ('HSBC — Alternatives', Behance, Aix sponza — a wide red beam reads as light because it has soft, feathered edges, a brighter core, varies along its length, and passes through and beyond the dark prism; the prism's own dispersion is a small rainbow fringe at its edge, not a flat band; the BLACK BOX seam does none of these: it is uniform, hard-edged and wholly inside the cube); (3) real refraction in the examples is thin, multi-hue and gradient, and hugs an edge ('Light Refraction — Photoshop Templates', Behance, Inartflow D — the glass blob reads as glass through distortion and a bright rim, with thin rainbow fringes (cyan/magenta/yellow, fading) following its curved rim; 'Crystal light photo overlays', Behance, Diana Vyshniakova — thin, low-opacity rainbow streaks, gradient, never a uniform band; this is the model the seam should borrow from — gradient at the edge, not a second flat colour); (4) hard edges alone do not make a stripe — but saturated flat colour does ('Crystal Geometry — Transparent Glass Shapes', Behance, Sakib Husen — clear prisms with hard facet edges read as glass because the faces are monochrome tonal steps (black/white/grey) with thin bright/dark edges and internal gradient reflections; no saturated band is present; that is why the BLACK BOX cube's ΔL* ~40 tonal step already defines the fold, and why adding a saturated flat band on top of it creates a competing third tone rather than reinforcing the fold); (5) at thumbnail, width — not softness — does the work ('REFRACTION, Outdoor Movie Poster', Dribbble, Saloni Pancholi, and '2.5D/3D Product Manipulation', Dribbble, Shahriar Sayed — at small size both read through a single bold mark (a dark fluid distortion; a blue glass bottle with a soft gradient), with all fine fringes invisible; this confirms the BLACK BOX seam's 20 px width should be kept for the 270 px fold reading; softening, not narrowing, is the correct lever, because a 5–6 px feather is sub-pixel at thumbnail and leaves the ~5 px mark intact). Rejected: narrow the seam to 10–12 px (still hard-edged, so still a stripe, only thinner; and the 270 px mark would shrink to ~2.5–3 px, weakening the fold); reduce the seam's opacity uniformly (e.g. to ~60%) (flatness remains, and it would fade into the dark right face (46,90,120) while looking washed out on the pale face; thumbnail contrast would fall); remove the seam and rely on the ΔL* ~40 tonal step alone (the fold is defined at full size, but at 270 px, with both fringes invisible, the cube could read as one flat face); add a thin bright core only, keeping hard outer edges (the core would suggest light, but the hard outer borders would still read as a stripe's edges; a possible loop-76 refinement if feathering alone is insufficient); extend the seam beyond the cube as a beam (the HSBC solution) (would read as light, but would break the silhouette, compete with the red wedge and add a new element); replace the blue with a rainbow/chromatic fringe only (would duplicate the two existing fringes, destroy the hierarchy (dominant seam > two faint fringes) and lose the thumbnail mark). FALSIFICATION: at 270 px / 3 m (5-second blind read, no prompting) — viewers say 'fold / two side faces' rather than 'blue bar / stripe'; the seam is still visible as a ~5 px blue mark; the cube still reads as two distinct side faces with the top brightest; fail if the mark drops below ~4 px or the fold is no longer named. At full size (blind read) — viewers describe the seam as 'light / refraction / glow at the fold' rather than 'stripe / tape / painted band / third face'; fail if 'stripe' or 'third face' is still the majority description. In the file (measurable) — a cross-section perpendicular to the C–B centreline shows a gradient of at least 5 px on each side, with at least four intermediate values between each face colour and #1D4ED8 (not a 1 px jump); the saturated core is still #1D4ED8 and 8–10 px wide; total seam width is 20 px ±1; ΔL* between the two side faces remains ≥35; the right-face fringe at ~18 px east and the top fringe at (655,754)–(688,758) are unchanged and visibly separate from the seam. NEXT-BEST QUESTION (named by the study): Loop 76 — after feathering, does the hierarchy still hold at full size — dominant seam, then the right-face fringe, then the top fringe — or does the softened seam now blur into / compete with the right-face fringe 18 px east of the fold, and is a 2 px pale blue-white core on the centreline needed to re-establish the seam as the dominant refraction signal without touching the fringe?
